The analytic de Rham stack in rigid geometry
Juan Esteban Rodríguez Camargo

Abstract
. Applying the new theory of analytic stacks of Clausen and Scholze we introduce a general notion of derived Tate adic spaces. We use this formalism to define the analytic de Rham stack in rigid geometry, extending the theory of 𝒟 wideparen \wideparen{\mathcal{D}} -modules of Ardakov and Wadsley to the theory of analytic D D -modules. We prove some foundational results such as the existence of a six functor formalism and Poincaré duality for analytic D D -modules, generalizing previous work of Bode. Finally, we relate the theory of analytic D D -modules to previous work of the author with Rodrigues Jacinto on solid locally analytic representations of p p -adic Lie groups.



1.
Introduction

Motivation

The main objective of this paper is to geometrically construct a six functor formalism for a suitable category of
D
-modules over rigid spaces. To further develop this idea let us first recall some aspects of the classical theory of
D
-modules.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
0
and
X
a smooth scheme over
K
. Classically, the category of
D
-modules over
X
is constructed by first defining a ring of algebraic differential operators
D_{X}
over
X
, and then taking the category of
D_{X}
-modules whose underlying
\mathscr{O}_{X}
-module is quasi-coherent. If
X
admits an étale map to an affine space
X\to\mathbb{A}^{d}_{K}
, then
D_{X}
can be explicitly constructed as the Weyl algebra

D_{X}=\mathscr{O}_{X}[\partial_{T_{1}},\ldots,\partial_{T_{d}}],

where
\mathbb{A}^{d}_{K}=\Spec K[T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}]
, and the variables
\partial_{T_{i}}
correspond to the partial derivations along the coordinate
T_{i}
.

In
[
Sim96
,
ST97
]
, Simpson has proposed a different perspective on the theory of
D
-modules by the employ of stacks. Let
X
be a smooth variety over
K
, Simpson attaches a space
X_{dR}^{alg}
from commutative rings over
K
to sets whose theory of quasi-coherent sheaves is naturally isomorphic to the theory of
D
-modules over
X
. More concretely, let
\mathrm{Ring}_{K}
be the category of commutative rings of finite type over
K
, then Simpson’s de Rham stack is defined as the stack in the étale topology given by

X_{dR}^{alg}(R)=X(R^{\red}),

where
R^{\red}
is the reduction of the ring
R
. An advantage from this definition is that one can easily construct categories of
D
-modules for any variety over
K
without the smooth assumption, namely the previous formula for the de Rham stack extends to any stack over
K
. The study of
D
-modules via the de Rham stack, and its application to geometric Langlands, can be found in the work of Gaitsgory and Rozenblyum
[
GR14
]
.

Specializing to
p
-adic geometry, let
K
be a non-archimedean extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and let
X
be a smooth rigid space over
K
. In the works
[
AW18
,
AW19
]
Ardakov and Wadsley have developed the theory of coadmissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-modules over rigid spaces. The departure point to define the category of coadmissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-modules is again a sheaf
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
of "infinite order
p
-adic differential operators over
X
". To describe this sheaf, let us suppose for simplicity that
X=\Spa A
is affinoid and that we have an étale map
f:X\to\mathbb{D}^{d}_{K}
to a polydisc
\mathbb{D}^{d}_{K}=\Spa K\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\rangle
. Then, as for schemes, we first consider the algebra of algebraic differential operators of
X
:

D_{X}=A[\partial_{T_{1}},\ldots,\partial_{T_{d}}].

The action of
\partial_{T_{i}}
on
A
by derivations is continuous, so we can find
N>0
such that for all
n\geq N
the left sub-
A^{\circ}
-module
A^{\circ}[p^{n}\partial_{T_{1}},\ldots,p^{n}\partial_{T_{d}}]\subset D_{X}
is stable under multiplication. Thus, by taking
p
-adic completions
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{(n)}:=A\langle p^{n}\partial_{T_{1}},\ldots,p^{n}\partial_{T_{d}}\rangle
, and taking limits along
n\to\infty
, one constructs the algebra of infinite order differential operators

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}=\varprojlim_{n}\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{(n)}.

The algebra
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
is known as a Fréchet-Stein algebra and its construction is motivated from the algebra of analytic distributions of
p
-adic Lie groups of Schneider-Teitelbaum
[
ST03
]
. In particular, there is a well defined category of coadmissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules given by the limit along pullbacks of the categories of finite type
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}^{(n)}
-modules. Categories of coadmissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules have been extended using bornological vector spaces, and a six functor formalism for
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules has been constructed by Bode in
[
Bod21
]
.

The theory of the analytic de Rham stack developed in this work is then a conciliation between the geometric theory of
D
-modules of Simpson via the de Rham stack, and the theory of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
-modules of Ardakov and Wadsley. To justify the tools used to construct the analytic de Rham stack let us start with an example. Let
K
be a field of characteristic
0
, and let
\mathbb{G}_{a}=\Spec K[T]
be an affine space over
K
seen as an additive group. It follows from the definition of the algebraic de Rham stack that

\mathbb{G}_{a,dR}^{alg}=\mathbb{G}_{a}/\widehat{\mathbb{G}}_{a}

where
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}_{a}
is the formal completion at
0\in\mathbb{G}_{a}
, acting by translations. It turns out that the Cartier dual of the stack
*/\widehat{\mathbb{G}}_{a}
is just
\mathbb{G}_{a}
, then, it is expected (and indeed the case) that modules over
\mathbb{G}_{a,dR}^{alg}
are given by sheaves on
\mathbb{G}_{a}
together with an operator
\partial_{T}
(Cartier dual of
*/\widehat{\mathbb{G}_{a}}
), that is equivariant with respect to the module structure on
\mathbb{G}_{a}
via the additive action of
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}_{a}
on
\mathbb{G}_{a}
(i.e. that
\partial_{T}
acts by derivations).

Let us now take
K/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
a non-achimedean extension, and let
\mathbb{G}_{a}=\mathbb{D}^{1}_{K}=\Spa K\langle T\rangle
be the open affinoid disc seen as an additive group. We would like to define an analytic de Rham stack
\mathbb{G}_{a,dR}
whose theory of quasi-coherent sheaves is related to the theory of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-modules. By construction, the sheaf
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
of infinite order
p
-adic differential operators has cotangent variables (i.e. the derivations
\partial_{T}
) that look like global sections of an analytic affine space
\mathbb{A}^{1,\an}_{K}
. Moreover, the category of coadmissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-modules is a non-commutative analogue of the category of coherent sheaves on a relative analytic affine space (eg.
\mathbb{A}^{1,\an}_{K}
). Therefore, we would like to define the analytic de Rham stack in such a way that

\mathbb{G}_{a,dR}=\mathbb{G}_{a}/\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\dagger},

with
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\dagger}\subset\mathbb{G}_{a}
a subgroup acting by translations, and such that
*/\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\dagger}
is the Cartier dual of
\mathbb{A}^{1,\an}_{K}
(in a suitable sense). It is known that the continuous dual of
\mathscr{O}(\mathbb{A}^{1,\an}_{K})
is given, as a Hopf algebra, by the ring of germs of functions at
0\in\mathbb{G}_{a}

K\{T\}^{\dagger}=\varinjlim_{n}K\langle\frac{T}{p^{n}}\rangle.

Therefore, a reasonable candidate for
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\dagger}
would be given by

\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\dagger}=\Spa K\{T\}^{\dagger}.

Here is where several foundational problems appear. First, we are obligated to work with topological rings, and in order to have a good theory of analytic
D
-modules as quasi-coherent sheaves of a stack, we also need to work with topological modules. This problem is solved thanks to the theory of analytic geometry and condensed mathematics of Clausen and Scholze
[
CS19
,
CS20
,
CS22
]
. Second, if we ever expect to built up a six functor formalism of analytic
D
-modules from the theory of complete modules of analytic rings, we need to construct categories of analytic stacks, and have a strong descent theory. Again, analytic geometry comes to the rescue, this time making use of the abstract theory of six functor formalisms and
\mathscr{D}
-stacks as in
[
Man22b
,
Man22a
]
and
[
Sch23
]
.

Once we have analytic stacks and complete modules at our disposal, we can make sense to objects such as
\mathbb{G}_{a}/\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\dagger}
, as well as to its category of complete modules. However, in order to make a good definition of the analytic de Rham stack, we would need to mimic Simpson’s construction of the algebraic de Rham stack. The key idea is that, while the space
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}_{a}
represents the nilradical of a discrete ring
R
(i.e. those elements
a
such that
a^{n}=0
for some
n
), the space
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\dagger}
represents a "
\dagger
-nilradical" for a suitable category of analytic rings
R
. In other words,
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\dagger}
represents elements
a\in R
"of spectral norm zero", i.e. such that
|a|\leq|p^{n}|
for all
n\geq 0
. Thus, our first step is to restrict the theory of analytic rings to a theory of "bounded affinoid rings"
R
for which we can construct a
\dagger
-nilradical
\Nil^{\dagger}(R)
. Another motivation for the introduction of bounded affinoid rings arises from Tate algebras: we should only expect to construct an analytic de Rham stack for rings that look like affinoid Tate algebras, namely, for rings admitting a pseudo-uniformizer in a suitable sense.

After bounded affinoid rings are introduced, we study some fundamental geometric properties of them in Sections
2
and
3
: an analytic topology analogue to the analytic topology of adic spaces; a theory of derived Tate adic spaces obtained by gluing bounded affinoid rings via rational localizations; the theory of the cotangent complex of analytic rings; different notions of morphisms of finite presentation appearing in non-archimedean analytic geometry; a deformation theoretic description of smooth maps of morphisms of (solid) finite presentation of derived Tate adic spaces; Serre duality; a new deformation condition involving
\dagger
-nilradicals. This study on derived rigid geometry settles the basis for the theory of the analytic the Rham stack.

Finally, once all the prerequisites in derived rigid geometry are done, we can start the study of the analytic de Rham stack. Let
X
be a rigid space over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, and let
\Aff_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}^{b}
be the (
\infty
-)category of bounded affinoid rings over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, then the analytic de Rham stack
X_{dR}
will be defined as a suitable sheafification of the prestack mapping
\mathcal{A}\in\Aff_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}^{b}
to

X_{dR}(\mathcal{A})=X(\mathcal{A}^{\dagger-\red}),

where
\mathcal{A}^{\dagger-\red}:=\mathcal{A}/\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})
is the "
\dagger
-reduction of
\mathcal{A}
". Our workhorse to prove properties on the analytic de Rham stack, such as the existence of six functors, Poincaré duality, and the construction of the Hodge filtration, will be a new theory of Cartier duality for analytic vector bundles over derived Tate adic spaces in Section
4
. The main theory of the analytic de Rham stack, in particular the construction of six functors for analytic
D
-modules, is the content of Section
5
. We finish the paper with a generalization of the analytic de Rham stack of smooth morphisms in the equivariant setting in Section
6
, obtaining a generalization of equivariant
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-modules of Ardakov
[
Ard21
]
, and of the theory of solid locally analytic representations of
p
-adic Lie groups of
[
RJRC22
,
RJRC23
]
.

Overview of the paper

The body of the paper is divided in two main parts. First, we develop the theory of derived Tate adic spaces and Tate stacks in Sections
2
and
3
. The second part consists on Sections
4
,
5
and
6
, where we study different incarnations of Cartier duality of vector bundles, we define the analytic de Rham stack for Tate stacks over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, and finally we relate the theory of the analytic de Rham stack with the theory of locally analytic representations of
p
-adic Lie groups.

§
2
Derived Tate adic spaces

The theory of adic spaces of Huber
[
Hub94
]
have been the language for non-archimedean analytic geometry in the last few decades, a weakness of this category are the restrictions imposed in the definition of complete Huber pairs. The main goal of this section is the introduction of the
\infty
-category of
bounded affinoid rings
(Definition
2.6.10
), generalizing the category of Tate Huber pairs, over which we can do both analytic and derived algebraic geometry. Similarly as for Huber pairs, one can construct a spectral space
\Spa\mathcal{A}
for any bounded affinoid ring
\mathcal{A}
(Definition
2.7.3
), generalizing the adic spectrum of an Huber ring. Maps between bounded affinoid rings will give rise to spectral maps of the adic spectra, and solid quasi-coherent modules will satisfy descent for the analytic topology. A bounded affinoid ring has the feature that any function is
bounded
in the sense of the theorem down below. Moreover, one can define condensed and
\dagger
-nilradicals for these rings, consisting on ideals of uniformly nilpotent or overconvergently close to zero elements respectively. The following summarizes the main results (cf. Propositions
2.5.7
,
2.6.9
,
2.6.14
,
2.7.8
and
2.7.14
).

Theorem 1.0.1
.

Let
R=\mathbb{Z}((\pi))
be the Huber ring parametrizing pseudo-uniformizers in Tate Huber pairs. There is a full subcategory
\AffRing^{b}_{R}\subset\AnRing_{R}
of the
\infty
-category of analytic
R
-algebras, called the category of bounded affinoid rings, containing fully faithfully the
1
-category of Tate Huber pairs over
R
. The category
\AffRing^{b}_{R}
is stable under small colimits in
\AnRing_{R}
. Furthermore, let
\mathcal{A}\in\AffRing^{b}_{R}
, the following hold

(1)

There is an animated subring
\mathcal{A}^{+}\subset\underline{\mathcal{A}}(*)
such that an
\underline{\mathcal{A}}
-module is
\mathcal{A}
-complete if and only if it is
\mathbb{Z}[a]_{{\square}}
-complete for all
a\in\mathcal{A}^{+}
.

(2)

For any map
\mathbb{Z}[T]\to\mathcal{A}
of analytic rings, there is some
n\in\mathbb{N}
and an extension to the Tate algebra
R\langle\pi^{n}T\rangle\to\mathcal{A}
. In other words, any element
a\in\mathcal{A}
is bounded.

(3)

The ring
\mathcal{A}
has a condensed nil-radical
\Nil(\mathcal{A})
whose
S
points (for
S
profinite) are given by maps
S\to\mathcal{A}
which are uniformly nilpotent. Furthermore, the analytic ring structure on
\mathcal{A}
is already determined by the analytic ring structure of the quotient
\mathcal{A}^{\red}:=\mathcal{A}/\Nil(\mathcal{A})
, and
\Nil(\mathcal{A}^{\red})=0
.

(4)

The ring
\mathcal{A}
has a
\dagger
-nil-radical
\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})
whose
S
-points (for
S
profinite) are given by maps
S\to\mathcal{A}
which are overconvergently close to zero. Furthermore, the analytic ring structure on
\mathcal{A}
is already determined by the analytic ring structure of the quotient
\mathcal{A}^{\dagger-\red}:=\mathcal{A}/\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})
, and
\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A}^{\dagger-\red})=0
.

(5)

There is a spectral space
\Spa\mathcal{A}
endowed with an analytic topology with a basis given quasi-compact rational subspaces, generalizing Huber’s construction of
\Spa(A,A^{+})
. Moreover, any map
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
of bounded affinoid rings gives rise a spectral morphism
\Spa\mathcal{B}\to\Spa\mathcal{A}
preserving rational localizations.

(6)

We have homeomorphisms of spectral spaces
\Spa\mathcal{A}=\Spa\mathcal{A}^{\red}=\Spa\mathcal{A}^{\dagger-\red}
.

(7)

The
\infty
-category
\Mod(\mathcal{A})
of solid
\mathcal{A}
-modules satisfies descent for the analytic topology of
\Spa\mathcal{A}
.

Having stated some basic properties for the category of bounded affinoid rings one can formally defined the category of derived Tate adic space by gluing bounded affinoid rings along open covers (Definition
2.7.22
).

Definition 1.0.2
.

We let
\Aff^{b}_{R}
denote be the opposite category of bounded affinoid rings, an object in
\Aff^{b}_{R}
is called a
bounded affinoid space
. Given
\mathcal{A}
a bounded affinoid ring we let
\AnSpec\mathcal{A}\in\Aff^{b}_{R}
be its
analytic spectrum
. A
derived Tate adic space
is a sheaf on anima
X:\Aff^{b,\op}_{R}\to\Ani
for the analytic topology of
\Aff^{b}_{R}
, such that
X
admits an open analytic cover by bounded affinoid spaces. Given
X
a derived Tate adic space we let
|X|=\varinjlim_{\AnSpec\mathcal{A}\to X}|X|
denote its underlying topological space. We let
\AdicSp_{R}
denote the
\infty
-category of derived Tate adic spaces over
R
.

The previous definition of derived Tate adic spaces is an extension of Huber’s analytic adic spaces. The choice of the analytic topology to glue bounded affinoid rings is an arbitrary choice that was taken to compare with the classical theory. We will see in §
3
that one can still do geometry in different kind of Grothendieck topologies, as long as one has descent for the six functor formalism of quasi-coherent sheaves.

§
3
Tate stacks

In this section we continue developing the theory of derived Tate adic spaces. In §
3.1
we recall some language in the theory of abstract six functor formalisms of
[
Man22b
,
Man22a
]
and
[
Sch23
]
. Then in §
3.2
, we use
[
Sch23
, Theorem 4.20]
to construct a very large six functor formalism on a category of analytic
\mathscr{D}
-stacks on bounded affinoid rings (Definitions
3.2.7
and
3.2.10
). We define different notions of morphisms of finite presentations in analytic rings in §
3.3
, study basic properties of cotangent complexes in analytic rings in §
3.4
, and introduce the notion of solid smooth and étale maps of derived Tate adic spaces in §
3.5
. We obtain an equivalent description of solid smooth and étale maps as formally smooth and étale maps of solid finite presentation (Theorem
3.5.6
and Corollary
3.5.16
).

Theorem 1.0.3
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism of solid finite presentation of derived Tate adic spaces over
R=\mathbb{Z}((\pi))
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

The map
f
is formally smooth (resp. étale).

(2)

The map
f
is solid smooth (resp. étale), namely, locally in the analytic topology of
X
and
Y
the map
f
is standard solid smooth (resp. standard solid étale).

We continue by studying some properties of the categories of modules of derived Tate adic spaces with the goal of proving Serre duality (following an argument of Clausen and Scholze by deformation to the normal cone). We have the following result (Theorem
3.6.15
).

Theorem 1.0.4
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism locally of solid finite presentation of derived Tate adic spaces. The following hold:

(1)

The map
f
admits
!
-functors in the six functor formalisms of solid quasi-coherent sheaves.

(2)

If
f
is solid smooth (resp. étale) then
f
is cohomologically smooth (resp. cohomologically étale) for the six functor formalism of quasi-coherent sheaves. Furthermore, there is a natural identification
f^{!}1_{Y}=\Omega^{d}_{X/Y}[d]
where
d
is the relative dimension of
f
, and
1_{Y}
is the unit in the category of solid quasi-coherent sheaves on
Y
(i.e. the structural sheaf).

Finally, we introduce a new deformation condition called
\dagger
-formally smoothness and étaleness, related with liftings along
\dagger
-nilpotent ideals such as
\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})
. Then, we prove the following lifting property for solid smooth and solid étale maps (Proposition
3.7.5
).

Proposition 1.0.5
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a solid smooth (resp étale) map of derived Tate adic spaces over
R
. Then
f
is
\dagger
-formally smooth (resp. étale) locally in the analytic topology of
X
and
Y
.

In the next sections we apply all the previous theory on derived rigid geometry to study Cartier duality of vector bundles, and to construct a six functor formalism for analytic
D
-modules.

§
4
Cartier duality for vector bundles

With the introduction of derived Tate adic spaces, new commutative group objects appear in the nature making possible new incarnations of Cartier duality. In this section we do not pretend to give a definition or an abstract set up for a Cartier duality theorem, instead we explore new examples of Cartier duality arising from analytic subspaces of vector bundles. For technical reasons, these Cartier duality isomorphisms are easier to describe if we restrict ourselves to analytic geometry over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, for simplicity let us even restrict ourselves to derived Tate adic spaces over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. We start with the definition of the analytic incarnations of vector bundles (Construction
4.3.9
)

Definition 1.0.6
.

Let
X
be a derived Tate adic space over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and
\mathscr{F}
a vector bundle over
X
of rank
d
. We let
\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F})^{\an}
be its geometric realization as an derived Tate adic space over
X
. Let
\iota:X\to\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F})^{\an}
be the zero section, and let
\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F})^{\dagger}
denote the overconvergent neighbourhood of zero.

So, if
X=\AnSpec\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is a point, and
\mathscr{E}
is free of rank
1
, the space
\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F})^{\an}
is isomorphic to the affine analytic line
\mathbb{A}^{1,\an}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. Similarly,
\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F})^{\dagger}
is nothing but the space
\mathbb{G}_{a,\mathbb{Q}_{p}}^{\dagger}
given by the analytic spectrum of the algebra
\mathbb{Q}_{p}\{T\}^{\dagger}=\varinjlim_{n}\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle\frac{T}{p^{n}}\rangle
of functions that overconverge at
0\in\mathbb{A}^{1,\an}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. The following theorem describes the theory of six functors for analytic vector bundles and their classifying stacks (Proposition
4.2.5
and Theorem
4.3.13
).

Theorem 1.0.7
.

Let
X
be a derived Tate adic space over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and let
\mathscr{E}
be a vector bundle over
X
of rank
d
.

(1)

Let
f:\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F})^{\an}\to X
, then
f
is cohomologically smooth and there are natural equivalences
f^{!}1_{X}=f^{*}\bigwedge^{d}\mathscr{F}^{\vee}[d]
and
f_{!}f^{!}1_{X}=\mathrm{Sym}^{\dagger}_{X}(\mathscr{F})
, where
\mathrm{Sym}^{\dagger}_{X}(\mathscr{F})
is the sheaf of functions of
\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F}^{\vee})^{\dagger}
.

(2)

The map
f:\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F})^{\dagger}\to X
satisfies
f_{*}=f_{!}
.

(3)

Let
g:X/\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F})^{\an}\to X
. Then
g
is cohomologically smooth with
g^{!}1_{X}=g^{*}\bigwedge^{d}\mathscr{F}[-d]
, and there is a natural equivalence
g_{!}\cong g_{*}[-2d]
.

(4)

Let
g:X/\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F})^{\dagger}\to X
. Then
g
is cohomologically smooth with
g^{!}1_{X}=g^{*}\bigwedge^{d}\mathscr{F}[d]
, and there is a natural equivalence
g_{!}\cong g_{*}
.

The analytic Cartier duality theorem is the following statement (Theorems
4.2.7
and
4.3.13
).

Theorem 1.0.8
.

Let
X
be a derived Tate adic space over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and let
\mathscr{F}
be a vector bundle over
X
of rank
d
.

(1)

There is a bilinear morphism
F:\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F})^{\dagger}\times(X/\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F}^{\vee})^{\an})\to B\mathbb{G}_{m}
such that
F^{*}(\mathscr{O}(1))
is an isomorphism
\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F})^{\dagger}\xrightarrow{\sim}X/\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F}^{\vee})^{\an}
in the category of Fourier-Moukai kernels for the six functor formalisms of solid quasi-coherent sheaves. Furthermore, the inverse of
F^{*}(\mathscr{O}(1))
is given by
F^{*}(\mathscr{O}(-1))\otimes\bigwedge^{d}\mathscr{F}^{\vee}[-d]
.

(2)

There is a bilinear morphism
G:\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F})^{\an}\times(X/\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F}^{\vee})^{\dagger})\to B\mathbb{G}_{m}
such that
G^{*}(\mathscr{O}(1))
is an isomorphism
\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F})^{\an}\xrightarrow{\sim}X/\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F}^{\vee})^{\dagger}
in the category of Fourier-Moukai kernels for the six functor formalisms of solid quasi-coherent sheaves. Furthermore, the inverse of
G^{*}(\mathscr{O}(1))
is given by
G^{*}(\mathscr{O}(-1))\otimes\bigwedge^{d}\mathscr{F}^{\vee}[d]
.

Corollary 1.0.9
.

In the notation of Theorem
1.0.8
, there are natural equivalences of stable
\infty
-categories given by Fourier-Mukai transforms

FM_{1}:\Mod_{{\square}}(\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F})^{\dagger})\xrightarrow{\sim}\Mod_{{\square}}(X/\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F}^{\vee})^{\an}).

and

FM_{2}:\Mod_{{\square}}(\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F})^{\an})\xrightarrow{\sim}\Mod_{{\square}}(X/\mathbb{V}(\mathscr{F}^{\vee})^{\dagger}).

We also review algebraic Cartier duality in Theorem
4.2.7
, and show two other versions of analytic Cartier duality in Theorems
4.3.8
and
4.3.20
.

§
5
Algebraic and analytic de Rham stacks

The first construction of the de Rham stack dates back to Simpson in his papers
[
Sim96
,
ST97
]
. In this work we propose an analogue of this construction in analytic geometry, more precisely in rigid analytic geometry over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. We begin by extending the construction of the algebraic de Rham stack from algebraic geometry to condensed mathematics. Specialized to derived Tate adic spaces we get the following (Definition
5.1.1
).

Definition 1.0.10

(Algebraic de Rham stack)
.

Let
R=\mathbb{Z}((\pi))
and let
X
be a derived Tate adic space over
R\otimes\mathbb{Q}
. The algebraic de Rham prestack of
X
is the presheaf on
\Aff^{b}_{R\otimes\mathbb{Q}}
given by

X_{dR}^{\alg}(\mathcal{A})=\varinjlim_{I\to\mathcal{A}}X(\cone(I\to\mathcal{A}))

where
I
runs over all the uniformly nilpotent ideals of
\mathcal{A}
. The de Rham stack of
X
is the sheafification of the de Rham prestack in the
\mathscr{D}
-topology, and we also denote it by
X_{dR}^{\alg}
. Given a morphism
X\to Y
of derived Tate adic spaces, the relative algebraic de Rham stack
X_{dR,Y}^{\alg}
is the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{dR,Y}^{\alg}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{dR}^{\alg}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{dR}^{\alg}.}

We call
\Mod_{{\square}}(X^{\alg}_{dR,Y})
the category of algebraic
D_{X/Y}
-modules.

In analogy to the algebraic de Rham stack, and in view that there is a second kind of nil-radical in the category of bounded affinoid rings (Theorem
1.0.1
(4)), we define the analytic de Rham stack as follows (Definition
5.2.2
).

Definition 1.0.11

(Analytic de Rham stack)
.

Let
X
be a derived Tate adic space over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, the analytic de Rham prestack is the presheaf on
\Aff^{b}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
defined by

X_{dR}(\mathcal{A}):=X(\mathcal{A}^{\dagger-\red}).

The analytic de Rham stack is the
\mathscr{D}
-sheafification of the analytic de Rham prestack, and we also denote it by
X_{dR}
. Let
f:X\to Y
be a map of derived Tate adic spaces, we define the relative de Rham stack
X_{dR,Y}
to be the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{dR,Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{dR}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{dR}.}

We call
\Mod_{{\square}}(X_{dR,Y})
the category of analytic
D_{X/Y}
-modules.

The main theorem on de Rham stacks is the following (see Corollaries
5.2.5
and
5.2.13
, Propositions
5.1.2
and
5.2.3
, and Theorems
5.1.12
,
5.1.13
,
5.2.10
,
5.3.7
and
5.4.1
).

Theorem 1.0.12
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism of derived Tate adic spaces locally of solid finite presentation and write
f_{dR}^{\alg}:X_{dR}^{\alg}\to Y_{dR}^{\alg}
and
f_{dR}:X_{dR}\to Y_{dR}
for the associated maps at the level of stacks.

(1)

The formation of
X\mapsto X_{dR}
and
X\mapsto X^{\alg}_{dR}
commutes with colimits and finite limits at the level of prestacks.

(2)

The maps
f_{dR}^{\alg}
and
f_{dR}
admit
!
-functors.

(3)

Suppose that
X
is a rigid space over a non-archimedean extension
K/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, then the map
h:X\to X_{dR,K}
is a
\mathscr{D}
-cover. In particular, quasi-coherent sheaves on
X_{dR,K}
descent along
h
.

(4)

(Kashiwara equivalence) Let
X\to Y
be a Zariski closed immersion of derived Tate adic spaces, and let
Y^{\dagger/X}
be the overconvergent neighbourhood of
X
in
Y
. Then there is an equivalence of analytic de Rham stacks
X_{dR}=Y^{\dagger/X}_{dR}
. In particular, analytic
D
-modules of
Y
supported on
X
are equivalent to analytic
D
-modules of
X
.

(5)

Suppose that
f
is solid smooth (resp. étale), then the maps
f_{dR}^{\alg}
and
f_{dR}
are cohomologically smooth (resp. étale).

(6)

Suppose that
f
is solid smooth, then we have natural equivalences
f_{dR}^{\alg,!}1_{Y_{dR}^{\alg}}=1_{X_{dR}^{\alg}}
and
f_{dR}^{!}1_{Y_{dR}}=1_{X_{dR}}[2d]
where
d
is the relative dimension of
f
.

(7)

Suppose that
f
is solid smooth and consider the map
g:X_{dR,Y}^{\alg}\to X_{dR,Y}
, then
g
admits
!
-functors,
g_{*}
satisfies the projection formula, and there is a natural equivalence

1_{X_{dR,Y}}\xrightarrow{\sim}g_{*}1_{X_{dR,Y}^{\alg}}.

In particular, the pullback functor
g^{*}
is fully faithful and induces an embedding of analytic
D_{X/Y}
-modules into algebraic
D_{X/Y}
-modules.

(8)

Suppose that
f
is solid smooth, and denote
f_{dR,Y}^{\alg}:X_{dR,Y}^{\alg}\to Y
and
f_{dR,Y}:X_{dR,Y}\to Y
the natural maps. Then there are natural equivalences of de Rham cohomology

DR(X/Y):=f_{dR,Y,*}^{\alg}1_{X_{dR,Y}^{alg}}=f_{dR,Y,*}1_{X_{dR,Y}}.

Furthermore,
DR(X/Y)
can be naturally promoted to a filtered object given by the Hodge filtration, with graded pieces

\gr^{i}DR(X/Y)=\Omega^{i}_{X/Y},

extending the Hodge filtration for smooth maps of rigid spaces.

Remark 1.0.13
.

In order to prove Theorem
1.0.12
we need to consider a variation of the de Rham stacks given by the filtered de Rham stacks
X_{dR^{+}}^{\alg}
and
X_{dR^{+}}
, see Definitions
5.1.1
and
5.2.2
.

§
6
Analytic de Rham stack and locally analytic representations

Finally we end with the relation between the analytic de Rham stack, the theory of solid locally analytic representations of
p
-adic Lie groups and a general notion of equivariant analytic
D
-module, generalizing definitions of
[
Ard21
]
.

We first briefly discuss the relation with representation theory. Let
G
be a
p
-adic Lie group, and denote by
G^{la}
and
G^{sm}
the analytic spaces defined by
G
endowed with the sheaf of locally analytic and locally constant functions respectively. In
[
RJRC23
]
we proved that the category of solid locally analytic and smooth representations are given by the category of solid quasi-coherent sheaves of the classifying stacks
*/G^{la}
and
*/G^{sm}
respectively. A first relation between
p
-adic Lie groups, representation theory and the de Rham stacks is encoded in the following proposition (see Lemma
6.2.2
).

Proposition 1.0.14
.

Let
G
be a
p
-adic Lie group. There is a natural equivalence

G^{la}_{dR}=G^{sm}.

In particular, we have an equivalence of classifying stacks

(*/G^{la})_{dR}=*/G^{sm}.

Next, both solid locally analytic representations and analytic
D
-modules extend to a theory of equivariant analytic
D
-modules. To motivate the definition let us make the following observation.

Remark 1.0.15
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a solid smooth map of derived Tate adic spaces. By Proposition
1.0.5
the map
h:X\to X_{dR,Y}
is an epimorphism of
\mathscr{D}
-stacks. The Čech nerve of
h
is given by the
analytic de Rham groupoid
, whose
n
-th is the overconvergent neighbourhood of the diagonal map
\Delta^{n+1}_{Y}X\to X^{\times_{Y}n+1}
, namely, the analytic space
(\Delta^{n+1}_{Y}X)^{\dagger}
whose functions are given by functions of
X^{\times_{Y}n+1}
that overconverge the locally closed subspace
|\Delta^{n+1}_{Y}|\subset|X^{\times_{Y}n+1}|
. This provides an equivalence of
\mathscr{D}
-stacks

X_{dR,Y}:=\varinjlim_{[n]\in\Delta^{\op}}(\Delta^{n+1}_{Y}X)^{\dagger}

We extend the analytic de Rham groupoid to
\dagger
-smooth groupoids
in Definition
6.1.3
. Roughly speaking, these are groupoid objects that look like the overconvergent neighbourhoods of the zero sections of vector bundles. Prototipical examples of
\dagger
-smooth groupoids are constructed from Lie algebroids over rigid spaces as explained in Example
6.1.8
(3). We recall the definition of a normal map in groupoids, and introduce equivariant analytic
D
-modules in great generality (Definition
6.2.5
).

Definition 1.0.16
.

Let
X
be a derived Tate adic space over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and
G
a
p
-adic Lie group acting locally analytically on
X
. Let
\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}
be a
\dagger
-smooth group over
X
and let
\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}\to G^{la}\times X
be a map of groupoids with given normal quotient
G^{la}/\mathbb{H}^{la}
. We define the category of analytic equivariant
D(G^{la}/\mathbb{H}^{\dagger})
-modules to be
\Mod(X/(G^{la}/\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}))
.

Equivariant
D
-modules over solid smooth maps have a good cohomological behaviour (Theorem
6.2.6
):

Theorem 1.0.17
.

Let
X\to Y
be a solid smooth morphism of derived Tate adic spaces over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
of relative dimension
d
, and let
G
be a
p
-adic Lie group of dimension
g
acting locally analytically on
X
over
Y
. Let
\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}
be a
\dagger
-smooth group over
X
of relative dimension
e
, and let
\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}\to G^{la}\times X
be a map of groupoids over
X
with given normal quotient
G^{la}/\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}
. Then
g:X/(G^{la}/\mathbb{H}^{\dagger})\to Y
is cohomologically smooth and its underlying
G^{la}
-equivariant invertible sheaf is equivalent to

\Omega^{d}_{X/Y}[d]\otimes\bigwedge^{g}\Lie G[g]\otimes\bigwedge^{e}\ell_{\mathbb{H}^{\dagger}/X}[-e].

Notations and conventions

Throughout this paper we freely use the language of higher category theory and higher algebra of
[
Lur09
]
and
[
Lur17
]
, the theory of condensed mathematics of
[
CS19
,
CS20
,
CS22
]
, and the theory of abstract six functor formalisms of
[
Man22b
,
Man22a
]
and
[
Sch23
]
.

To avoid any confusion,
(\infty,1)
-categories will be called
\infty
-categories while classical categories will be called
1
-categories. We let
\Cat_{\infty}
denote the large
\infty
-category of
\infty
-categories, let
\Cat_{\infty}^{\colim}
be the subcategory with objects given by
\infty
-categories admitting small colimits and morphisms given by colimit preserving functors, and let
\Cat^{\ex}_{\infty}
be the subcategory of stable
\infty
-categories with exact functors. We let
\mathcal{P}r^{L}
(resp.
\mathcal{P}r^{R}
) be the
\infty
-category of presentable
\infty
-categories with colimit preserving functors (resp. accessible and limit preserving functors). Combining adjectives, we let
\mathcal{P}r^{L,\ex}
denote the
\infty
-category of presentable stable
\infty
-categories.

Following Lurie, the previous categories have natural cartesian symmetric monoidal structures, we have the following translation of commutative algebra objects with respect to the cartesian product:

\bullet

\CAlg(\Cat_{\infty})
is naturally equivalent to the
\infty
-category of symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories
\Cat^{\otimes}_{\infty}
.

\bullet

\CAlg(\Cat_{\infty}^{\colim})
is the
\infty
-category of colimit preserving symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories, i,.e.
\infty
-categories admitting small colimits, endowed with a symmetric monoidal structure that commutes with colimits in each variable, and symmetric monoidal colimit preserving functors.

\bullet

\CAlg(\Cat_{\infty}^{\ex})
is the
\infty
-category of stable symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories, i.e. stable
\infty
-categories with a symmetric monoidal structure which is exact in each variable, and symmetric monoidal exact functors.

\bullet

\CAlg(\Cat_{\infty}^{\colim,\ex})
is the
\infty
-category of colimit preserving symmetric monoidal stable
\infty
-categories; it is the full subcategory of
\CAlg(\Cat_{\infty}^{\colim})
with objects having a underlying stable
\infty
-category.

\bullet

\CAlg(\mathcal{P}r^{L})
is the
\infty
-category of presentably symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories, i.e. presentable
\infty
-categories with a symmetric monoidal structure that commutes with colimits in each variable, and symmetric monoidal colimit preserving functors.

\bullet

\CAlg(\mathcal{P}r^{L,\ex})
is the
\infty
-category of presentably symmetric monoidal stable
\infty
-categories, i.e. presentable stable
\infty
-categories with a symmetric monoidal structure that commutes with colimits in each variable, and symmetric monoidal colimit preserving functors.

Given an arrow
f:X\to Y
in a pointed
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
, we let
[X\to Y]
and
\cofib[X\to Y]
denote the fiber and cofiber of
f
respectively. The notion of descendable algebra in a symmetric monoidal stable
\infty
-category will be used repeatedly along the document, we send to
[
Mat16
]
for its definition and main properties.

We let
\Prof
and
\Extdis
be the sites of profinite and extremally disconnected sets with covers given by finite jointly surjective maps. Given an
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
with finite products and small colimits, we let
\mathrm{Cond}(\mathcal{C})
be the condensification of
\mathcal{C}
, see
[
CS20
, §11.1]
and
[
Man22b
, Definition 2.1.1]
. Let
\mathcal{C}
be a
1
-category that admits small colimits and that is generated by small colimits under its compact projective objects
\mathcal{C}^{cp}
, we let
\Ani(\mathcal{C})
be the animation of
\mathcal{C}
, see
[
CS20
, §11.4]
.

We shall write
\AnRing
for the
\infty
-category of complete commutative analytic (animated) rings as in
[
Man22b
, Definition 2.3.10]
; unless otherwise specified all analytic rings will be assume to be objects in
\AnRing
. Given
\mathcal{A}
an analytic ring, we let
\underline{\mathcal{A}}
be its underlying condensed ring, and for
S\in\Extdis
we let
\mathcal{A}[S]
be the free
\mathcal{A}
-module generated by
S
, we also write
\AnRing_{\mathcal{A}}
for the slice
\infty
-category of analytic
\mathcal{A}
-algebras. We denote by
\Mod_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{A})
the
\infty
-category of animated
\mathcal{A}
-modules, and let
\Mod(\mathcal{A})
be its stabilization. Throughout this paper we use homotopical notation, so for a complex
M
the fundamental group
\pi_{i}(M)
is the same as the
(-i)
-th cohomology group
H^{-i}(M)
. We shall write
\Mod^{\heartsuit}(\mathcal{A})
for the heart of
\Mod(\mathcal{A})
.

Given
\mathcal{A}
an analytic ring, we shall write
\AniAlg_{\mathcal{A}}
for the
\infty
-category of animated
\mathcal{A}
-algebras, namely, the category of condensed animated
\underline{\mathcal{A}}
-algebras that are
\mathcal{A}
-complete. Given
B
an animated
\mathcal{A}
-algebra, we let
B_{\mathcal{A}/}
denote the analytic ring obtained by restriction of analytic ring structure from
\mathcal{A}
to
B
, see
[
Man22b
, Definition 2.3.13]
. More generally, given
B
an
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-algebra in
\Mod(\mathcal{A})
, we let
B_{\mathcal{A}/}
be the analytic ring with underlying condensed ring
\underline{B}
, and whose category of left modules is given by
\Mod(B_{\mathcal{A}/})=\Mod_{B}(\Mod(\mathcal{A}))
, see Definition
2.1.1
.

In this paper all analytic rings are complete, and we always consider colimits as complete analytic rings unless otherwise specified. Let
\mathcal{A}
be an analytic ring, we will write
-\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}-
and
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{A}}(-,-)
for the tensor product and the internal
\mathrm{Hom}
space on
\Mod(\mathcal{A})
, omitting in this way further decorations regarding derived functors. In case we want to consider a classical tensor or Hom space for objects sitting in degree
0
, we will write
\pi_{0}(-\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}-)
and
\pi_{0}(\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{A}}(-,-))
instead. For
\mathcal{C}
a
1
-category with all small colimits and generated by compact projective objects, an object
X
in
\Ani(\mathcal{C})
is called
static
if it belongs to the essential image of
\mathcal{C}\to\Ani(\mathcal{C})
. We call an analytic ring
\mathcal{A}
static if
\underline{\mathcal{A}}
is a static condensed animated ring, i.e. a usual condensed ring sitting in degree
0
.

2.
Derived Tate adic spaces

Clausen and Scholze’s analytic geometry is a framework where classical algebraic, archimedean and non-archimedean geometries can be treated as equals. Throughout this paper we will focus on the non-archimedean side of the theory, namely the solid theory. By taking as inspiration classical (derived) algebraic geometry (eg.
[
Lur04
]
), and Huber’s theory of (analytic) adic spaces
[
Hub96
]
, we will introduce a category of derived Tate adic spaces
1
1

1

Following the conventions of Clausen-Scholze, we will replace the adjective
analytic
on Huber rings by the adjective
Tate
, meaning that we work with Huber rings admitting a pseudo-uniformizer.
.

As primary point, we need to introduce the categories of rings that serve as building blocks of our theories. The first approximation will be modelled by analytic rings associated to generalized Huber pairs
[
Man22b
, Definition 2.12.8]
, called in this paper
solid affinoid rings
, see Definition
2.6.6
. Roughly speaking, the data of a solid affinoid ring is provided by a pair
(A,A^{+})
, where
A
is a solid animated ring, and
A^{+}\subset\pi_{0}(A)(*)
is a discrete subring that determines which variables of
A
are “solid”.

The next step towards non-archimedean analytic geometry requires some technical constructions. In one hand, we want to differentiate algebraic varieties from rigid spaces. On the other hand, we want to define a class of rings that mimics the relevant features of analytic complete Huber pairs
(A,A^{+})
, endowed with a fixed pseudo-uniformizer
\pi
. By
[
And21
]
, the
1
-category of complete Huber pairs embeds fully faithfully in the category of analytic rings. Since
(A,A^{+})
admits a pseudo-uniformizer, the subring
A^{0}
of power bounded elements is an open subring of
A
. We can determine the objects in
A^{0}
in the following way: consider the map
R:=\mathbb{Z}((\pi))\to A
defined by the pseudo-uniformizer
\pi
. An element
a\in A
belongs to
A^{0}
if and only if the map
R[T]\to A
sending
T\mapsto a
extends to
R\langle T\rangle\to A
, where
R\langle T\rangle
is the Tate algebra of
R
. Using this observation, we are able to define a class of
bounded affinoid rings
that provides the building blocks for our non-archimedean geometry.

After the introduction of the category of bounded affinoid rings, we extend the construction of the adic spectrum
\Spa\mathcal{A}
from Huber pairs to bounded affinoid rings, then, by gluing along rational covers, we define the category of (analytic) derived adic spaces, which is a large generalization of the classical
1
-category of (analytic) adic spaces endowed with a fix pseudo-uniformizer.

2.1.
Preliminaries

In this section we address some technical results and definitions that will be used throughout the paper. The reader can skip it on a first reading and come back when the corresponding statement is referenced.

2.1.1.
Analytic
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings

The definition of (complete) analytic ring and the main properties in
[
CS20
, Lecture XII]
can be extended to connective
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-condensed algebras instead of animated rings with minor changes in the proofs. Moreover, using
[
CS19
, Proposition 12.20]
one can even extend the definition of analytic ring to general
\mathbb{E}_{1}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebras. In this paper we will essentially only use the animated definition of analytic ring (
[
Man22b
, Definition 2.3.10]
). However, it is useful to have this slightly more general notion in mind, for example, when constructing idempotent algebras in the category of complete modules of analytic rings.

Definition 2.1.1
.

An analytic
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-ring
\mathcal{A}
is the data of a condensed
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-ring
\underline{\mathcal{A}}
together with a full subcategory
\Mod(\mathcal{A})\subset\Mod(\underline{\mathcal{A}})
satisfying the following properties.

(1)

\Mod(\mathcal{A})
is stable under small limits and colimits in
\Mod(\underline{\mathcal{A}})
.

(2)

For all
S\in\Extdis
and
M\in\Mod(\mathcal{A})
the object
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\underline{\mathcal{A}}}(\underline{\mathcal{A}}[S],M)
belongs to
\Mod(\mathcal{A})
.

(3)

The inclusion
\Mod(\mathcal{A})\subset\Mod(\underline{\mathcal{A}})
has a left adjoint
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\underline{\mathcal{A}}}-
.

We say that an analytic ring is complete if the natural map
\underline{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathcal{A}[*]
is an equivalence. An analytic
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring is an analytic
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-ring whose underlying ring has a structure of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring. A morphism
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
of analytic
\mathbb{E}_{1}
(resp.
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
)-rings is a morphism of condensed rings such that the forgetful functor
\Mod(\underline{\mathcal{B}})\to\Mod(\underline{\mathcal{A}})
sends
\Mod(\mathcal{B})
to
\Mod(\mathcal{A})
. We let let
\AnCRing
denote the
\infty
-category of complete
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-analytic rings.

Remark 2.1.2
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an analytic
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring, then
\Mod(\mathcal{A})
is naturally a symmetric monoidal category. Indeed, the same argument of
[
CS20
, Proposition 12.4]
shows that the kernel of
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\underline{\mathcal{A}}}-
is a tensor ideal. Furthermore, by the proof of
[
Man22b
, Proposition 2.3.8]
, we have a natural transformation of functors
\Mod(\underline{(-)})\Rightarrow\Mod(-)
given by the analytification functors.

The following lemma says that an analytic
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring is completely determined by its category of complete modules

Lemma 2.1.3
.

Let
\underline{\mathbb{S}}
be the sphere spectrum considered as a condensed spectrum, let
\Mod(\underline{\mathbb{S}})
be the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category of condensed spectra and
\CAlg(\Cat_{\infty}^{\colim})_{\underline{\mathbb{S}}/}
the
\infty
-category of colimit preserving symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories tensored over
\Mod(\underline{\mathbb{S}})
. Then the functor
\Mod(-):\AnCRing\to\CAlg(\Cat_{\infty}^{\colim})_{\underline{\mathbb{S}}/}
sending an analytic
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring
\mathcal{A}
to the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
\Mod(\mathcal{A})
is a fully faithful embedding.

Proof.

Consider
\CAlg(\Mod(\underline{\mathbb{S}}))
the
\infty
-category of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-condensed rings and let

\Mod(\underline{(-)}):\CAlg(\Mod(\underline{\mathbb{S}}))\to\CAlg(\Cat_{\infty}^{\colim})_{\underline{\mathbb{S}}/}

be the functor sending a ring to its category of modules, by
[
Lur17
, Corollary 4.8.5.21]
this functor is fully faithful. Furthermore,
[
Man22b
, Proposition 2.3.8]
provides a natural transformation
\Mod(\underline{(-)})\Rightarrow\Mod(-)
of functors
\AnCRing\to\CAlg(\Cat_{\infty}^{\colim})_{\underline{\mathbb{S}}/}
given by the analytification functor
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\underline{\mathcal{A}}}-:\Mod_{\underline{\mathcal{A}}}\to\Mod_{\mathcal{A}}
.

Let
A,B\in\AnCRing
, observe that any colimit preserving
\Mod(\underline{\mathbb{S}})
-tensored symmetric monoidal morphism
f^{*}:\Mod(\mathcal{A})\to\Mod(\mathcal{B})
is compatible with the natural morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Mod(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Mod(\mathcal{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\End}_{\Mod(\mathcal{A})}(\mathcal{A}[*])-\Mod(\Mod(\underline{\mathbb{S}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\End}_{\Mod(\mathcal{B})}(\mathcal{B}[*])-\Mod(\Mod(\underline{\mathbb{S}})),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}

but
\underline{\End}_{\Mod(\mathcal{A})}(\mathcal{A}[*])=\underline{\mathcal{A}}
and
\underline{\End}_{\Mod(\mathcal{B})}(\mathcal{B}[*])=\underline{\mathcal{B}}
since the analytic rings are complete. Then, we have a natural commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Mod(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Mod(\mathcal{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Mod(\underline{\mathcal{A}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Mod(\underline{\mathcal{B}}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\underline{\mathcal{A}}}-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{B}}\otimes_{\underline{\mathcal{A}}}-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\underline{\mathcal{B}}}-}

which shows that
f^{*}
is naturally equivalent to
\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}-
by definition of the analytic base change. Therefore, by definition of the mapping space of analytic rings as a full subanima of the mapping space of the underlying condensed rings, cf.
[
CS20
, Lecture XII]
or
[
Man22b
, Definition 2.3.1 (d)]
, the mapping space from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
in
\AnCRing
is naturally equivalent to the mapping space from
\Mod(\mathcal{A})
to
\Mod(\mathcal{B})
in
\CAlg(\Cat_{\infty}^{\colim})_{\underline{\mathbb{S}}/}
, which finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 2.1.4
.

The previous lemma only applies for analytic
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings and not for analytic animated rings. The obstruction for the statement to hold for analytic animated rings is that the forgetful functor of animated rings towards
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings is not fully faithful. Nevertheless, the lemma holds for analytic animated rings over
\mathbb{Q}
. In general, the functor
\Mod(-)
is always conservative.

2.1.2.
Generalized Huber pairs

For future reference we define generalized Huber pairs, see
[
Man22b
, Definition 2.12.8]
. Let
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}
denote the analytic ring of solid integers, mapping a profinite set
S=\varprojlim_{i}S_{i}\in\Pro(\FinSet)
to the condensed abelian group
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]=\varprojlim_{i}\mathbb{Z}[S_{i}]
. More generally, for
R
a
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra of finite type we shall write
R_{{\square}}
for the analytic ring such that
R_{{\square}}[S]=\varprojlim_{i}R[S_{i}]
, and for
R
a discrete ring we set
R_{{\square}}[S]=\varinjlim_{A\subset R}A_{{\square}}[S]
where
A
runs over all the finitely generated subrings of
R
, cf.
[
CS19
, Examples 7.3]
.

Definition 2.1.5
.

A generalized Huber pair consists on a tuple
(A,S)
with
A
an animated
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}
-algebra, and
S
a set of elements
S\subset\pi_{0}(A)(*)
, such that
A
is
\mathbb{Z}[X_{s}]
-solid for all
s\in S
, with
\mathbb{Z}[X_{s}]\to A
a map sending
X_{s}\mapsto s
. We let
(A,S)_{{\square}}
denote the analytic ring
A_{\mathbb{Z}[X_{s}:s\in S]_{{\square}}/}
.

Remark 2.1.6
.

In the notation of Definition
2.1.5
, the analytic ring structure of
(A,S)_{{\square}}
only depends on the variables
S
, and not on the lifts
\mathbb{Z}[X_{s}]\to A
, thanks to
[
CS20
, Proposition 12.21]
.

2.2.
Categorified locale

In the following section we recall the formalism of categorified locales of
[
CS22
, Lectures V-VII]
and
[
Aok23
]
. The notion of categorified locale replaces the more classical definition of locally ringed space; the building blocks of analytic geometry are analytic rings, and these provide the data of a condensed ring and a category of complete modules. Thus, instead of gluing rings as in classical algebraic geometry we need to glue the categories of modules, the language of categorified locales formalizes this idea. Moreover, categorifies locales offer a clean understanding of open and closed immersions from a six functor point of view, these notions will be repetitively used throughout the paper.

Let
\CAlg(\Cat^{\colim,\ex}_{\infty})
be the
\infty
-category of colimit preserving symetric monoidal stable
\infty
-categories, with morphisms denoted by pullback functors
f^{*}:C\to D
. In §
3
we shall restrict ourselves to the framework of presentably symmetric monoidal stable
\infty
-categories
\CAlg(\mathcal{P}r^{L,\ex})
; as it is explain in
[
Aok23
]
, this is not an important restriction since we will eventually take categories of
\kappa
-small condensed sets for some cut-off cardinal
\kappa
. Given
C\in\CAlg(\Cat^{\colim,\ex}_{\infty})
, we let
\mathcal{S}(C)
denote the class of isomorphism classes of idempotent algebras in
C
, that is, the class of isomorphism classes of objects
A\in C
endowed with a morphism from the tensor unit
1\to A
such that the arrow

A\xrightarrow{1\otimes\id}A\otimes A

is an equivalence. We endow
\mathcal{S}(C)
with a partial order as follows:
A\leq A^{\prime}
if and only if there is an arrow
A^{\prime}\to A
commuting with the unit maps. By
[
Aok23
, Theorem 3.13]
, if
C
is presentably symmetric monoidal, the category of idempotent algebras is in fact essentially small, so
\mathcal{S}(C)
defines a honest poset.

Proposition 2.2.1

(
[
CS22
, Proposition 5.3]
)
.

The poset
\mathcal{S}(C)
is a locale with closed subspaces
Z\in\mathcal{S}(C)
defined by the isomorphism classes of idempotent algebras
A
. More explicitly, the following hold:

(1)

The “empty subset” corresponds to
0
.

(2)

The “whole space” corresponds to
1
.

(3)

The “intersection”
Z\cap Z^{\prime}
corresponds to
A\otimes A^{\prime}
.

(4)

An “arbitrary intersection”
\bigcap_{i}Z_{i}
corresponds to
\varinjlim_{i}A_{i}
.

(5)

The “union”
Z\cup Z^{\prime}
corresponds to the fiber
B=[A\bigoplus A^{\prime}\to A\otimes A^{\prime}]
together with the unit
1\to B
induced by the map
1\xrightarrow{(1,-1)}A\oplus A^{\prime}
.

Let
Z\in\mathcal{S}(C)
correspond to
A
. The closed subspace
Z
has a natural category of
modules supported on
Z
defined by the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
C(Z):=\Mod_{A}(C)
of
A
-modules in
C
. For
Z\in\mathcal{S}(C)
, the category
C(Z)
is a tensor ideal of
C
stable under all limits and colimits. The
frame

\mathcal{S}(C)^{\op}
can be thought as the open complements of the class of closed subspaces in
\mathcal{S}(C)
. Let
U\in\mathcal{S}(C)^{\op}
be the open complement of
Z\in\mathcal{S}(C)
, we can define an
\infty
-category of modules on
U
by taking the localization
C(U)=C/C(Z)
. One can explicitly define natural six functors associated to open and closed immersions.

Definition 2.2.2
.

Let
Z\in\mathcal{S}(C)
be a closed subspace with associated idempotent algebra
A
and complementary open
U
. We define the following functors:

(1)

The upper star functors
\iota^{*}_{Z}:C\to C(Z)
and
j^{*}_{U}:C\to C(U)
given by
\iota^{*}_{Z}M=A\otimes M
and the natural projection respectively.

(2)

The lower star functors
\iota_{Z,*}:C(Z)\to C
and
j_{U,*}:C(U)\to C
given by the forgetful functor and
j_{U,*}j^{*}_{U}M=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{C}([1\to A],M)
for
M\in C
.

(3)

The upper shriek functors
\iota^{!}_{Z}:C\to C(Z)
and
j^{!}_{U}:C\to C(U)
given by
\iota^{!}_{Z}M=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{C}(A,M)
and
j^{!}_{U}=j^{*}_{U}
respectively.

(4)

The lower shriek functors
\iota_{Z,!}:C(Z)\to C
and
j_{U,!}:C(U)\to C
given by
\iota_{Z,!}=\iota_{Z,*}
and
j_{!}j^{*}_{U}M=[1\to A]\otimes M
for
M\in C
respectively.

An edge
f^{*}:C\to D
in
\CAlg(\Cat^{\colim,\ex}_{\infty})
is said an
open (resp. closed) immersion
if it is equivalent to an edge of the form
j_{U}^{*}:C\to C(U)
(resp.
\iota_{Z}^{*}:C\to C(Z)
).

Remark 2.2.3
.

Let
M\in C
, by construction we have natural excision fiber sequences

j_{!}j^{*}M\to M\to\iota_{*}\iota^{*}M

and

\iota_{*}\iota^{!}M\to M\to j_{*}j^{*}M.

The following proposition tells us that the notions of open and closed immersions in
\CAlg(\Cat^{\colim,\ex}_{\infty})
behave categorically as expected from a
6
-functors point of view.

Proposition 2.2.4

(
[
CS22
, Proposition 6.5]
)
.

Let
f^{*}:C\to D
in
\CAlg(\Cat^{\colim,\ex}_{\infty})
.

(1)

f
is a closed immersion if and only if
f^{*}
has a fully faithful right adjoint
f_{*}
which preserves colimits and satisfies the projection formula

c\otimes f_{*}d\xrightarrow{\sim}f_{*}(f^{*}c\otimes d)

for
c\in C
and
d\in D
.

(2)

f
is an open immersion if and only if
f^{*}
has a fully faithful left adjoint
f_{!}:D\to C
which satisfies the projection formula

f_{!}(f^{*}c\otimes d)\xrightarrow{\sim}c\otimes f_{!}d

for
c\in C
and
d\in D
.

Finally, one has the following theorem saying that the functor mapping
U\in\mathcal{S}(C)
to
C(U)
is a sheaf for the natural topology of the locale.

Theorem 2.2.5

(
[
CS22
, Theorem 6.7]
)
.

(1)

There is a Grothendieck topology on
\CAlg(\Cat^{\colim,\ex}_{\infty})
where the sieve coverings over
C
are those which contain a set of open immersions whose corresponding open subsets cover
\mathcal{S}(C)
.

(2)

The identity functor
(\CAlg(\Cat^{\colim,\ex}_{\infty})^{\op})^{\op}\to\CAlg(\Cat^{\colim,\ex}_{\infty})
is a sheaf with respect to this Grothendieck topology.

(3)

The poset of open (resp. closed) immersions satisfies descent with respect to this Grothendieck topology.

With the previous preparations done we can define the
\infty
-category of categorified locale.

Definition 2.2.6

(
[
CS22
, Definition 7.1]
and
[
Aok23
, Definition 4.2]
)
.

A categorified locale is a triple
(X,C,f)
consisting on a locale
X
, a presentably symmetric monoidal stable
\infty
-category
C\in\CAlg(\Cat^{\colim,\ex}_{\infty})
, and a morphism of locales
f:\mathcal{S}(C)\to X
. Morphisms of categorified locales
F:(X,C,f)\to(Y,C,g)
consist on morphisms on the topological spaces,
F:X\to Y
and morphisms of presentably symmetric monoidal categories
f^{*}:D\to C
commuting with the arrows
f:\mathcal{S}(C)\to X
and
g:\mathcal{S}(D)\to Y
.

Given
C\in\CAlg(\Cat^{\colim,\ex}_{\infty})
we let
\CatLoc_{C}
be the
\infty
-category of
C
-tensored categorified locales, equivalently, the
\infty
-category of categorified locales
(X,C^{\prime},f)
with
C^{\prime}\in\CAlg(\Cat^{\colim,\ex}_{\infty})_{C/}
and morphisms given by
C
-linear morphisms of categorified locales.

We record the following lemma for future reference:

Lemma 2.2.7
.

Let
(X,C,f)
be a categorified locale. The category of dualizable (resp. invertible) objects on
C=C(X)
is a sheaf on
X
.

Proof.

By Lemma
[
Man22a
, Lemma 6.2]
, an object
\mathcal{L}\in C
is dualizable if and only if the natural map
\mathcal{L}\otimes\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{C}(\mathcal{L},1_{C})\to\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{C}(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L})
is an equivalence. Moreover, it is invertible if in addition the natural map
1_{C}\to\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{C}(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L})
is an equivalence. For
U\subset X
an open subspace, and objects
N,M\in C
, we have a natural equivalence

j_{U}^{*}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{C}(N,M)=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{C(U)}(j_{U}^{*}N,j_{U}^{*}M),

the lemma follows.
∎

2.3.
Tate adic spaces as categorified locale

The goal of this section is to construct a categorified locale for classical Tate Huber pairs using the main results of
[
And21
]
, obtaining an analogue of the construction of categorified locales for complex analytic spaces of
[
CS22
]
. In the following we only consider sheafy Tate Huber rings
(A,A^{+})
that admit a pseudo-uniformizer
\pi
, we let
\Spa(A,A^{+})
denote the adic spectrum of equivalence classes of continuous multiplicative valuations
|-|_{x}:A\to\Gamma
that satisfy
|a|_{x}\leq 1
for all
a\in A^{+}
, cf.
[
Hub96
]
. Let us recall some basic properties of the adic spectrum: by
[
Hub93
, Theorem 3.5]
,
\Spa(A,A^{+})
is a spectral space with a basis of quasi-compact open subsets given by rational localizations
\{|f_{i}|\leq|g|\neq 0:i=1,\ldots,d\}
for
f_{1},\ldots,f_{d},g\in A
elements generating
A
. Furthermore, since
\{|f_{i}|\leq|g|\neq 0:i=1,\ldots,d\}
is quasi-compact, there is
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\{|f_{i}|\leq|g|\neq 0:i=1,\ldots,d\}\subset\{|\pi^{n}|\leq|g|\neq 0\}
, so that we can always assume that some
f_{i}
is a pseudo-uniformizer of
A
. We have the following lemma

Lemma 2.3.1
.

Let
U\subset\Spa(A,A^{+})
be a rational subset, then
U
can be written as a composition of rational localizations of the form
\{|g|\leq 1\}
and
\{1\leq|g|\}
.

Proof.

Let us write
U=\{|f_{i}|\leq|g|\neq 0:i=1,\ldots,d\}
, with
f_{d}=\pi^{n}
. Then
U
is the composite of the rational localizations
\{1\leq|\pi^{-n}g|\}
and
\{|f_{i}/g|\leq 1\}
.
∎

In
[
And21
, Theorem 4.1]
, Andreychev proved that the functor mapping a rational localization
U\subset\Spa(A,A^{+})
to the category of solid modules
\Mod((\mathscr{O}(U),\mathscr{O}^{+}(U))_{{\square}})
is in fact a sheaf on
\CAlg(\Cat^{\colim,\ex}_{\infty})
(see Definition
2.1.5
for the notion of generalized Huber pair and the construction of
(A,S)_{{\square}}
). The next proposition says that this functor can be upgraded to a categorified locale.

Proposition 2.3.2
.

Let
(A,A^{+})
be an Tate Huber pair and let
X=\Spa(A,A^{+})^{\op}
be the poset of open subspaces of
\Spa(A,A^{+})
. Consider the functor

\Mod_{X,{\square}}(-):\Spa(A,A^{+})^{\op}\to\CAlg(\Cat^{\colim,\ex}_{\infty})

sending a rational localization
U
to
\Mod((\mathscr{O}(U),\mathscr{O}^{+}(U))_{{\square}})
. Then for any open
U\subset X
the localization functor

j^{*}_{U}:\Mod((A,A^{+})_{{\square}})\to\Mod_{X,{\square}}(U)

is an open localization in the sense of Proposition
2.2.4
(2).

Proof.

It suffices to prove the statement for rational localizations, by Lemma
2.3.1
we can even reduce to rational localizations of the form
\{|g|\leq 1\}
or
U=\{1\leq|g|\}
for
g\in A
. Then, by
[
And21
, Proposition 4.11]
we have

(\mathscr{O}(U),\mathscr{O}^{+}(U))_{{\square}}=(A,A^{+})_{{\square}}\otimes_{(\mathbb{Z}[T],\mathbb{Z})_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}\mbox{ and }(\mathscr{O}(U),\mathscr{O}^{+}(U))_{{\square}}=(A,A^{+})_{{\square}}\otimes_{(\mathbb{Z}[T],\mathbb{Z})_{{\square}}}(\mathbb{Z}[T^{\pm 1}],\mathbb{Z}[T^{-1}])_{{\square}}

respectively, where
T
is mapped to
g
. Thus, it suffices to show that
(\mathbb{Z}[T],\mathbb{Z})_{{\square}}\to\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
and
(\mathbb{Z}[T],\mathbb{Z})_{{\square}}\to(\mathbb{Z}[T^{\pm 1}],\mathbb{Z}[T^{-1}])_{{\square}}
define open localizations for their categories of modules. By the proof of
[
CS19
, Theorem 8.1]
, the former localization is the complement of the idempotent
(\mathbb{Z}[T],\mathbb{Z})_{{\square}}
-algebra
\mathbb{Z}((T^{-1}))=\mathbb{Z}[[T^{-1}]][T]
, and the last is the complement of the idempotent algebra
\mathbb{Z}[[T]]
, this ends the proof of the proposition.
∎

Definition 2.3.3
.

Let
(A,A^{+})
be an Tate Huber pair, we let
\Spa(A,A^{+})_{{\square}}
denote the categorified locale
(\Spa(A,A^{+}),\Mod((A,A^{+})_{{\square}}))
obtained by Proposition
2.3.2
.

Corollary 2.3.4
.

The functor
(A,A^{+})\mapsto\Spa(A,A^{+})_{{\square}}
extends to a conservative functor from the
1
-category of analytic adic spaces to the
\infty
-category of categorified locales tensored over
\Mod(\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}})
. Moreover, this functor is fully faithful when restricted to the full subcategory of analytic adic spaces over
\mathbb{Q}
.

Proof.

Let
X
be an analytic adic space, and let
U_{\bullet}
be an hypercover of
X
by open affinoid subspaces. By Proposition
2.3.2
, we can construct the simplicial categorified locale
(|U_{\bullet}|,\Mod_{U_{\bullet},{\square}})
, taking geometric realizations we obtain a categorified locale
(|X|,\Mod_{X,{\square}})
, where
|X|
is the underlying space of
X
,
\Mod_{X,{\square}}
is the
\infty
-category of solid quasi-coherent sheaves on
X
, and
f:\mathcal{S}(\Mod_{X,{\square}})\to|X|
is the geometric realization of the map
\mathcal{S}(\Mod_{U_{\bullet},{\square}})\to U_{\bullet}
. It is easy to verify that this construction is independent of the hypercover, so that it gives rise a well defined functor from adic spaces to categorified locales. The conservativity of the functor is clear by Remark
2.1.4
and Lemma
2.1.3
. To prove the last statement about the fully faithful inclusion for adic spaces over
\mathbb{Q}
, one can reduce to Huber pairs
(A,A^{+})
, where by
[
And21
, Proposition 3.34]
and Lemma
2.1.3
it suffices to show that the morphism of categorified locales
\mathcal{S}(\Mod((A,A^{+})_{{\square}}))\to\Spa(A,A^{+})
is surjective, this will be proved independently in more generality in Proposition
2.7.8
(3).
∎

2.4.
Some idempotent algebras

We let
\Mod(\underline{\mathbb{Z}})
denote the
\infty
-derived category of condensed abelian groups. Let
\mathrm{Cond}(\AniRing)
be the
\infty
-category of condensed animated rings, the forgetful functor
\mathrm{Cond}(\AniRing)\to\Mod_{\geq 0}(\underline{\mathbb{Z}})
has a left adjoint given by the symmetric group algebra
\mathrm{Sym}^{\bullet}M
. Moreover, for each
n\geq 0
we have a symmetric power functors
\mathrm{Sym}^{n}M
that are computed as the sheafification of
S\mapsto\mathrm{Sym}^{n}(M(S))
for
S\in\Extdis
. Analogously, one has wedge products
\bigwedge^{n}M=\mathrm{Sym}^{n}M[1][-n]
(which are given by the sheafification of
S\mapsto\bigwedge^{n}M(S)
), and divided powers functors
\Gamma^{n}(M)=\mathrm{Sym}^{n}(M[2])[-2n]
.

For a free abelian group
F
, a concrete description of its
n
-th symmetric and divided power functor is given by the (co-)invariants of the symmetric group
\Sigma_{n}
in its
n
-th fold tensor product respectively:

\mathrm{Sym}^{n}F=(F^{\otimes n})_{\Sigma_{n}}\mbox{ and }\Gamma^{n}F=(F^{\otimes n})^{\Sigma_{n}}.

Thus, for
S\in\Extdis
, we have explicit descriptions
\mathrm{Sym}^{n}\mathbb{Z}[S]=\mathbb{Z}[S^{n}]_{\Sigma_{n}}=\mathbb{Z}[S^{n}_{\Sigma_{n}}]
and
\Gamma^{n}\mathbb{Z}[S]=\mathbb{Z}[S^{n}]^{\Sigma_{n}}
, where
S^{n}_{\Sigma_{n}}
is the quotient space of
S^{n}
by the natural action of
\Sigma_{n}
. In particular, the symmetric algebra of
\mathbb{Z}[S]
is described as
\mathrm{Sym}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{Z}[S])=\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
where
\mathbb{N}[S]=\bigcup_{c}\mathbb{N}[S]_{\leq c}
with
\mathbb{N}[S]_{\leq c}=\varprojlim_{i}\mathbb{N}[S_{i}]_{\leq c}
, and
\mathbb{N}[S_{i}]_{\leq c}
being the space of sequences
\sum_{s\in S_{i}}a_{s}s
with
a_{s}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\sum_{s\in S_{i}}a_{s}\leq c
, note that
\mathbb{N}[S]_{=c}=S^{c}_{\Sigma_{c}}
.

We want to describe explicitly the solidification of the symmetric powers, wedge products and divided power functors for the groups
\mathbb{Z}[S]
.

Lemma 2.4.1
.

Let
S
be an extremally disconnected set, we have natural exact sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigwedge^{n}\mathbb{Z}[S]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sym}^{n-1}\mathbb{Z}[S]\otimes\bigwedge^{1}\mathbb{Z}[S]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sym}^{n}\mathbb{Z}[S]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{n}\mathbb{Z}[S]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigwedge^{n-1}\mathbb{Z}[S]\otimes\Gamma^{1}\mathbb{Z}[S]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigwedge^{n}\mathbb{Z}[S]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

Proof.

By definition,
\mathbb{Z}[S]
is the sheafification of the presheaf mapping
T\in\Extdis
to the free abelian group
\mathbb{Z}[S(T)]
. For a finite free
\mathbb{Z}
-module
F
we have obvious exact sequences

(2.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigwedge^{n}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sym}^{n-1}F\otimes\bigwedge^{1}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sym}^{n}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{n}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigwedge^{n-1}F\otimes\Gamma^{1}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigwedge^{n}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

where
\mathrm{Sym}^{i}F\otimes\bigwedge^{j}F\to\mathrm{Sym}^{i+1}F\otimes\bigwedge^{j-1}F
maps
(a_{1}\odot\cdots\odot a_{i})\otimes(b_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge b_{j})\mapsto\sum_{k=1}^{j}(-1)^{k-1}(a_{1}\odot\cdots\odot a_{i}\odot b_{k})\otimes(b_{1}\wedge\cdots\widehat{b_{k}}\wedge\cdots\wedge b_{j})
, and the second sequence is obtained by taking duals to the first one evaluated at
F^{\vee}
. Note that both constructions are natural and covariant for
F
, taking filtered colimits we obtain the same exact sequences for an arbitrary free
\mathbb{Z}
-module. Taking
F=\mathbb{Z}[S(T)]
and sheafifications we get exact sequences as stated in the lemma.
∎

Definition 2.4.2
.

For
\mathcal{A}
an analytic ring and
M\in\Mod_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{A})
, let
\mathrm{Sym}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A}}M
be the left adjoint of the forgetful functor
\mathrm{AniAlg}_{\mathcal{A}}\to\Mod_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{A})
from animated
\mathcal{A}
-algebras towards connective
\mathcal{A}
-modules. Equivalently, we let
\mathrm{Sym}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A}}M=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{Sym}^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}M
and
\mathrm{Sym}^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}M=\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\underline{\mathcal{A}}}\mathrm{Sym}^{n}_{\underline{\mathcal{A}}}M
, where
\mathrm{Sym}^{n}_{\underline{\mathcal{A}}}M
is the symmetric power as condensed
\underline{\mathcal{A}}
-module, see
[
CS20
, Proposition 12.26]
. We denote the wedge and divided power functors by
\bigwedge^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}M=(\mathrm{Sym}^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}M[1])[-n]
and
\Gamma^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}M=(\mathrm{Sym}^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}M[2])[-2n]
. Finally, for
S
an extremally disconnected set we write
\mathcal{A}[\mathbb{N}[S]]:=\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\bullet}\mathcal{A}[S]
.

Lemma 2.4.3
.

Let
S
be a profinite set and let
I
be an index set such that
C(S,\mathbb{Z})\cong\bigoplus_{I}\mathbb{Z}e_{i}
, so that
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]\cong\prod_{I}\mathbb{Z}e_{i}^{\vee}
. The following hold:

(1)

\mathrm{Sym}^{n}_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]=\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S^{n}_{\Sigma_{n}}]\cong\prod_{\underline{\alpha}\in I^{n}_{\Sigma_{n}}}\mathbb{Z}(\odot_{i\in\underline{\alpha}}e_{i}^{\vee})
, where
\odot
is the symmetric tensor product.

(2)

\bigwedge^{n}_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]\cong\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\subset I\\
|J|=n\end{subarray}}\mathbb{Z}(\wedge_{j\in J}e_{j}^{\vee})
, where we have fixed a total order for
I
in the wedge product.

(3)

\Gamma^{n}_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]=\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S^{n}]^{\Sigma_{n}}\cong\prod_{\underline{\alpha}\in I^{n}_{\Sigma_{n}}}\mathbb{Z}((\odot_{i\in\underline{\alpha}}e_{i}))^{\vee}
, where
(-)^{\vee}
is the dual basis.

(4)

The sequences of Lemma
2.4.1
remain exact after solidification.

Proof.

We can assume without loss of generality that
S
is extremally disconnected. The first equality in part (1) follows from the explicit description of the symmetric power functor of the free condensed abelian group generated by
S
, and the fact that
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[T]
is the derived solidification of
\mathbb{Z}[T]
for any profinite set
T
. Then, Lemma
2.4.1
and an inductive argument show that
\bigwedge^{n}_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]
and
\Gamma^{n}_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]
are compact
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}
-modules for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. A compact
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}
-module is reflexive, namely, it is a retract of a finite complex of compact projective modules
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[T]\cong\prod\mathbb{Z}
and
\prod\mathbb{Z}
is reflexive as solid
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}
-module. Therefore, to compute
\bigwedge^{n}_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]
and
\Gamma^{n}_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]
it suffices to compute their dual. But then, by taking duals of the sequences of Lemma
2.4.1
with
F=\mathbb{Z}[S]
one obtains the analogue sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{n}C(S,\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{1}C(S,\mathbb{Z})\otimes\bigwedge^{n-1}C(S,\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigwedge^{n}C(S,\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigwedge^{n}C(S,\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sym}^{n-1}C(S,\mathbb{Z})\otimes\bigwedge^{1}C(S,\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sym}^{n}C(S,\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

By
[
CS19
, Theorem 5.4]
the
\mathbb{Z}
-module
\Cont(S,\mathbb{Z})
is a free abelian group, so that the previous sequences are actually exact. This gives the isomorphism
\Cont(S,\mathbb{Z})\cong\bigoplus_{I}\mathbb{Z}e_{i}
we fixed in the proposition, proving that
\mathrm{Sym}^{n}\Cont(S,\mathbb{Z})\cong\bigoplus_{\underline{\alpha}\in I^{n}_{\Sigma_{n}}}\mathbb{Z}(\odot_{i\in\underline{\alpha}}e_{i})
,
\Gamma^{n}C(S,\mathbb{Z})\cong\bigoplus_{\underline{\alpha}\in I^{n}_{\Sigma_{n}}}\mathbb{Z}(\odot_{i\in\underline{\alpha}}e_{i}^{\vee})^{\vee}
and
\bigwedge^{n}\Cont(S,\mathbb{Z})\cong\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\subset I\\
|J|=n\end{subarray}}\mathbb{Z}(\wedge_{j\in J}e_{j})
. Taking duals one deduces that the solidification of the exact sequences of Lemma
2.4.1
are still exact obtaining (4), and that the other explicit descriptions of (1)-(3) also hold.
∎

Corollary 2.4.4
.

Let
S
be a profinite set, then the trivial
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
-module
\mathbb{Z}
has a long Koszul resolution

(2.2)

\cdots\to\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]\otimes\bigwedge^{2}_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]\to\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]\otimes\bigwedge^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]\to\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]\to\mathbb{Z}\to 0.

Dually, we have a long co-Koszul resolution

(2.3)

0\to\mathbb{Z}\to\Gamma^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]\to\Gamma^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]\otimes\bigwedge^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]\to\Gamma^{\bullet}_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]\otimes\bigwedge^{2}_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]\to\cdots.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
2.4.3
by taking the direct sums of the exact sequences

0\to\bigwedge^{n}_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]\to\cdots\to\mathrm{Sym}^{n-1}_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]\otimes\bigwedge^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]\to\mathrm{Sym}^{n}_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]\to 0

and

0\to\Gamma^{n}_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]\to\cdots\to\bigwedge^{n-1}_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]\otimes\Gamma^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]\to\bigwedge^{n}_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]\to 0.

∎

After the previous preparations we can introduce some large idempotent algebras. We let
(R,R^{+})=(\mathbb{Z}((\pi)),\mathbb{Z}[[\pi]])
and
R_{{\square}}=(R,R^{+})_{{\square}}
.

Definition 2.4.5
.

Let
S
be a profinite set, we define the following objects:

(1)

Let
I^{\bullet}_{S}
be the natural ideal decreasing filtration of
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
. We define
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]_{n}:=\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]/I^{n}_{S}
. For
\mathcal{A}
an analytic ring we let
I^{\bullet}_{S,\mathcal{A}}
and
\mathcal{A}[\mathbb{N}[S]]_{n}
be the base change of
I^{\bullet}_{S}
and
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]_{n}
to
\mathcal{A}
.

(2)

We let
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[[\mathbb{N}[S]]]:=\varprojlim_{n}\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]_{n}
.

(3)

We let
R^{+}_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle=\varprojlim_{n}(R^{+}/\pi^{n})_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
and
R_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle=R^{+}_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle[\frac{1}{\pi}]
.

(4)

We let
R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger}=\varinjlim_{n\to\infty}R_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[\frac{S}{\pi^{n}}]\rangle
.

(5)

More generally, for an analytic ring
\mathcal{A}
over
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}
(resp. over
R^{+}_{{\square}}
or
R_{{\square}}
) we let
\mathcal{A}[[\mathbb{N}[S]]]
,
\mathcal{A}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle
and
\mathcal{A}\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger}
denote the base change of the constructions in (2)-(4) to
\mathcal{A}
.

Warning 2.4.6
.

The ring
\mathcal{A}[[\mathbb{N}[S]]]
is not in general equal to the limit
\varprojlim_{n}\mathcal{A}[\mathbb{N}[S]]_{n}
. This holds for example if
\mathcal{A}=B_{{\square}}
it the analytic ring of a finitely generated algebra over
\mathbb{Z}
, or if
\mathcal{A}=R^{+}=\mathbb{Z}[[\pi]]
is a power series ring.

Lemma 2.4.7
.

The following hold:

(1)

The ring
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[[\mathbb{N}[S]]]
is an idempotent
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
-algebra.

(2)

The ring
R^{+}_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle
is an idempotent
R^{+}_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
-algebra.

(3)

The rings
R_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle
and
R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger}
are idempotent
R_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
-algebras.

Moreover they have a natural structure of co-commutative Hopf algebras.

Proof.

The ring
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
is naturally a co-commutative Hopf algebra since it correpresents the functor on solid animated
\mathbb{Z}
-algebras
A\mapsto A(S)
. Moreover, the co-multiplication map is induced from the map of solid abelian groups

\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]\to\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S\bigsqcup S]]:\;\;s\mapsto s\otimes 1+1\otimes s,

in particular it preserves the
I^{\bullet}_{S}
-filtration. Taking completions we see that
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[[\mathbb{N}[S]]]
has a natural co-commutative Hopf algebra structure. The Hopf algebra structure for the other algebras is constructed by taking the base change from
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}
to
R_{{\square}}
of the algebra
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
, taking
\pi
-adic completions, inverting
\pi
and taking colimits along
R_{{\square}}[\frac{S}{\pi^{n}}]\to R_{{\square}}[\frac{S}{\pi^{n+1}}]
.

Now we prove idempotency. Let
B
denote
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
or its
R^{+}_{{\square}}
or
R_{{\square}}
-base change, and let
C
denote
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[[\mathbb{N}[S]]]
,
R^{(+)}_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle
or
R\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger}
. Let
A
be
\mathbb{Z}
,
R^{+}
or
R
depending on the situation. Then both
B
and
C
are
A
-linear Hopf algebras, and by
[
RJRC23
, Proposition 1.0.6]
, to prove idempotency it suffices to show that
C\otimes_{(B,A)_{{\square}}}A=A
. By Corollary
2.4.4
the map
C\otimes_{(B,A)_{{\square}}}A\to A
is represented by the long Koszul complex

\cdots\to C\otimes_{A_{{\square}}}\bigwedge^{2}_{A_{{\square}}}A_{{\square}}[S]\to C\otimes_{A_{{\square}}}\bigwedge^{1}_{A_{{\square}}}A_{{\square}}[S]\to C\to A\to 0.

In the case of (1) the previous sequence is exact since after taking graded pieces one recovers the long Koszul resolution of
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
. In the situation (2), exactness follows by taking the
\pi
-adic completion of the Koszul resolution of
R^{+}_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]\to R^{+}
. Finally, case (3) follows by inverting
\pi
in (2) and taking colimits.
∎

We address the following technical lemma that will be used recurrently in the next section.

Lemma 2.4.8
.

Let
A_{{\square}}=\mathbb{Z}[T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}]_{{\square}}
be a solid polynomial algebra in
n
-variables, and let
B^{(+)}_{{\square}}=A_{{\square}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}R^{(+)}_{{\square}}
be the associated solid Tate algebra over
R^{(+)}_{{\square}}
. Let
S_{1}
,
S_{2}
and
S_{3}
be profinite sets.

(1)

For any map
S_{3}\to B^{+}_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S_{1}]\rangle
the natural morphism
B^{+}_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S_{3}]]\to B^{+}_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S_{1}]\rangle
extends uniquely to
B^{+}_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S_{3}]\rangle
.

(2)

Consider the
B
-algebra
\mathscr{T}=B_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S_{1}]\rangle\otimes_{B_{{\square}}}B_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S_{2}]\}^{\dagger}
and let
\mathscr{I}_{2}=\ker(B_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S_{2}]\}^{\dagger}\to B)
be the augmentation ideal of the second factor. For any map
S_{3}\to B_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S_{1}]\rangle\otimes_{B_{{\square}}}\mathscr{I}_{2}
the natural morphism of algebras
B_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S_{3}]]\to\mathscr{T}
extends uniquely to
B_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S_{3}]\}^{\dagger}
.

(3)

Let
\mathscr{I}=\ker(A_{{\square}}[[\mathbb{N}[S_{1}]]]\to A)
be the augmentation ideal. Then for all map
S_{3}\to\mathscr{I}
, the natural morphism of algebras
A_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S_{3}]]\to A_{{\square}}[[\mathbb{N}[S_{1}]]]
extends uniquely to
A_{{\square}}[[\mathbb{N}[S_{3}]]]
.

Proof.

(1)

This follows by taking
\pi
-adic completions.

(2)

Let us denote
\mathscr{S}_{i,n}=B^{+}_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[\frac{S_{i}}{\pi^{n}}]\rangle
and let
\mathscr{I}_{i,n}
be the augmentation ideal of
\mathscr{S}_{i,n}
. By construction of the algebras we can find
n
big enough such that the image of
S_{3}
in
\mathscr{T}
lands in
\mathscr{S}_{1,0}\otimes_{B^{+}_{{\square}}}\mathscr{I}_{2,n}
. This shows that for all
m\geq 0
the image of
S_{3}
in
\mathscr{S}_{1,0}\otimes_{B^{+}_{{\square}}}\mathscr{S}_{2,n+m}
lands in
\pi^{m}\mathscr{S}_{1,0}\otimes_{B^{+}_{{\square}}}\mathscr{S}_{2,n+m}
. Thus, dividing
S_{3}
by
\pi^{m}
, by part (1) we get an arrow
\mathscr{S}_{3,m}\to\mathscr{S}_{1,0}\otimes_{B^{+}_{{\square}}}\mathscr{S}_{2,n+m}
. One gets (2) by inverting
\pi
and taking colimits as
m\to\infty
.

(3)

This follows from the fact that the filtration
