I^{\bullet}_{S_{1}}
of
A_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S_{1}]]
is multiplicative.

∎

Corollary 2.4.9
.

The following hold

(1)

The Hopf algebra
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[[\mathbb{N}[S]]]
corepresents a module sheaf over the ring sheaf corepresented by
\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
.

(2)

The Hopf algebra
R^{+}_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle
corepresents an algebra sheaf over the ring sheaf corepresented by
R^{+}\langle T\rangle_{{\square}}:=R^{+}_{{\square}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
.

(3)

The Hopf algebra
R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger}
corepresents a module sheaf over the ring sheaf corepresented by
R\langle T\rangle_{{\square}}
.

Proof.

The algebra
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
corepresents an algebra over the sheaf corepresented by
\mathbb{Z}[T]
, namely, for
\mathcal{A}
an analytic ring
\mathcal{A}(S)
is always an
\mathcal{A}(*)
-algebra. Its module action is given by the map

(2.4)

\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]\to\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[T]:\;\;s\mapsto s\otimes t

which satisfies the diagrams of a comodule over a co-ring. In particular,
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]
lands in the ideal generated by the augmentation ideal of
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
.

All the Hopf algebras on points (1)-(3) are idempotent over
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
or
R^{+}_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
, also
\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
is an idempotent analytic ring over
\mathbb{Z}[T]
. Thus, in order to show that the algebras of (1)-(3) correpresent algebras/modules over
\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
or
R^{+}\langle T\rangle_{{\square}}
, we only need to prove it for the
\pi_{0}
of the correpresented sheaves, and assume without loss of generality that
\mathcal{A}
is static.

Then, after taking base change of (
2.4
) by
\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
or
R^{+}[T]_{{\square}}
,
\pi
-completions and colimits for points (2) and (3), Lemma
2.4.8
implies that the comodule (resp. co-algebra) diagrams of
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
over
\mathbb{Z}[T]
can be extended to the corresponding diagrams in each point (1)-(3).
∎

2.5.
Condensed Nil-radical

To motivate forthcoming constructions let us discuss the concept of nilpotency for condensed rings. Let
A
be a static condensed ring over
\mathbb{Z}
, since
A
is a sheaf on rings, the most natural definition of the nil-radical of
A
consists on the ideal
I
whose values at
S
are
\mathrm{nil}(A(S))
. Equivalently, we could define

\mathrm{nil}(A)(S)=\varinjlim_{n}\mathrm{Hom}_{\CondRing_{\mathbb{Z}}}(\mathbb{Z}[T]/T^{n},\mathrm{Cont}(S,A)).

This definition only asks for a function
f:S\to A
to be uniformly point-wise nilpotent, i.e that there is
n\geq 0
such that
f(s)^{n}=0
for all
s\in S
. However, we could also ask for a more uniform nilpotent condition, namely, that there is some
n>0
such that for any familly of elements
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\in S
, the product
f(s_{1})\cdots f(s_{n})
vanishes. When
S
is just a point
*
, there is no difference between these two conditions. When
S=\{*,*\}
is two points, there is a difference on the
n
-nilpotent elements, namely, one is correpresented by the ring
\mathbb{Z}[X,Y]/(X^{n},Y^{n})
and the other by
\mathbb{Z}[X,Y]/(X,Y)^{n}
, yet both cofiltered systems are cofinal. For a general profinite set both possible definitions of nilpotent elements differ:

Example 2.5.1
.

Let
S
be a profinite set and let
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
be the symmetric algebra of
\mathbb{Z}[S]
. Let
n\geq 1
, consider the map
\mathbb{Z}[S]\xrightarrow{\mathbb{Z}[\Delta]}\mathbb{Z}[S^{n}_{\Sigma_{n}}]\subset\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
with
\Delta:S\to S^{n}_{\Sigma_{n}}
given by the diagonal map, and let
F:\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]\to\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
be the induced map of algebras. Then, the static ring that co-represents elements
f\in A(S)
with
f^{n}=0
is the algebra

\mathcal{R}=\pi_{0}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]\otimes_{F,\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]}\mathbb{Z}).

In particular, the
k
-th graded piece of
\mathcal{R}
for
k\geq n
is non-zero and equal to the cokernel of
\mathbb{Z}[S]\otimes\mathbb{Z}[S^{k-n}_{\Sigma_{k-n}}]\xrightarrow{\Delta}\mathbb{Z}[S^{k}_{\Sigma_{k}}]
. On the other hand, the quotient

\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]_{n}:=\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]/I^{n}_{S}

is the static ring that represents elements
f\in A(S)
with the property that the
n
-th fold map
f^{\otimes n}:S^{n}_{\Sigma_{n}}\to A(S^{n}_{\Sigma_{n}})
is zero. In other words, it correpresents the maps
f:S\to A
such that
f(s_{1})\cdots f(s_{n})=0
for any sequence
s_{i}\in S
.

The previous discussion motivates the following definition

Definition 2.5.2
.

Let
A
be a static condensed ring, we define the following presheaf on
\Extdis
:

\Nil^{\prime}_{n}(A)(S):=\mathrm{Hom}_{\CondRing_{\mathbb{Z}}}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]_{n},A).

We let
\Nil^{\prime}(A)
denote the ind-presheaf
(\Nil^{\prime}_{n}(A))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
on
\Extdis
. For an animated condensed ring
A
we define
\Nil^{\prime}_{n}(A)
(resp.
\Nil^{\prime}(A)
) to be the full condensed sub-anima of
A
(resp. the constant ind-system of sub-anima of
A
) whose connected components are
\Nil^{\prime}_{n}(\pi_{0}(A))
(resp.
\Nil^{\prime}(\pi_{0}(A))
).

An apparent disadvantage of the above definition is that the objects
\Nil^{\prime}_{n}(A)(S)
are not sheaves, the reason being that for
S
and
S^{\prime}
extremally disconnected sets, the natural map

\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]_{n}\otimes\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S^{\prime}]]_{n}\to\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S\bigsqcup S^{\prime}]]_{n}

is not an equivalence. However, in analogy to the inclusions
(X,Y)^{2n}\subset(X^{n},Y^{n})\subset(X,Y)^{n}
for two elements
X
and
Y
in a ring
R
, we have a factorization

\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S\bigsqcup S^{\prime}]]_{2n}\to\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]_{n}\otimes\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S^{\prime}]]_{n}\to\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S\bigsqcup S^{\prime}]]_{n},

proving that the ind-system
\Nil^{\prime}(A)
is actually a sheaf. Furthermore, the formation of
\Nil^{\prime}(A)
is compatible with analytic ring structures as follows:

Lemma 2.5.3
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an analytic ring, then
\varinjlim_{n}\Nil^{\prime}_{n}(\underline{\mathcal{A}})
is a complete
\mathcal{A}
-module.

Proof.

By
[
CS20
, Proposition 12.4]
it suffices to show that
\pi_{0}(\varinjlim_{n}\Nil^{\prime}_{n}(\underline{\mathcal{A}}))
is
\mathcal{A}
-complete, so we can assume that
\mathcal{A}
is static. Let us first see that
\varinjlim_{n}\Nil^{\prime}_{n}(\mathcal{A})
has a natural structure of
\underline{\mathcal{A}}
-module. For this, it suffices to see that the pro-condensed sheaf
S\mapsto(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a condensed comodule for the condensed co-ring
S\mapsto\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
. This follows from the fact that for profinite sets
S
,
S^{\prime}
and
S^{\prime\prime}
, and any map
S\to\mathbb{Z}[S^{\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime}]
, we have a factorization

\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]_{n}\to\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S^{\prime}]]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S^{\prime\prime}]]_{n},

as we have a natural map
(S^{\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime})^{n}_{\Sigma_{n}}\to S^{{}^{\prime},n}_{\Sigma_{n}}\times S^{n}_{\Sigma_{n}}
. Moreover, the fact that
\varinjlim_{n}\Nil^{\prime}_{n}(\mathcal{A})
is
\mathcal{A}
-complete follows by the same argument: any map
S^{\prime}\to\mathcal{A}[S^{\prime}\times S^{\prime\prime}]
induces a morphism

\mathcal{A}[\mathbb{N}[S]]_{n}\to\mathcal{A}[\mathbb{N}[S^{\prime}]]\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{A}[\mathbb{N}[S^{\prime\prime}]]_{n}.

Therefore, any map
f:S\to\Nil^{\prime}_{n}(\underline{\mathcal{A}})
can be lifted to a map
S\to\mathcal{A}[S^{\prime}]
for some extremally disconnected
S
, so that we have a factorization
S\to\mathcal{A}[\mathbb{N}[S^{\prime}]]_{n}\to\mathcal{A}
, then we can extend
f
to

\mathcal{A}[\mathbb{N}[S]]_{n}\to\mathcal{A}[\mathbb{N}[S^{\prime}]]_{n}\to\mathcal{A},

proving that
\varinjlim_{n}\Nil^{\prime}_{n}(\mathcal{A})
is the image of maps
\bigoplus_{i}\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}[S])\to\underline{\mathcal{A}}
, so
\mathcal{A}
-complete.
∎

Definition 2.5.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an analytic ring, the
condensed nil-radical
of
\mathcal{A}
is the
\mathcal{A}
-analytic ideal

\Nil(\mathcal{A})=\varinjlim_{n}\Nil_{n}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A}).

Our next task is to show that the condensed nil-radical is corepresented by an explicit pro-system of analytic rings, this requires a slight modification of
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]_{n}
.

Definition 2.5.5
.

For
S\in\Extdis
and
n\geq 0
we let
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]^{\mathbb{L}}_{n}:=\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S^{n}_{\Sigma_{n}}]]}\mathbb{Z}
.

Lemma 2.5.6
.

Let
S\in\Extdis
, the pro condensed ring
(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]_{n}^{\mathbb{L}})_{n}
has a natural structure of additive Hopf algebra such that the natural map
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]\to(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]_{n}^{\mathbb{L}})_{n}
is a morphism of Hopf algebras.

Proof.

The Hopf algebra structure of
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
is encoded in the cosimplicial ring
(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}S]])_{[k]\in\Delta}=(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]])_{[k]\in\Delta}
obtained by the comultiplication map defined by
s\mapsto s\otimes 1+1\otimes s
. Let us fix
m\geq 0
and consider the truncation
(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[\bigsqcup^{k}_{i=1}S]])_{[k]\in\Delta_{\leq m}}
. For a fix
k
and any
l\geq k
we have inclusions

(2.5)

\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{Z}[S^{ln}_{\Sigma^{ln}}]\subset\mathrm{Sym}^{ln}(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{Z}[S])\subset\mathrm{Sym}^{l}(\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{Z}[S^{n}_{\Sigma^{n}}]).

On the other hand, we have a
(\leq m)
-cosimplicial submodule
(\mathrm{Sym}^{n}(\mathbb{Z}[\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}S]))_{[k]\leq\Delta_{\leq m}}\subset(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}S]])_{[k]\leq\Delta_{\leq m}}
, it induces a morphism of
(\leq m)
-cosimplicial algebras

\left(\mathrm{Sym}^{\bullet}(\mathrm{Sym}^{n}(\mathbb{Z}[\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}S]))\right)_{[k]\in\Delta_{\leq m}}\to\left(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}S]]\right)_{[k]\leq\Delta_{m}},

taking the push-out from the left term towards the constant
(\leq m)
-cosimplicial ring
(\mathbb{Z})_{[k]\in\Delta_{\leq m}}
one gets a
(\leq m)
-cosimplicial ring

\left(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}S]]^{\mathbb{L}}_{n}\right)_{[k]\in\Delta_{\leq m}}.

Taking the pro-ring as
n\to\infty
, by (
2.5
) one gets a
(\leq m)
-cosimplicial pro-ring

\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{k}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]^{\mathbb{L}}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}})\right)_{[k]\in\Delta_{\leq m}}.

Taking colimits as
m\to\infty
, we get a cosimplicial pro-ring

(2.6)

\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{k}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]^{\mathbb{L}}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}})\right)_{[k]\in\Delta}.

By
[
Lur09
, Proposition 6.1.2.6 (4)]
, the cosimplicial ring (
2.6
) pro-correpresents a group object in
\AnRing_{\mathbb{Z}}
, proving that it is in fact a Hopf algebra. By construction, it is clear that
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]\to(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]^{\mathbb{L}}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a morphism of Hopf algebras, proving the lemma.
∎

Proposition 2.5.7
.

Let
S\in\Extdis
, the functor
\mathcal{A}\mapsto\Nil(\mathcal{A})(S)
is correpresented by the pro-condensed ring
(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]_{n}^{\mathbb{L}})_{n}
.

Proof.

We follow the same argument of
[
GR23
, Proposition 6.3.3]
. Let
\widetilde{\Nil}
be the functor

\widetilde{\Nil}(\mathcal{A})(S):=\varinjlim_{n}\Map_{\AnRing_{\mathbb{Z}}}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]_{n}^{\mathbb{L}},\mathcal{A}).

By Lemma
2.5.6
,
\widetilde{\Nil}(\mathcal{A})(S)
is naturally an animated
\mathbb{Z}
-module and the natural map
f:\widetilde{\Nil}(\mathcal{A})(S)\to\underline{\mathcal{A}}(S)
is a morphism of animated
\mathbb{Z}
-modules. It suffices to show that
f
is a fully faithful sub-anima with connected components
\pi_{0}(\Nil(\mathcal{A})(S))
. The claim about
\pi_{0}
is clear since
\pi_{0}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]_{n}^{\mathbb{L}})=\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]_{n}
. It is left to show that for all
i\geq 1
the map
\pi_{i}(\widetilde{\Nil}(\mathcal{A})(S))\to\pi_{i}(\underline{\mathcal{A}}(S))
is an isomorphism. Let us denote
\widetilde{\Nil}_{n}(\mathcal{A})(S):=\Map_{\AnRing_{\mathbb{Z}}}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]^{\mathbb{L}}_{n},\mathcal{A})

By definition, we have a cartesian square of anima

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\Nil}_{n}(\mathcal{A})(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}(S^{n}_{\Sigma^{n}})}

where the map
\mathcal{A}(S)\to\mathcal{A}(S^{n}_{\Sigma^{n}})
is induced by the map
S^{n}_{\Sigma^{n}}\to\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
. Thus, by taking
0
as marked point, we have a long exact sequence of homotopy groups for
i\geq 1

\pi_{i+1}(\mathcal{A}(S^{n}_{\Sigma^{n}}))\to\pi_{i}(\widetilde{\Nil}_{n}(\mathcal{A})(S))\to\pi_{i}(\mathcal{A}(S))\to\pi_{i}(\mathcal{A}(S^{n}_{\Sigma^{n}})).

For
m\geq 1
we have a commutative triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}(S^{n}_{\Sigma^{n}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}(S^{nm}_{\Sigma^{nm}})}

induced by the map
S^{nm}_{\Sigma^{nm}}\to\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S^{n}_{\Sigma^{n}}]]\to\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
. This gives rise a natural map
\widetilde{\Nil}_{n}(\mathcal{A})(S)\to\widetilde{\Nil}_{nm}(\mathcal{A})(S)
defining a map of fiber sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\Nil}_{n}(\mathcal{A})(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}(S^{n}_{\Sigma^{n}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\Nil}_{nm}(\mathcal{A})(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}(S^{nm}_{\Sigma^{nm}}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}

This induces a morphism of long exact sequence of homotopy groups

(2.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i+1}(\mathcal{A}(S^{n}_{\Sigma^{n}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}(\widetilde{\Nil}_{n}(\mathcal{A})(S))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}(\mathcal{A}(S))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}(\mathcal{A}(S^{n}_{\Sigma^{n}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i+1}(\mathcal{A}(S^{nm}_{\Sigma^{nm}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}(\widetilde{\Nil}_{nm}(\mathcal{A})(S))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}(\mathcal{A}(S))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}(\mathcal{A}(S^{nm}_{\Sigma^{nm}})).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}

We claim that the map
\pi_{i}(\mathcal{A}(S^{n}_{\Sigma^{n}}))\to\pi_{i}(\mathcal{A}(S^{nm}_{\Sigma^{nm}}))
is zero for any
m\geq 2
. Indeed, it factors through the map

\mathcal{A}(S^{n}_{\Sigma^{n}})\xrightarrow{\Delta}\bigoplus^{m}_{k=1}\mathcal{A}(S^{n}_{\Sigma^{n}})\to\bigotimes_{k=1}^{m}\mathcal{A}(S^{n}_{\Sigma^{n}})\to\mathcal{A}(S^{nm}_{\Sigma^{nm}}),

and the induced arrow

\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}\pi_{i}(\mathcal{A}(S^{n}_{\Sigma^{n}}))\to\pi_{i}(\bigotimes_{k=1}^{m}\mathcal{A}(S^{n}_{\Sigma^{n}}))

is zero for
i\geq 1
. Taking colimits as
m\to\infty
in (
2.7
) one finds that

\pi_{i}(\widetilde{\Nil}(\mathcal{A})(S))\xrightarrow{\sim}\pi_{i}(\mathcal{A}(S))

is an isomorphism, proving what we wanted.
∎

With the previous proposition proven, we can define a stronger notion of nilpotent ideal.

Definition 2.5.8
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be a morphism of analytic rings surjective on
\pi_{0}
with
\mathcal{B}
static and endowed with the induced analytic structure, let
I=[\underline{\mathcal{A}}\to\underline{\mathcal{B}}]
. We say that
I
is
n
-uniformly nilpotent
if for any map
S\to I
with
S
an extremally disconnected set, there is an extension

\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]^{\mathbb{L}}_{n}\to\mathcal{A}.

We say that
I
is
uniformly nilpotent
if it is
n
-uniformly nilpotent for some
n\geq 1
. Finally, we say that
I
is
locally uniformly nilpotent
if for any map
f:S\to I
there exists
n
such that
f
extends to
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{N}[S]]_{n}^{\mathbb{L}}\to\mathcal{A}
.

Remark 2.5.9
.

Note that any uniformly nilpotent ideal of an analytic ring is also a nilpotent ideal as condensed ring. On the other hand, by definition,
\Nil(\mathcal{A})
is a locally nilpotent ideal of
\mathcal{A}
.

We finish this section by proving the invariance of solid structure under locally nilpotent ideals.

Proposition 2.5.10
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a solid affinoid ring and let
I\to\mathcal{A}
be a locally uniformly nilpotent ideal. Let
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{A}/I
. Then a map
\mathbb{Z}[T]\to\mathcal{A}
extends to
\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
if and only if the composite
\mathbb{Z}[T]\to\mathcal{B}
does so.

Proof.

The map
\mathbb{Z}[T]\to\mathcal{A}
extends to
\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
if and only if
\mathbb{Z}((T^{-1}))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[T]}\mathcal{A}=0
. By hypothesis we know that
\mathbb{Z}((T^{-1}))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[T]}\mathcal{B}=0
, this implies that

(\mathbb{Z}((T^{-1}))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[T]}I)\otimes_{\underline{\mathcal{A}}}\mathcal{A}=(\mathbb{Z}((T^{-1}))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[T]}\mathcal{A})[*].

Therefore,
(\mathbb{Z}((T^{-1}))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[T]}\mathcal{A})[*]
is a locally uniformly nilpotent ideal when considered as ideal over itself, in particular the unit is nilpotent and so the ring must be zero proving what we wanted.
∎

2.6.
Bounded affinoid rings and
\dagger
-nil-radicals

Let
(R,R^{+})=(\mathbb{Z}((\pi)),\mathbb{Z}[[\pi]])
and
R_{{\square}}=(R,R^{+})_{{\square}}
, we denote by
R^{(+)}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\rangle_{{\square}}:=R^{(+)}_{{\square}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}[T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}]_{{\square}}
the solid Tate algebra over
R^{(+)}
in
n
-variables. In the previous section we constructed a nilpotent radical for arbitrary analytic rings. The first motivation to introduce the category of bounded affinoid rings is the construction of a new nil-radical that will play a fundamental role in the definition of the analytic de Rham stack. This new nil-radical will measure elements
a\in A
that are “topologically zero”, namely, elements such that
|a|\leq|\pi^{n}|
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The second motivation to define the bounded affinoid rings is to construct a category of rings that behaves as Tate affinoid algebras in classical rigid geometry, namely, algebras
A
admitting some pseudo-uniformizer
\pi
and some subring of “power bounded functions”
A^{0}
with
A=A^{0}[\frac{1}{\pi}]
, such that any
a\in A^{0}
satisfies the norm inequality
|a|\leq 1
with respect to
\pi
in a suitable sense.

To make this idea precise we need some further definitions.

Definition 2.6.1
.

(1)

Let
\mathcal{A}\in\AnRing_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}
be an analytic ring over
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}
. The subring of
+
-bounded
or
solid elements
is the discrete animated ring given by the mapping space

\mathcal{A}^{+}=\Map_{\AnRing_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}}(\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}},\mathcal{A}).

(2)

Let
A\in\AniAlg_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}
be an animated algebra over
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}
. We define the subgroup of
topologically nilpotent elements
to be the condensed animated abelian group mapping an extremally disconnected set
S
to the anima

A^{00}(S):=\Map_{\AniAlg_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}}(\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[[\mathbb{N}[S]]],A).

(3)

Let
A\in\AniAlg_{R_{{\square}}}
be an animated algebra over
R_{{\square}}
. We define its condensed subring of
power bounded elements
to be the condensed animated ring with values at
S\in\Extdis
given by

A^{0}(S)=\Map_{\AniAlg_{R_{{\square}}}}(R_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle,A).

(4)

Let
A\in\AniAlg_{R_{{\square}}}
, the condensed
R
-subring of
bounded elements
is defined as
A^{b}=A^{0}[\frac{1}{\pi}]
.

(5)

Finally, let
A\in\AniAlg_{R_{{\square}}}
, the
\dagger
-nil-radical ideal
is the condensed
A^{b}
-ideal whose values at
S\in\Extdis
are

\Nil^{\dagger}(A)(S)=\Map_{\AniAlg_{R_{{\square}}}}(R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger},A).

(6)

For
\mathcal{A}\in\AnRing_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}
we let
\mathcal{A}^{00}
,
\mathcal{A}^{0}
,
\mathcal{A}^{b}
and
\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})
be as in (2)-(5) for its underlying ring
\underline{\mathcal{A}}
.

Remark 2.6.2
.

By definition,
\mathcal{A}^{+}\subset\mathcal{A}(*)
is the full animated subring consisting on those connected components
a\in\mathcal{A}(*)
for which the induced map of analytic rings
\mathbb{Z}[a]\to\mathcal{A}
extends to
\mathbb{Z}[a]_{{\square}}\to\mathcal{A}
. Indeed, the co-ring structure of
\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
naturally induces a ring structure on
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}^{+})
, and we endow
\mathcal{A}^{+}
with an animated ring structure thanks to the following cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathcal{A}}(*)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}^{+})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\underline{\mathcal{A}})(*).}

Remark 2.6.3
.

The spaces
A^{00}
,
A^{0}
and
\Nil^{\dagger}(A)
are full condensed sub-anima of
A
. Indeed, they are condensed sheaves since for any of the algebras
B(\mathbb{N}[S])
as above we have
B(\mathbb{N}[S\bigsqcup S^{\prime}])=B(\mathbb{N}[S])\otimes B(\mathbb{N}[S^{\prime}])
, and they are full condensed subanima since the algebras
B(\mathbb{N}[S])
are idempotent over the corresponding free algebra generated by
S
. Therefore, for
A^{*}
representing any of the previous full condensed subanima of
A
, we have a cartesian square of anima

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(A^{*})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(A).}

In particular, to endow
A^{*}
with a natural animated abelian group, module or ring structure compatible with the map
A^{*}\to A
, it suffices to do so for
\pi_{0}(A^{*})
. Furthermore, Corollary
2.4.9
implies that these objects are naturally
\mathcal{A}^{+}
-modules. Moreover, as
R^{+}_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle
is a co-ring algebra,
\mathcal{A}^{0}
is also a full condensed animated subring of
\mathcal{A}
.

Remark 2.6.4
.

In Definition
2.6.1
we restricted ourselves to define the
S
-valued points of different condensed objects attached to an animated solid ring
A
, for
S
extremally disconnected. Since
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]
is compact projective for
S
an arbitrary profinite set, the description of
S
-valued points of the objects in Definition
2.6.1
also extends to
S
profinite.

As a first reality check we prove that the objects
A^{0}
,
A^{00}
and
\mathcal{A}^{+}
agree with the classical definitions for complete Tate algebras

Lemma 2.6.5
.

Let
(A,A^{+})
be a complete Tate Huber pair with pseudo-uniformizer
\pi
and set
\mathcal{A}=(A,A^{+})_{{\square}}
. The condensed spaces
A^{0}
and
A^{00}
agree with the classical subspaces of
A
of power bounded and topologically nilpotent elements. In addition,
\mathcal{A}^{+}=A^{+}
.

Proof.

By
[
And21
, Proposition 3.34]
one can recover the underlying discrete ring of
A^{+}
simply as
\mathcal{A}^{+}
. It is left to identity the condensed subobjects
A^{0}
and
A^{00}
for general
A
. By definition, the underlying points of
A^{0}
and
A^{00}
consist on all the elements
a\in A
for which the map
R^{+}[T]\to A
extends to
R^{+}\langle T\rangle
and
R^{+}[[T]]
respectively. Then, by definition
A^{0}(*)
is the subset of power-bounded elements, and
A^{00}(*)
is the set of topologically nilpotent elements. Therefore, to prove the lemma it suffices to show that
A^{0}
and
A^{00}
are open subspaces of
A
, or equivalently, that
A/A^{00}
is discrete. Let
A_{0}\subset A
be a ring of definition of
A
, it will suffices to show that
\pi A_{0}\subset A^{00}
. Let
S
be a profinite set and let
f:S\to\pi A_{0}
be a map of condensed sets. Since
\pi A_{0}
is
\pi
-adically complete, the map
f
extends to a morphism of rings

R^{+}_{{\square}}[[\mathbb{N}[S]]]\to A_{0},

this proves that
\pi A_{0}\subset A^{00}
, obtaining the claim.
∎

In order to prove further properties of the condensed subspaces constructed previously, we need to introduce the category of solid rings that will serve as building blocks for the geometric theory treated in this paper.

Definition 2.6.6
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an analytic ring over
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}
, we say that
\mathcal{A}
is a
solid affinoid ring
if the natural map
(\underline{\mathcal{A}},\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}^{+}))_{{\square}}\to\mathcal{A}
is an equivalence of analytic rings. We let
\AffRing_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\subset\AnRing_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}
denote the full subcategory of solid affinoid rings. Given
\mathcal{A}
a solid affinoid ring we let
\AffRing_{\mathcal{A}}
denote the slice category of solid affinoid
\mathcal{A}
-algebras.

Example 2.6.7
.

(1)

Let
A
be an animated discrete ring, by
[
And21
, Proposition 3.34]
and
[
CS20
, Proposition 12.19]
solid affinoid ring structures on
A
are in bijection with integrally closed subrings
A^{+}\subset\pi_{0}(A)
via the map
A^{+}\mapsto(A,A^{+})_{{\square}}
. If
A^{+}=A
we denote
A_{{\square}}=(A,A)_{{\square}}
.

(2)

An example of an analytic ring over
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}
that is not solid affinoid is the ring of ultra-solid rational numbers
\mathbb{Q}_{{\square}{\square}}
(construction due to Clausen and Scholze). It has by compact projective generators the
\mathbb{Q}
-vector spaces
\prod_{I}\mathbb{Q}
. In terms of locales,
\mathbb{Q}_{{\square}{\square}}
is the open complement of
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}
associated to the idempotent solid algebra
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}=\prod_{p}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, we left the proof of this fact for a future work.

Polynomials algebras are the compact projective generators in the
\infty
-category of discrete commutative animated rings. Similarly, one can explicitly provide a class of compact projective generators for the
\infty
-category of solid affinoid rings.

Proposition 2.6.8
.

The
\infty
-category
\AffRing_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}
is stable under small colimits and finite products in
\AnRing_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}
. Furthermore, it has a basis of compact projective generators given by the analytic rings
\mathbb{Z}[T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}]_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
, where
\{T_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
is a finite set of variables, and
S
is a profinite set. Moreover, these rings are compact projective in
\AnRing_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}
.

Proof.

It is clear that the category
\AffRing_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}
is generated by the rings
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
and
\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
under colimits, namely, the rings
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
are generators of animated solid algebras and for any
\mathcal{A}\in\AffRing_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}
we can write

\mathcal{A}=(\underline{\mathcal{A}},\mathbb{Z})_{{\square}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}^{+})]}\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}^{+})]_{{\square}}.

In particular,
\AffRing_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}
is stable under small colimits in
\AnRing_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}
. Stability under finite products is clear since for
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
solid affinoid rings, one has
\mathcal{A}\prod\mathcal{B}=(\underline{\mathcal{A}}\times\underline{\mathcal{B}},\mathcal{A}^{+}\times\mathcal{B}^{+})_{{\square}}
.

It is left to see that the rings
\mathbb{Z}[\underline{T}]_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
are compact projective in the
\infty
-category of solid affinoid rings. Since the category of compact projective objecs is stable under finite coproducts, it suffices to show that
\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
are compact projective. The ring
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
is clearly compact projective since it corepresents
\mathcal{A}\mapsto\underline{\mathcal{A}}(S)=R\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}(\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S],\underline{\mathcal{A}})
, and
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}[S]
is a compact projective solid abelian group. It is left to show that
\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
is compact projective in
\AnRing_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}
.

Let
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a sifted diagram of analytic
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}
-algebras with colimit
\mathcal{A}
. We want to prove that the natural map

(2.8)

\varinjlim_{i}\Map_{\AnRing_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}}(\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}},\mathcal{A}_{i})\to\Map_{\AnRing_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}}(\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}},\mathcal{A})

is an equivalence. First, note that both sides are full subanima of
\Map_{\AnRing_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}}(R[T],\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{A}(*)
as
\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
is an idempotent
(\mathbb{Z}[T],\mathbb{Z})_{{\square}}
-algebra, so it suffices to show that they have the same connected components. Let
f:\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}\to\mathcal{A}
be a morphism of analytic rings, we want to show that
f
factors through some
\mathcal{A}_{i}
. As
\mathbb{Z}[T]
is compact projective, we can find a lift
f_{i}:\mathbb{Z}[T]\to\mathcal{A}_{i}
to some
i
. The map
f_{i}
extends to
\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
if and only if
\mathbb{Z}((T^{-1}))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathcal{A}_{i}=0
. By hypothesis
\mathbb{Z}((T^{-1}))\otimes_{(\mathbb{Z}[T],\mathbb{Z})_{{\square}}}\mathcal{A}=0
, and we have that

\mathbb{Z}((T^{-1}))\otimes_{(\mathbb{Z}[T],\mathbb{Z})_{{\square}}}\mathcal{A}=\varinjlim_{i}\mathbb{Z}((T^{-1}))\otimes_{(\mathbb{Z}[T],\mathbb{Z})_{{\square}}}\mathcal{A}_{i}.

Then, there is some
i
for which the unit of
\mathbb{Z}((T^{-1}))\otimes_{(\mathbb{Z}[T],\mathbb{Z})_{{\square}}}\mathcal{A}_{i}
vanishes, implying that for any
i\to i^{\prime}
one has
\mathbb{Z}((T^{-1}))\otimes_{(\mathbb{Z}[T],\mathbb{Z})_{{\square}}}\mathcal{A}_{i^{\prime}}=0
, this proves that
f_{i^{\prime}}
extends to
\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}\to\mathcal{A}_{i^{\prime}}
and that (
2.8
) is an equivalence.
∎

We go back to Definition
2.6.1
, our next task is to show that the objects there constructed are complete and have the corresponding algebraic structure.

Proposition 2.6.9
.

The following hold:

(1)

Let
\mathcal{A}\in\AffRing_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}
, then
\mathcal{A}^{00}
is a solid
\mathcal{A}^{+}_{{\square}}
-module.

(2)

Let
\mathcal{A}\in\AffRing_{R^{+}_{{\square}}}
, then
\mathcal{A}^{0}
is an animated
\mathcal{A}^{+}_{{\square}}
-algebra.

(3)

Let
\mathcal{A}\in\AffRing_{R_{{\square}}}
, then
\mathcal{A}^{b}
is a solid
\mathcal{A}^{+}_{{\square}}
-algebras and
\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})
a solid
\mathcal{A}^{+}_{{\square}}
-module. Moreover,
\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})
has a natural structure of
\mathcal{A}^{b}
-module, defining a full subideal
\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})\subset\mathcal{A}^{b}
.

Proof.

By Remarks
2.6.2
and
2.6.3
, to prove that the objects in the proposition are solid
\mathcal{A}^{+}_{{\square}}
-modules and that have the claimed algebraic structures, we can take the
0
-truncation. Therefore, we can assume without loss of generality that
\mathcal{A}
is static. Corollary
2.4.9
shows that any of the objects in (1)-(3) are
\mathcal{A}^{+}
-modules, and that
\mathcal{A}^{0}\subset\underline{\mathcal{A}}
is a subring. In particular,
\mathcal{A}^{+}\to\mathcal{A}^{0}\to\mathcal{A}^{b}
are morphisms of (static) commutative rings. To prove
\mathcal{A}^{+}_{{\square}}
-completeness, it suffices to do it for
\mathcal{A}^{00}
,
\mathcal{A}^{0}
and
\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})
, let
\mathcal{A}^{*}
denote one of these condensed modules. We make the following conventions:

(1)

We let
B
denote
\mathbb{Z}
,
R^{+}
or
R
depending on the situation.

(2)

We take
I\subset\mathcal{A}^{+}
a finite set of variables, set
B_{I}=B\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}[T_{I}]_{{\square}}
, and let
B_{I}(\mathbb{N}[S])
be the algebra
B_{I}[[\mathbb{N}[S]]]
,
B_{I}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle
or
B_{I}\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger}
depending on the situation.

Let
S
be a profinite set and let
S\to\mathcal{A}^{*}\subset\mathcal{A}
be a map of condensed sets, by definition it extends uniquely to a map
B_{I}(\mathbb{N}[S])\to\mathcal{A}
, we claim that
B_{I}[S]\to\mathcal{A}
factors through
\mathcal{A}^{*}
. Suppose this holds, then
\mathcal{A}^{*}
would be the image of maps
\bigoplus_{S}B_{I}[S]\to\underline{\mathcal{A}}
, proving that
\mathcal{A}^{*}
is
B_{I}
-complete, taking colimits along all
I\subset\mathcal{A}^{+}
, one gets that
\mathcal{A}^{*}
is indeed
\mathcal{A}^{+}_{{\square}}
-complete. Let
\bigsqcup_{i}S^{\prime}_{i}\to B_{I}[S]
be a surjection of condensed sets. Since
B_{I}[S]
is in the augmentation ideal of
B_{I}(\mathbb{N}[S])
, Lemma
2.4.8
implies that the map
S^{\prime}_{i}\to B_{I}(\mathbb{N}[S])
extends to
B_{I}(\mathbb{N}[S^{\prime}_{i}])\to B_{I}(\mathbb{N}[S])
. Taking the composition we get maps
B_{I}(\mathbb{N}[S^{\prime}_{i}])\to\mathcal{A}
for all
i\in I
, that must send
S^{\prime}_{i}
to
\mathcal{A}^{*}
by definition. This shows that
B_{I}[S]
is sent to
\mathcal{A}^{*}
proving the claim.

Finally, suppose that
\mathcal{A}
is a solid affinoid ring over
R_{{\square}}
, we want to prove that
\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})
is naturally an
\mathcal{A}^{b}
-module. For this, by looking at the corresponding diagrams, it suffices to prove that for all profinite set
S
and all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the diagonal map
S\to S\times S
induces a morphism of algebras

R\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger}\to R\langle\mathbb{N}[\pi^{n}S]\rangle\otimes_{R_{{\square}}}R\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger},

but this follows by Lemma
2.4.8
.
∎

After the previous preparations we can define the desired category of bounded affinoid rings.

Definition 2.6.10
.

(1)

An animated
R_{{\square}}
-algebra
A
is
bounded
if the natural map
A^{b}\to A
is an equivalence. We let
\AniAlg^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
be the full subcategory of
\AniAlg_{R_{{\square}}}
consisting on bounded animated
R_{{\square}}
-algebras.

(2)

Let
A
be a bounded
R_{{\square}}
-algebra, the cone
A^{\dagger-\red}
of the map
\Nil^{\dagger}(A)\to A
is called the
\dagger
-reduction
of
A
. We say that
A
is
\dagger
-reduced
if
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}^{\dagger-\red}
is an equivalence. We let
\AniAlg^{\dagger-\red}_{R_{{\square}}}\subset\AniAlg_{R_{{\square}}}
be the full subcategory consisting on
\dagger
-reduced animated rings.

(3)

A solid affinoid
R_{{\square}}
-algebra is
bounded
if its underlying condensed ring is bounded. We let
\AffRing^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}\subset\AffRing_{R_{{\square}}}
be the full subcategory of bounded affinoid
R_{{\square}}
-algebras. For
\mathcal{A}\in\AffRing^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
we let
\AffRing^{b}_{\mathcal{A}}
be the slice category of bounded affinoid
\mathcal{A}
-algebras.

(4)

Given
\mathcal{A}
a bounded affinoid ring, we let
\mathcal{A}^{\dagger-\red}:=\underline{\mathcal{A}}^{\dagger-\red}_{\mathcal{A}/}
be its
\dagger
-reduction, we say that
\mathcal{A}
is
\dagger
-reduced if the previous map is an equivalence. We let
\AffRing^{\dagger-\red}_{R_{{\square}}}\subset\AffRing_{R_{{\square}}}^{b}
be the full subcategory of
\dagger
-reduced bounded affinoid rings.

The following notation will be used throughout the rest of the paper.

Definition 2.6.11
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a solid affinoid ring, we let
\mathcal{A}[T]_{{\square}}:=\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
be the
solid polynomial algebra
over
\mathcal{A}
. If
\mathcal{A}
is a bounded
R_{{\square}}
-algebra, we write
\mathcal{A}\langle T\rangle_{{\square}}:=\mathcal{A}[T]_{{\square}}
and call it the
solid Tate algebra
over
\mathcal{A}
.

We end this section by proving some permanence properties of the category of bounded affinoid rings, in particular that the
\dagger
-reduction is an idempotent functor.

Lemma 2.6.12
.

Let
A
be an animated
R_{{\square}}
-algebra.

(1)

A
is bounded if and only if
\pi_{0}(A)
is bounded.

(2)

An animated
R_{{\square}}
-algebra is bounded if and only if there is a surjection
\bigoplus_{i}R_{{\square}}[S_{i}]\to A
of animated
R_{{\square}}
-modules (i.e. surjection on
\pi_{0}
) with
S_{i}
profinite, such that each
R_{{\square}}[S_{i}]\to A
extends to an algebra morphism
R_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[\pi^{n}S_{i}]\rangle\to A
for some
n
depending on
i
.

Proof.

The first statement is clear since
A^{b}
is a full condensed subanima of
A
. For the second statement, the hypothesis is clearly necessary, let us show that it is sufficient. Let
A
be a static algebra satisfying the hypothesis of the lemma and let
S\to A
be a map from a profinite set. By (1) we can assume that
A
is static. We can lift
S
to a finite direct sum
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}R_{{\square}}[S_{i}]
, after rescaling we can even assume that it lands in
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}R^{+}_{{\square}}[S_{i}]
and that each map
R^{+}_{{\square}}[S_{i}]\to A
extends to
R^{+}_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S_{i}]\rangle
. Since

\bigotimes_{i=1}^{k}R^{+}\langle\mathbb{N}[S_{i}]\rangle=R^{+}\langle\mathbb{N}[\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}S_{i}]\rangle,

the natural map

R^{+}_{{\square}}[S]\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}R^{+}_{{\square}}[S_{i}]\to R^{+}_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}S_{i}]\rangle

can be extended to
R^{+}_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle\to R^{+}_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}S_{i}]\rangle
, and the map
S\to A
extends to
R_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle\to A
proving that
A
is bounded.
∎

Lemma 2.6.13
.

Let
A
be a
\pi
-adically complete animated
R^{+}_{{\square}}
-algebra, then
A[\frac{1}{\pi}]
is a bounded subring.

Proof.

Let
S
be profinite and
f:S\to A[\frac{1}{\pi}]
, after rescaling we can assume that
f
factors through a map
f:S\to A
. Then, since
A
is
\pi
-adically complete, we have an extension

R^{+}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle\to A

and so a map
R\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle\to A[\frac{1}{\pi}]
, proving that
A[\frac{1}{\pi}]
is bounded as wanted.
∎

Proposition 2.6.14
.

The following hold

(1)

The category of bounded animated
R_{{\square}}
-algebras
\AniAlg^{b}_{R_{\square}}
is stable under all small colimits in
\AniAlg_{R_{{\square}}}
.

(2)

The category of bounded animated
R_{{\square}}
-algebras admits all limits. More precisely, let
\{A_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be an
I
-diagram in
\AniAlg^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
, then its limit in
\AniAlg^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
is given by the “restricted limit”

\varprojlim^{\prime}_{I}A_{i}:=(\varprojlim_{i\in I}A_{i}^{0})[\frac{1}{\pi}].

(3)

Let
A
be an animated
R_{{\square}}
-solid
R[T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}]
-algebra whose underlying
R_{{\square}}
-algebra is bounded. Then
A\otimes_{(R[T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}],R^{+})_{{\square}}}R\langle T_{1},\ldots T_{n}\rangle_{{\square}}
is bounded.

(4)

Let
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be a morphism of bounded affinoid
R_{{\square}}
-algebras, let
C
be an animated
\mathcal{A}
-algebra whose underlying
R_{{\square}}
-algebra is bounded, then
\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}C
is a bounded algebra.

(5)

More generally, the category of bounded affinoid rings
\AffRing^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
is stable under all colimits and finite products in
\AnRing_{R_{{\square}}}
.

(6)

Let
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be a morphism of bounded affinoid rings and let
\mathcal{C}
be a bounded affinoid
\mathcal{A}
-algebra. Then the natural map
\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{C}
factors through
\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

(1)

Let
A
be a bounded
R_{{\square}}
-algebra,
B
and
C
bounded
A
-algebras and
D=B\otimes_{(A,R^{+})_{{\square}}}C
. The property of being a bounded algebra only depends on
\pi_{0}
, so we can assume that
A
,
B
and
C
are static and take
D=\pi_{0}(B\otimes_{(A,R^{+})_{{\square}}}C)
the non-derived pushout. Let us take surjections
\bigoplus_{i}R_{{\square}}[S_{i}]\to B
and
\bigoplus_{j}R_{{\square}}[S^{\prime}_{j}]\to C
, then
\bigoplus_{i,j}R_{{\square}}[S_{i}\times S_{j}^{\prime}]\to D
is a surjection. By hypothesis the maps
R_{{\square}}[S_{i}]\to B
and
R_{{\square}}[S_{j}^{\prime}]\to C
extend naturally to morphisms of algebras
R_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S_{i}]\rangle\to B
and
R_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S_{i}^{\prime}]\rangle\to C
respectively (after rescaling the maps). This implies that
R_{{\square}}[S_{i}\times S_{j}^{\prime}]\to D
extends to a morphism of algebras
R_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S_{i}\times S_{j}^{\prime}]\rangle\to D
which by Lemma
2.6.12
proves that
D
is bounded. Next we prove stability under sifted colimits. Let
\{A_{i}\}_{i}
be a sifted diagram of bounded animated
R_{{\square}}
-algebras with colimit
A
, let
S\to A
be a map from a profinite set, then
S
lifts to some
S\to A_{i}
and after rescaling it extends to
R_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle\to A_{i}
. Thus,
S\to A
extends to
R_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle\to A
proving that
A
is bounded.

(2)

Let
\{A_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a diagram of bounded animated
R_{{\square}}
-algebras, and let
B\in\AniAlg^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
. We need to show that
\varprojlim_{i}^{\prime}A_{i}
is bounded and that the natural map

\Map_{\AniAlg_{R_{{\square}}}}(B,\varprojlim_{i}^{\prime}A)\to\varprojlim_{i}\Map_{\AniAlg_{R_{{\square}}}}(B,A)

is an equivalence. To see that
\varprojlim_{i}^{\prime}A_{i}
is bounded, note that for any profinite set
S
, a map
f:S\to\varprojlim_{i}A^{0}_{i}
naturally extends to
R^{+}_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle\to\varprojlim_{i}A_{i}^{0}
as so does any projection to
A_{i}
. On the other hand, since
B
is bounded, there is a natural equivalence of mapping spaces

(2.9)

\Map_{\AniAlg_{R_{{\square}}}}(B,A_{i})=\Map_{\AniAlg_{R^{+}_{{\square}}}}(B^{0},A_{i})=\Map_{\AniAlg_{R^{+}_{{\square}}}}(B^{0},A_{i}^{0}),

where the second equivalence follows from the fact that any map of animated
R^{+}
-algebras
B^{0}\to A_{i}
factors through
A_{i}^{0}
, and
A_{i}^{0}
is a full subring of
A_{i}
.
Taking limits along
i
we see that

\varprojlim_{i}\Map_{\AniAlg_{R_{{\square}}}}(B,A_{i})=\Map_{\AniAlg_{R^{+}_{{\square}}}}(B^{0},\varprojlim_{i}A_{i}^{0}).

Note that
\varprojlim_{i}A_{i}^{0}
is a full condensed subanima of
\varprojlim_{i}A_{i}
, namely, for finite limit it is an equivalence, and cofiltered limits are left exact with respect to the natural
t
-structure. Then, to prove that (
2.9
) is an equivalence, it suffices to show that
(\varprojlim^{\prime}_{i}A_{i})^{0}=\varprojlim_{i}A_{i}^{0}
. It is clear that
\varprojlim_{i}A_{i}^{0}\subset(\varprojlim^{\prime}_{i}A_{i})^{0}
, conversely, given
S
profinite and a map
S\to(\varprojlim^{\prime}_{i}A_{i})^{0}
, we have an extension

R^{+}_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle\to(\varprojlim^{\prime}_{i}A_{i})^{0},

and by composing with projections, maps
R^{+}_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle\to A_{i}^{0}
, proving that we have a factorization

R^{+}_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle\to\varprojlim_{i}A_{i}^{0}

as wanted.

(3)

Let
S
be a profinite set and
S\to A
, after rescaling we can assume that it lifts canonically to
R_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle\to A
. Then, by Lemma
2.6.12
it suffices to prove that
R\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{s}\rangle_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle
is a bounded algebra, but this follows from Lemma
2.6.13
.

(4)

This is a direct consequence of parts (1) and (3), namely, we have that

\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}C=\varinjlim_{I\subset\mathcal{B}^{+}}R\langle T_{I}\rangle_{{\square}}\otimes_{(R[T_{I}],R^{+})_{{\square}}}(\underline{\mathcal{B}}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}_{R_{{\square}}/}}C),

where
I
runs over all the finite subsets. The tensor product is a bounded algebra by part (1), the solidification is bounded by part (3) and the colimit is bounded by part (1) again.

(5)

We need to prove that
\AffRing^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
is stable under pushouts and sifted colimits. Let
\mathcal{C}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be a diagram in
\AffRing^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
, we want to prove that
\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{C}
is still in
\Aff_{R_{{\square}}}^{b}
. Since
\mathcal{A}=(\underline{\mathcal{A}},\mathcal{A}^{+})_{{\square}}
(resp. for
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
), by construction of the pushout, we have
\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{C}=(\underline{\mathcal{E}},\mathcal{B}^{+}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}^{+}}\mathcal{C}^{+})_{{\square}}
, where
\mathcal{E}
is the completion of
\underline{\mathcal{B}}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\underline{\mathcal{C}}
with respect to the variables in
\mathcal{B}^{+}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}^{+}}\mathcal{C}^{+}
. By parts (1) and (3) one deduces that
\mathcal{E}
is bounded, so that
\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{C}
is a bounded affinoid ring. Let
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a sifted diagram in
\AffRing^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
with colimit
\mathcal{A}
. By Proposition
2.6.8
one has that
\mathcal{A}^{+}=\varinjlim_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}^{+}
, we find that

\mathcal{A}=(\underline{\mathcal{A}},\mathcal{A}^{+})_{{\square}}=\varinjlim_{i}(\underline{\mathcal{A}_{i}},\mathcal{A}_{i}^{+})_{{\square}}

proving that
\mathcal{A}
is bounded affinoid. Finally, for stability under finite products, note that
(\underline{\mathcal{A}},\mathcal{A}^{+})_{{\square}}\times(\underline{\mathcal{B}},\mathcal{B}^{+})_{{\square}}=(\underline{\mathcal{A}}\times\underline{\mathcal{B}},\mathcal{A}^{+}\times\mathcal{B}^{+})_{{\square}}
.

(6)

Given a map
\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
of bounded affinoid rings, we have an induced map
\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{D})\to\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
on the
\dagger
-nil radical. Then, by Proposition
2.6.9
(3) we can assume without loss of generality that
\mathcal{A}=A
,
\mathcal{B}=B
and
\mathcal{C}=C
have the induced analytic structure from
R_{{\square}}
. Since
\pi_{0}(B\otimes_{A}\Nil^{\dagger}(C))
is a quotient of
\pi_{0}(B\otimes_{R_{{\square}}}\Nil^{\dagger}(C))
we can further assume that
A=R
, and that
B
and
C
are static. It suffices to prove that the image of
\pi_{0}(B\otimes_{R_{{\square}}}\Nil^{\dagger}(C))
in
\pi_{0}(B\otimes_{R_{{\square}}}C)
lands in
\pi_{0}(\Nil^{\dagger}(B\otimes_{R_{{\square}}}C))
. Let
\bigoplus_{i\in I}R_{{\square}}[S_{i}]\twoheadrightarrow B
and
\bigoplus_{j\in J}R_{{\square}}[T_{j}]\twoheadrightarrow\Nil^{\dagger}(C)
be surjections, by hypothesis we have extensions to morphisms of algebras after rescaling
R_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S_{i}]\rangle\to B
and
R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[T_{j}]\}^{\dagger}\to C
. We then have induced maps

R_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S_{i}]\rangle\{\mathbb{N}[T_{j}]\}^{\dagger}\to B\otimes_{R_{{\square}}}C

such that the image of
\bigoplus_{i,j}R_{{\square}}[S_{i}\times T_{j}]
in
\pi_{0}
is the image of
\pi_{0}(B\otimes_{R_{{\square}}}\Nil^{\dagger}(C))
. By Lemma
2.4.8
(2) we can extend the inclusion
R_{{\square}}[S_{i}\times T_{j}]\to R_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S_{i}]\rangle\{\mathbb{N}[T_{j}]\}^{\dagger}
to a morphism of algebras
R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S_{i}\times T_{j}]\}^{\dagger}\to R_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S_{i}]\rangle\{\mathbb{N}[T_{j}]\}^{\dagger}
, proving that the map
R_{{\square}}[S_{i}\times T_{j}]\to B\otimes_{R}C
extends to
R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S_{i}\times T_{j}]\}^{\dagger}
, in particular its image in
\pi_{0}(B\otimes_{R_{{\square}}}C)
lands in
\pi_{0}(\Nil^{\dagger}(B\otimes_{R_{{\square}}}C))
as wanted.

∎

Example 2.6.15
.

In Proposition
2.6.8
we provided a class of compact projective generators for solid affinoid rings, we next describe the power series developements of their
\pi
-completions.

Let
S
be a profinite set with
R_{{\square}}[S]\cong\prod_{i\in I}Rs_{i}
and
d\geq 1
, let us consider the algebra
\mathcal{A}=R^{+}_{{\square}}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\rangle_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle
. First, by Lemma
2.4.3
we have that

R^{+}_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle=\widehat{\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}}}\prod_{\alpha\in I^{n}_{\Sigma_{n}}}R^{+}\mathbf{s}^{\alpha}.

By definition
\mathcal{A}=R^{+}_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}[T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}]_{{\square}}
. This implies that for
S^{\prime}
another profinite set one has

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}[S^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=\widehat{\bigoplus}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\prod_{\alpha\in I^{n}_{\Sigma_{n}}}(R^{+}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\rangle_{{\square}}[S^{\prime}])\mathbf{s}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\bigoplus}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\prod_{\alpha\in I^{n}_{\Sigma_{n}}}\widehat{\bigoplus}_{\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}R^{+}_{{\square}}[S^{\prime}]T^{\beta}\mathbf{s}^{\alpha}.

In particular, we can write an element in
\mathcal{A}[*]
as a power series

f(T,\mathbf{s})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{d}\\
\alpha\in I^{n}_{\Sigma_{n}}\end{subarray}}c_{\alpha,\beta}T^{\beta}\mathbf{s}^{\alpha}

such that for any reduction modulo
\pi^{c}
, there is
N>>0
such that
c_{\alpha,\beta}=0
for
|\alpha|\geq N
, and for each
\alpha
there is
M_{\alpha}>>0
such that
c_{\alpha,\beta}=0
for
|\beta|\geq M_{\alpha}
.

By construction of
\AffRing^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
, the rings
R\langle\underline{T}\rangle_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle
form a class of (non-compact!) generators. Moreover, since
\AffRing^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}\subset\AffRing_{R_{{\square}}}
is a full subcategory, being bounded is a property and not additional data on solid affinoid
R_{{\square}}
-algebras.

In Proposition
2.5.10
we showed that the solid affinoid structure of an analytic ring was independent of the condensed nil-radical. The next result will prove an analogue statement when restricted to the category of bounded affinoid rings.

Proposition 2.6.16
.

(1)

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a bounded affinoid
R_{{\square}}
-algebra and
f:R[T]\to\mathcal{A}
a morphism of analytic
R_{{\square}}
-algebras. Then
f
extends to
R\langle T\rangle_{{\square}}
if and only if the induced map
R[T]\to\mathcal{A}^{\dagger,\red}
extends to
R\langle T\rangle_{{\square}}
.

(2)

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a bounded affinoid
R_{{\square}}
-algebra and
R[T]\to\mathcal{A}
a morphism of analytic
R_{{\square}}
-algebras. The image of
T
is invertible if and only if its image in
\mathcal{A}^{\red-\dagger}
is invertible.

(3)

Let
A
be a bounded
R
-algebra,
S
a profinite set and
S\to A
a map. Then
S
extends to
R_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle
if and only if the composite
S\to A^{\dagger-\red}
does so.

(4)

Let
A
be a bounded
R
-algebra. Then
(A^{\dagger-\red})^{\dagger-\red}=A^{\dagger-\red}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.6.12
(1) and
[
CS20
, Proposition 12.21]
we can assume that
A
and
\mathcal{A}
are static rings.

(1)

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a bounded affinoid ring over
R
. We want to prove that a map
f:R[T]\to\mathcal{A}
of analytic rings extends to
R\langle T\rangle_{{\square}}
if and only if the composition
R[T]\to\mathcal{A}^{\dagger-\red}
does so. This condition is clearly necessary, let us show that it is sufficient. Let
n>0
be such that
f
extends to
B=R\langle\pi^{n}T\rangle_{{\square}}
and let
B_{\infty}=R^{+}\langle\pi^{n}T\rangle[[T^{-1}]][\frac{1}{\pi}]
. Then
f
extends to
R\langle T\rangle_{{\square}}
if and only if
B_{\infty}\otimes_{B_{{\square}}}\mathcal{A}=0
, and this holds if and only if

(B_{\infty}\otimes_{B_{{\square}}}\mathcal{A})[*]=0.

We have a fiber sequence of
\mathcal{A}
-modules

(B_{\infty}\otimes_{B_{{\square}}}\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A}))\otimes_{\underline{\mathcal{A}}}\mathcal{A}\to(B_{\infty}\otimes_{B_{{\square}}}\mathcal{A})[*]\to(B_{\infty}\otimes_{B_{{\square}}}\mathcal{A}^{\dagger-\red})[*].

Suppose that
B_{\infty}\otimes_{B_{{\square}}}\mathcal{A}^{\dagger-\red}=0
, then
(B_{\infty}\otimes_{B_{{\square}}}\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A}))\otimes_{\underline{\mathcal{A}}}\mathcal{A}=(B_{\infty}\otimes_{B_{{\square}}}\mathcal{A})[*]
. The ring
B_{\infty}
is bounded by Lemma
2.6.13
, and by Proposition
2.6.14
(6) the map
B_{\infty}\otimes_{B_{{\square}}}\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})\to B_{\infty}\otimes_{B_{{\square}}}\mathcal{A}
lands in the
\dagger
-nil-radical of the tensor. This implies that the map
R[T]\to B_{\infty}\otimes_{B_{{\square}}}\mathcal{A}
sending
T\mapsto 1
extends to
R\{T\}^{\dagger}
which shows that
1=0
as
R\{T\}^{\dagger}\otimes_{R[T],T\mapsto 1}R=0
, proving what we wanted.

(2)

Let
R[T]\to\mathcal{A}
be a morphism such that the composite
R[T]\to\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}^{\dagger-\red}
sends
T
to an invertible element. By hypothesis there is
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
such that
aa^{\prime}-1\in\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})
, as
T+1
is invertible in
R\{T\}^{\dagger}
, we have that
aa^{\prime}
is invertible which implies that
a
is invertible as we wanted.

(3)

This follows a similar argument as parts (1) and (3). Let
S\to A
be a map such that the composite
f:S\to A^{\dagger-\red}
extends to
C=R_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle
. We want to show that
f
extends to
C
. As
A
is bounded there is
n>>0
such that
f
extends to
B=R_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[\pi^{n}S]\rangle\to A
. Then, by the excision fiber sequences of Remark
2.2.3
,
f
extends to
C
if and only if
D:=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{B}([B\to C],A)=0
, note that this
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}
space is naturally an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebra thanks to the formalism of locale and Definition
2.2.2
, namely, it is of the form
j_{*}j^{*}A
for some open localization
j
of
\Mod(B_{R_{{\square}}/})
. By hypothesis we know that
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{B}([B\to C],A^{\dagger,\red})=0
, so we have

(2.10)

\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{B}([B\to C],\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A}))=D.

Let
S
be a profinite set, we have maps functorial on
R_{{\square}}[S]

(2.11)

\displaystyle\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{R}(R_{{\square}}[S],D)

\displaystyle=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{R}(R_{{\square}}[S],\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{B}([B\to C],\Nil^{\dagger}(A)))

\displaystyle=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{B}([B\to C],\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{R}(R_{{\square}}[S],\Nil^{\dagger}(A)))

\displaystyle=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{B}([B\to C],\underline{\Map}_{\mathrm{AniRing}_{R}}(R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger},A))

\displaystyle\to\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{B}([B\to C],\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{R}(R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger},A))

\displaystyle=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{R}(R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger},\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{B}([B\to C],A))

\displaystyle=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{R}(R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger},D),

where
\underline{\Map}_{\mathrm{AniRing}_{R}}(R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger},A)
is the condensed anima given by

\underline{\Map}_{\mathrm{AniRing}_{R}}(R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger},A)(S^{\prime})=\Map_{\mathrm{AniRing}_{R}}(R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S\times S^{\prime}]\}^{\dagger},A),

that coincides with
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{R}(R_{{\square}}[S],\Nil^{\dagger}(A))
. This implies that any map
R_{{\square}}[S]\to D
can be naturally extended to a map
R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger}\to D
. We claim that such a map induces an algebra homomorphism
R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger}\to\pi_{0}(D)
. Suppose the claim holds, by taking the composite
R[T]\xrightarrow{T=1}R\xrightarrow{\mu}D
where
\mu
is the unit, the algebra morphism
R[T]\to\pi_{0}(D)
extends to an algebra morphism
R\{T\}^{\dagger}\to\pi_{0}(D)
, which implies that
1=0
, this forces
\pi_{0}(D)=0
and
D=0
.

Next, we prove the claim. Let
S
be a profinite set, let
f:R_{{\square}}[S]\to D
be a morphism of solid
R
-modules and let
g:R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger}\to D
be the map constructed above. Let
\pi_{0}(g):R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger}\to\pi_{0}(D)
be the associated map on
\pi_{0}
. We want to prove that
\pi_{0}(g)
is compatible with the multiplication diagrams, for this, consider the map
R_{{\square}}[S]\oplus R_{{\square}}[S]\xrightarrow{f\oplus f}D\oplus D\to D\otimes_{R_{{\square}}}D
. By (
2.11
) we have a natural map
R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}\otimes_{R_{{\square}}}R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}\xrightarrow{g\otimes g}D\otimes_{R_{{\square}}}D
. Furthermore, since we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{{\square}}[S]\oplus R_{{\square}}[S]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\otimes_{R_{{\square}}}D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{{\square}}[S]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes 1\oplus 1\otimes f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{1}+s_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

we have an induced commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger}\otimes_{R_{{\square}}}R\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\otimes_{R_{{\square}}}D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\otimes g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Taking
\pi_{0}
and knowing that
g\otimes g
factors through
\pi_{0}(D)\otimes_{R_{{\square}}}\pi_{0}(D)\to\pi_{0}(D\otimes_{R_{{\square}}}D)
, one deduces that
\pi_{0}(g)
is an algebra homomorphism.

(4)

Finally, let
S
be profinite, let
S\to\Nil^{\dagger}(A^{\dagger-\red})
be a map, and take a lift
S\to A
. Then,
S\to A^{\dagger-\red}
extends to
B=R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger}
by definition, and
S\to\mathcal{A}
extends to
B
by part (4). This implies that the image of
S
in
A
is in its
\dagger
-nil-radical which shows that
S\to A^{\dagger-\red}
is
0
, proving
\Nil^{\dagger}(A^{\dagger-\red})=0
as wanted.

∎

Remark 2.6.17
.

We believe that the map
R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger}\to D
in the proof of part (3) of Proposition
2.6.16
can be naturally promoted to a morphism of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings.

The following lemma explains why classical Tate Huber pairs do not have many
\dagger
-nilpotent elements.

Lemma 2.6.18
.

Let
A
be a solid animated
R_{{\square}}
-algebra and suppose that
\pi_{0}(A^{0})
is
\pi
-adically separated. Then
\Nil^{\dagger}(A)=0
.

Proof.

Let
S
be a profinite set and let
S\to\Nil^{\dagger}(A)
be a map. For all
n\geq 1
we have that
\pi^{-n}S
maps to
A^{0}
, which implies that
S
is divisible by
\pi^{n}
for all
n\geq 0
in
\pi_{0}(A^{0})
. Then, as
\pi_{0}(A^{0})
is
\pi
-adically separated, the map
S\to\pi_{0}(A^{0})
must be zero proving the lemma.
∎

Example 2.6.19
.

Let
A
be a Tate algebra topologically of finite type over a non-archimidean field
K
, and let
\mathrm{nil}(A)
be the classical nil-radical of
A
seen as a closed ideal. Then the reduction
A^{\red}=A/\mathrm{nil}(A)
is a Tate algebra topologically of finite type such that
A^{\red,0}
is
\pi
-adically complete and separated (see
[
Bos14
, Proposition 3.1.10]
). Lemma
2.6.18
shows then that

\Nil^{\dagger}(A)=\mathrm{nil}(A),

i.e. for classical Tate algebras the
\dagger
-nil-radical recovers the usual nil-radical of the ring.

Corollary 2.6.20
.

Let
\mathcal{A}=R\langle X_{1},\ldots,X_{d}\rangle_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle\{\mathbb{N}[S^{\prime}]\}
for profinite sets
S
and
S^{\prime}
. Let
I
be the augmentation ideal of
R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S^{\prime}]\}\to R
. Then
\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})=I\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

The quotient
\mathcal{A}/I\mathcal{A}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{B}=R_{{\square}}\langle X_{1},\ldots,X_{d}\rangle_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle
. It is easy to see that
\mathcal{B}^{0}=R^{+}\langle X_{1},\ldots,X_{d}\rangle_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle
and that it is
\pi
-adically separated. By Lemma
2.6.18
we have
\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{B})=0
. This shows that
\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})\subset I\mathcal{A}
. On the other hand, Lemma
2.4.8
(2) implies that
I\mathcal{A}\subset\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})
which proves the equality.
∎

Finally, the
\dagger
-nil-radical is related with the closure of ideals in classical Huber rings.

Corollary 2.6.21
.

Let
(A,A^{+})
be a classical Tate Huber pair, and let
I\subset A
be a non-necessarily closed ideal in
A
generated by its global sections. Let
\overline{I}
be the closure of
I
in
A
and suppose that
(A/\overline{I})^{0}
is
\pi
-adically separated. Then
\Nil^{\dagger}(A/I)=\overline{I}/I
and
(A/I)^{\dagger-\red}=A/\overline{I}
.

Proof.

Since
(A/\overline{I})^{0}
is
\pi
-adically separated,
A/\overline{I}
is
\dagger
-reduced by Lemma
2.6.18
. This implies that
\Nil^{\dagger}(A/I)\subset\overline{I}/I
. Let
f:S\to\overline{I}
be a map from a profinite set, we want to show that it extends to
R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger}
. Let
A_{0}\subset A
be a ring of definition, we can assume without loss of generality that
S
lands in
A_{0}\cap\overline{I}
. By hypothesis, the subspace
A_{0}\subset I
is dense in
A_{0}\cap\overline{I}
, then for any
n\geq 0
, we have that

\overline{I}+\pi^{n}A_{0}=I+\pi^{n}A_{0}.

Thus,
\overline{I}+\pi^{n}A_{0}/I=I+\pi^{n}A_{0}/I\subset A/I
, and the image of
\pi^{n}A_{0}
in
A/I
contains
\overline{I}/I
for all
n\geq 0
. Then, the composite map
S\to\overline{I}\to\overline{I}/I
has a lift
S\to\pi^{n}A_{0}
, proving that we have a factorization

R_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[\frac{S}{\pi^{n}}]\rangle\to A/I,

taking colimits as
n\to\infty
we get the desired map from
R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[\frac{S}{\pi^{n}}]\}^{\dagger}
, proving that
\underline{I}/I=\Nil^{\dagger}(A/I)
as wanted.
∎

2.7.
Adic spectrum and derived Tate adic spaces

Let
(R,R^{+})=(\mathbb{Z}((\pi)),\mathbb{Z}[[\pi]])
and
R_{{\square}}=(R,R^{+})_{{\square}}
. Let
\AffRing^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
be the
\infty
-category of bounded affinoid rings over
R_{{\square}}
. Similarly as for Tate Huber pairs, given a bounded affinoid ring
\mathcal{A}
we want to construct
the adic spectrum

|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
, as well as a map of locales
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A})\to|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
generalizing the one of Definition
2.3.3
. Instead of trying to define this space using valuations, we construct it using the existing maps of locales for classical Huber rings.

Construction 2.7.1
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\in\AffRing_{R_{{\square}}}^{b}
be a bounded affinoid
R_{{\square}}
-algebra. For any finite set
I\subset A^{0}
we have a morphism of analytic rings (depending on lifts)
(R\langle T_{I}\rangle,R^{+})_{{\square}}\to\mathcal{A}
. By Proposition
2.3.2
we have maps of locales (independent of lifts)

\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{S}((R\langle T_{I}\rangle,R^{+})_{{\square}})\to\Spa(R\langle T_{I}\rangle,R^{+}+R\langle T_{I}\rangle^{00}).

Taking limits we set
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}:=\varprojlim_{I\subset\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}^{0})}\Spa(R\langle T_{I}\rangle,R^{+}+R\langle T_{I}\rangle^{00})
and let

\rho_{\mathcal{A}}:\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}

be the associated map of locales. Note that the formation of both
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\rho_{\mathcal{A}}
are functorial on
\mathcal{A}
and only depend on
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A})
.

The following theorem is the key input to define the adic spectrum of a bounded affinoid ring.

Theorem 2.7.2
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\in\AffRing_{R_{{\square}}}^{b}
. There is a maximal open subspace
U\subset\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
in the constructible topology such that
\rho_{\mathcal{A}}
factors through a map
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}\backslash U\to\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}\backslash U
is surjective.

Definition 2.7.3
.

The
adic spectrum
of
\mathcal{A}
is the space
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|=\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}\backslash U
, with
U
as in Theorem
2.7.2
. We let
\rho_{\mathcal{A}}:\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A})\to|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
be the associated maps of locales, and let
\Spa\mathcal{A}
denote the categorified locale
(|\Spa\mathcal{A}|,\Mod(\mathcal{A}),\rho_{\mathcal{A}})
.

In order to prove Theorem
2.7.2
we need some preparations.

Lemma 2.7.4
.

Let
Z\subset\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
be a constructible closed subspace, then the idempotent algebra
\mathcal{A}(Z):=\rho_{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}(Z)
is a compact module in
\Mod(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that the complement
U
of
Z
is a finite union of rational affinoid localizations
U_{i}
associated to analytic rings
\mathcal{A}_{i}
, and that the forgetful functor
j_{i,*}:\Mod(\mathcal{A}_{i})\to\Mod(\mathcal{A})
commutes with colimits. Indeed, the forgetful functor
j_{*}:\Mod(U)\to\Mod(\mathcal{A})
commutes with colimits, and it is given by

j_{*}j^{*}M=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{A}}([\underline{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathcal{A}(Z)],M),

since
\underline{\mathcal{A}}
is compact one deduces that
\mathcal{A}(Z)
is compact.
∎

Lemma 2.7.5
.

Let
x\in X=\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
, then the constructible neighbourhoods
C
of
x
of the form

X\{f_{i}\leq g:\;\;i=1,\ldots,n\}\cap X\{g<h\},

with
f_{n}=\pi
and
h\in\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A})
, are cofinal in all the constructible neighbourhoods of
x
. We call such a constructible space
C
a rational constructible subspace of
X
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
is a limit of spectra of Tate algebras over
R
, it suffices to prove the statement for
X:=\Spa(R\langle T_{I}\rangle,R^{+}+R\langle T_{I}\rangle^{00})
. It is clear that a basis of neighbourhoods of
x
in
X
for the adic topology are rational localizations of the form
X\{f_{i}\leq g:i=1,\ldots,n\}
with
f_{n}=\pi
. Let
\{x\}_{\gen}
be the space of generalizations of
x
in
X
, then we can write

\{x\}_{\gen}=\bigcap_{x\in U\subset X}U

where
U
runs over all the open neighbourhoods of
x
. Then
\{x\}_{\gen}
is a poset being homeomorphic to the adic spectrum of the residue field
\{x\}_{\gen}=\Spa(k(x),k(x)^{+})
. But now, any rational subspace of
\{x\}_{\gen}
is of the form
\{x\}_{\gen}\{\tilde{h}\leq 1\}
for some
\tilde{h}\in k(x)
, equivalently, any constructible closed subspace of
\{x\}_{\gen}
is of the form
\{x\}_{\gen}\{1<\tilde{h}\}
for
\tilde{h}\in k(x)
. Thus, we can find a neighbourhood
U=\{f_{i}\leq g\}
of
x
, and a lift
h^{\prime}
of
\tilde{h}
in
\mathscr{O}(U)
such that
\{x\}_{\gen}\cap U\{1<h^{\prime}\}=\{x\}_{\gen}\{1<\tilde{h}\}
. After multiplying
h^{\prime}
by a power of
g
, we can find an element
h\in R\langle T_{I}\rangle
and an integer
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that

U\cap X\{g^{n}<h\}=U\{1<h^{\prime}\}.

Thus, after replacing
g
by
g^{n}
and
f_{i}
by
f_{i}^{n}
, we have found an element
h
such that

U\cap X\{g<h\}=U\{1<h^{\prime}\}.

The lemma follows from the previous construction, and the fact that

\{x\}=\bigcap_{Z\subset\{x\}_{\gen}}Z

where
Z
runs over the constructible closed subspaces.
∎

Lemma 2.7.6
.

Let
C
be a rational constructible subspace of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
. Then the category
\Mod(C)
obtained via
\rho^{-1}_{\mathcal{A}}(C)
defines a natural analytic ring structure for
\underline{\mathcal{A}}
. We let
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{C}}
denote the associated analytic ring.

Proof.

We have a natural localization functor
f^{*}:\Mod(\mathcal{A})\to\Mod(C)
with fully faithful right adjoint
f_{*}
. We let
\Mod(C)_{\geq 0}=f_{*}\Mod(C)\cap\Mod(\mathcal{A})_{\geq 0}
. By
[
CS20
, Proposition 12.20]
it suffices to show that
\Mod(C)_{\leq 0}
is the category of complete modules of an analytic animated ring. Take any presentation
C=U\cap Z
where
U=X\{f_{i}\leq g:i=1,\ldots,n\}
with
f_{n}=\pi
, and
Z=X\{g<h\}
. Then
\mathcal{A}_{U}
is an analytic ring structure of
\underline{A}
, and
\mathcal{A}(Z)
is an idempotent algebra in
\Mod(\mathcal{A})
. Then, the category
f_{*}\Mod(C)
is the category of
\mathcal{A}_{U}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{A}(Z)
-modules in
\Mod(\mathcal{A}_{U})
. But we can write

\mathcal{A}_{U}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{A}(Z)=\mathcal{A}_{U}\otimes_{(\mathbb{Z}[T],\mathbb{Z})}\mathbb{Z}((T^{-1})),

where
T
is sent to
h/g
in
\mathcal{A}_{U}
. This last tensor is clearly an analytic animated ring, proving that
\Mod(C)_{\geq 0}
is the the category of animated modules over
\mathcal{A}_{U}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{A}(Z)
.
∎

Definition 2.7.7
.

Let
x\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
.

(1)

The
adic stalk
of
\mathcal{A}
at
x
is the filtered colimit of analytic animated rings

\mathcal{A}(x):=\varinjlim_{x\in U}\mathcal{A}(U),

where
U
runs over all the open rational neighbourhoods of
x
in
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
.

(2)

The
constructible stalk
of
\mathcal{A}
at
x
is the filtered colimit of analytic animated rings

\mathcal{A}(x)_{\cons}:=\varinjlim_{x\in C}\mathcal{A}_{C},

where
C
runs over all rational constructible neighbourhoods of
x
as in Lemma
2.7.5
.

Proof of Theorem
2.7.2
.

We define
U
as the set of
x\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
such that
\mathcal{A}(x)_{\cons}=0
. To deduce the proposition it suffices to show the following claim:

Claim
.

U
us an open subspace in the constructible topology of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
.

Suppose that the claim holds and let us write
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|=\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}\backslash U
. We want to show that
\rho_{\mathcal{A}}
factors by a surjective map onto
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
. We have to prove the following:

(a)

If
Z_{1},Z_{2}
are closed subspaces of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
such that
Z_{1}\cap|\Spa\mathcal{A}|=Z_{2}\cap|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
then
\rho_{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}(Z_{1})=\rho_{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}(Z_{2})
.

(b)

Let
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
be closed subspaces of
\mathcal{T}_{A}
such that
\rho_{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}(Z_{1})=\rho_{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}(Z_{2})
, then
Z_{1}\cap|\Spa\mathcal{A}|=Z_{2}\cap|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
.

We can assume without loss of generality that
Z_{1}\subset Z_{2}
. We first prove part (a). For a closed subspaces
Z\subset\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
we let
\mathcal{A}(Z)=\rho_{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}(Z)
be its associated idempotent algebra in
\Mod(\mathcal{A})
. Let us write
Z_{i}=\bigcap_{j}C_{i,j}
as an intersection of a filtered collection of constructible closed subspaces, we have that

\mathcal{A}(Z)=\varinjlim_{i}\mathcal{A}(C_{i,j}).

By Lemma
2.7.4
each
\mathcal{A}
-module
\mathcal{A}(C_{i,j})
is compact. Then, by replacing
C_{1,j}
with
C_{1,j}\cap C_{2,j}
, we can assume without loss of generality the
Z_{i}
are constructible subspaces. We want to show that the natural map
\mathcal{A}(Z_{2})\to\mathcal{A}(Z_{1})
is an equivalence. By the claim, and the assumption of (a), we know that for all
x\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
the natural arrow

\mathcal{A}(x)_{\cons}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{A}(Z_{2})\to\mathcal{A}(x)_{\cons}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{A}(Z_{1}).

Indeed, if
x\in U
then both terms are zero, and if
x\in|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
this follows from the fact that
Z_{1}\cap|\Spa\mathcal{A}|=Z_{2}\cap|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
and that the
Z_{i}
are constructible. Since the algebras
\mathcal{A}(Z_{1})
are compact
\mathcal{A}
-modules, for each
x\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
there is a rational constructible neighbourhood
C_{x}
such that
\mathcal{A}_{C_{x}}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{A}(Z_{2})\to\mathcal{A}_{C_{x}}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{A}(Z_{1})
is an equivalence. Since
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
is compact for the constructible topology, we can find a finite cover
\{C_{i}\}
by such
C_{x}
. But now the spaces
C_{i}
are locally closed for the adic topology and their union is the whole
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
. Therefore the localization functor

\Mod(\mathcal{A})\to\prod_{i}\Mod(\mathcal{A}_{C_{i}})

is conservative, which proves that
\mathcal{A}(Z_{2})=\mathcal{A}(Z_{1})
as wanted.

Next we prove part (b). Let
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
be closed subspaces of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
such that
\mathcal{A}(Z_{1})=\mathcal{A}(Z_{2})
. We can assume without loss of generality that
Z_{1}\subset Z_{2}
. Moreover, by writing
Z_{i}
as colimits of constructible closed subspaces, by Lemma
2.7.4
we can even assume that
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
are constructible. By hypothesis, we know that for all
x\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
we have
\mathcal{A}(x)_{\cons}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{A}(Z_{2})=\mathcal{A}(x)_{\cons}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{A}(Z_{1})
, but the set of those
x
such that
\mathcal{A}(x)_{\cons}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{A}(Z_{i})\neq 0
is precisely
Z_{i}\cap|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
thanks to the claim. One gets part (b).

Finally, we prove the claim. Let
x\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
be such that
\mathcal{A}(x)_{\cons}=\varinjlim_{x\in C}\mathcal{A}_{C}=0
, where
C
runs over all the rational constructible neighbourhoods of
x
in
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
. Since

\mathcal{A}(x)_{\cons}[*]=\varinjlim_{x\in C}\mathcal{A}_{C}[*],

there is some
C
such that
\mathcal{A}_{C}[*]=0
, so
C\subset U
, proving that
U
is open in the constructible topology as wanted.
∎

Our next task is to prove that the adic spectrum of a bounded affinoid ring enjoys the same properties of adic spectra of Tate Huber rings. More precisely, we shall prove the following:

Proposition 2.7.8
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be a morphism of bounded affinoid rings.

(1)

|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
is a spectral space and has a basis of qcqs open subspaces given by pullbacks of rational localizations of the adic spaces
\Spa(R\langle T_{I}\rangle,R^{+})
for some finite set
I\subset\mathcal{A}^{0}
.

(2)

The morphism
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
induces a spectral map
|\Spa\mathcal{B}|\to|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
. Moreover, the pullback of an open rational subspace is a rational subspace.

(3)

Let
\mathcal{A}=(A,A^{+})_{{\square}}
be the analytic ring associated to a Tate Huber pair, then the natural map

|\Spa\mathcal{A}|\to|\Spa(A,A^{+})|

is a homeomorphism.

Proof.

(1)

By Theorem
2.7.2
we know that the space
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
is pro-constructible in
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
, so an spectral space. Since
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
has a basis given by rational localizations, the same holds for
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
.

(2)

Let
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be a morphism in
\AffRing^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
. We have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|\Spa\mathcal{B}|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|\Spa\mathcal{A}|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}},}

namely, the fiber of
x\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
in
|\Spa\mathcal{B}|
is given by analytic ring
\mathcal{A}(x)_{\cons}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{B}
, and this vanishes if
\mathcal{A}(x)_{\cons}=0
. The right vertical arrow is spectral and the horizontal arrows are pro-constructible immersions, this implies that the left vertical arrow is spectral. It is clear that the inverse image of a rational localization is again a rational localization.

(3)

Let
\mathcal{A}=(A,A^{+})_{{\square}}
be the analytic ring attached to an Tate Huber pair. Let us write
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}=\varprojlim_{I}\Spa(R\langle T_{I}\rangle,R^{+}+R\langle T_{I}\rangle^{00})
, it is easy to see that
\Spa(A,A^{+})\to\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
is a pro-constructible immersion. Since the map
\rho_{\mathcal{A}}:\mathcal{S}((A,A^{+})_{{\square}})\to|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
is surjective, we have immersions
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|\to\Spa(A,A^{+})\to\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{A}}
. We are left to show that the map of locales
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A})\to\Spa(A,A^{+})
is surjective. Let
x\in\Spa(A,A^{+})
, we have a map of affinoid rings
(A,A^{+})\to(\kappa(x),\kappa(x)^{+})
and an induced map of topological spaces
|\Spa((\kappa(x),\kappa(x)^{+})_{{\square}})|\to\Spa(\kappa(x),\kappa(x)^{+})
, thus one can reduce to the case of an affinoid field
\Spa(K,K^{+})
. Then the open subsets of
\Spa(K,K^{+})
form a totally ordered set, and the connected constructible subspaces of
\Spa(K,K^{+})
containing the generic point are in bijection with open integrally closed subrings
K^{+}\subset\tilde{K}^{+}\subset\mathcal{O}_{K}
, with
\mathcal{O}_{K}
the valuation ring of
K
. On the other hand, the functor
(A,A^{+})\mapsto(A,A^{+})_{{\square}}
is a fully faithful embedding of Huber pairs in analytic rings by
[
And21
, Proposition 3.34]
. This shows that
|\Spa(K,K^{+})_{{\square}}|\to\Spa(K,K^{+})
must be a bijection which finishes the proof.

∎

Remark 2.7.9
.

By Lemma
2.1.3
and Theorem
2.7.2
, the functor
\AffRing^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}\to\CatLoc_{\Spa R_{{\square}}}
sending
\mathcal{A}
to
\Spa\mathcal{A}
is fully faithful when restricted to bounded affinoid
R_{{\square}}
-algebras over
\mathbb{Q}
. In particular, after specializing to
R\to\mathbb{Q}_{p}
for any prime number
p
, we have a fully faithful embedding
\AffRing^{b}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p},{\square}}\to\CatLoc_{\Spa\mathbb{Q}_{p,{\square}}}
from bounded affinoid
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-algebras to categorified locales over
\Spa\mathbb{Q}_{p,{\square}}
.

Thanks to the
\dagger
-nilradical we can define residue fields for both the analytic and constructible topologies of
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
.

Definition 2.7.10
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\in\AffRing^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
, and let
x\in|\Spa\mathcal{A}|

(1)

The
residue field
of
\mathcal{A}
at
x
is defined as the
\dagger
-reduced quotient
\kappa(x):=\mathcal{A}(x)^{\dagger-\red}
.

(2)

The
constructible residue field
of
\mathcal{A}
at
x
is the
\dagger
-reduced quotient
\kappa(x)_{\cons}=\mathcal{A}(x)_{\cons}^{\dagger-\red}
.

Next, we prove that the underlying rings of the previous residue fields are honest fields. We need the following lemma.

Lemma 2.7.11
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a bounded affinoid ring.

(1)

The following are equivalent

(a)

The open subsets of
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
form a totally ordered set.

(b)

There is a unique closed point in
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
.

(c)

For any
f,g\in\mathcal{A}\backslash\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})
either
\{|f|\leq|g|\neq 0\}=|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
or
\{|g|\leq|f|\neq 0\}=|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
.

Moreover, adic stalks of bounded affinoid rings satisfy these equivalent properties.

(2)

The following are equivalent

(a)

|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
is a point.

(b)

|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
has a unique closed point and for any
f\in\mathcal{A}
we have
f\in\mathcal{A}^{+}
or
f
is invertible and
f^{-1}\in\mathcal{A}^{00}
.

Moreover, constructible stalks of bounded affinoid rings satisfy these equivalent properties.

Proof.

(1)

Suppose that the open subsets of
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
form a total order. By taking complements, the closed subspaces also form a total order. Let
\mathscr{I}
be the total ordered family of non-empty closed subspaces. Then, since
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
is constructible, by Zorn’s lemma one has that
Z=\bigcap_{C\in\mathscr{I}}C
is the minimal non-empty closed subspace of
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
. The space
Z
is pro-constructible, so it is spectral. Suppose that
Z
has more than two points, as it is a
T_{0}
-topological space, there is a non-empty properly contained closed subspace in
Z
which is a contradiction with the fact that it is the minimal closed subspace of
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
. Thus,
Z
is a point showing that (a) implies (b).

Suppose that
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
has a unique closed point
x
. As
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
is spectral, the unique open subset of
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
containing
x
is
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
. Let
f\in\mathcal{A}
, if for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
the open set
\{|f|\leq|\pi^{n}|\}
contains
x
, then the map
R[T]\to\mathcal{A}
defined by
f
extends to
R\{T\}^{\dagger}\to\mathcal{A}
proving that
f\in\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})
. Thus, for
f\in\mathcal{A}\backslash\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})
there is some
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\{|f|\leq|\pi^{n}|\}
does not contain
x
, which implies that
\{|\pi^{n}|\leq|f|\}
does contain
x
and therefore that
\{|\pi^{n}|\leq|f|\}=|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
. In particular such an
f
must be invertible. Now let
f,g\in\mathcal{A}\backslash\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})
, then the open sets
\{|f/g|\leq 1\}
and
\{|g/f|\leq 1\}
form an open cover of
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
, in particular
x
belongs to one of them, which shows that either
\{|f|\leq|g|\neq 0\}=|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
or
\{|g|\leq|f|\neq 0\}=|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
as wanted.

Now suppose that (c) holds. It suffices to show that the poset of open rational subspaces forms a total order. Let
f\in\mathcal{A}\backslash\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})
. Then there is some
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\{|f|\leq|\pi^{n}|\}\neq|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
, by hypothesis this implies that
\{|\pi^{n}|\leq|f|\neq 0\}=|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
proving that
f
is invertible. We define the following partial order in
\mathcal{A}\backslash\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})
: we say that
|f|\leq|g|
if
\{|f|\leq|g|\neq 0\}=|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
. By hypothesis, given two elements
f,g\in\mathcal{A}\backslash\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})
we have either
|f|\leq|g|
or
|g|\leq|f|
. Let
U\subset\Spa\mathcal{A}
be a rational set of the form
\{|f_{i}|\leq|g|\neq 0:\;i=1,\ldots,d\}
with
f_{d}=\pi^{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. If
U
is non-empty then
g\notin\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})
, in particular it is invertible and by taking
h_{i}=f_{i}/g
we can write
U=\{|h_{i}|\leq 1:i=1,\ldots,d\}
. Let
h
be one of the
h_{i}
with maximal norm
|h|
, then
U=\{|h|\leq 1\}
. Now, if
U=\{|h|\leq 1\}
and
V=\{|g|\leq 1\}
, as we have either
|h|\leq|g|
or
|g|\leq|h|
, then
U\subset V
or
V\subset U
proving that the rational open subspaces of
\Spa\mathcal{A}
form a total order.
