Finally the last assertion about stalks of bounded affinoid rings hold since property (c) can be easily verified by construction.

(2)

Suppose that
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
is a point and let
f\in\mathcal{A}
, we have an induced map
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|\to\Spa(R\langle T\rangle,R^{+}+R\langle T\rangle^{00})
sending
T
to
f
. We can write
\Spa(R\langle T\rangle,R^{+}+R\langle T\rangle^{00})=\{|T|\leq 1\}\bigsqcup\{|T|>1\}
. Since
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
is a point it must land in one and only one term of the disjoint union, which translates in property (b) by definition of
\mathcal{A}^{+}
and
\mathcal{A}^{00}
.

Conversely, suppose that (b) holds. By the proof of part (1), all the rational subspaces of
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
are of the form
\{|f|\leq 1\}
for some
f\in\mathcal{A}
. But then, if
\{|f|\leq 1\}
does not contain the maximal point of
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
, one has that
f\notin\mathcal{A}^{+}
, which implies that the complement
\{|f^{-1}|<1\}=|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
, i.e. that
\{|f|\leq 1\}=\emptyset
. This shows that
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
has the trivial topology, and being a spectral space with a unique closed point it must consist on a single point.

Finally, the last assertion about constructible stalks holds since property (b) can be easily verified by construction.

∎

Lemma 2.7.12
.

Let
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i}
be a sifted diagram of bounded affinoid rings with colimit
\mathcal{A}
, then the natural map
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|\to\varprojlim_{i}|\Spa\mathcal{A}_{i}|
is a homeomorphism.

Proof.

We have a natural map
f:|\Spa\mathcal{A}|\to\varprojlim_{i}|\Spa\mathcal{A}_{i}|
. Since
\mathcal{A}[*]=\varinjlim_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}[*]
, any rational localization of
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
arises as the pullback of a rational localization of some
|\Spa\mathcal{A}_{i}|
. In particular, any constructible set of
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
is the pullback of some constructible set of some
|\Spa\mathcal{A}_{i}|
. Thus, it suffices to show that
f
is a bijection, this can be proved using the constructible topology. Let
x_{i}\in|\Spa\mathcal{A}_{i}|
be a compatible sequence of points, and let
\mathcal{A}_{i}(x_{i})_{\cons}
be the constructible stalk of
\mathcal{A}_{i}
at
x_{i}
. Then we have a map

\mathcal{A}\to\varinjlim_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}(x_{i})_{\cons}

where the right term is non-zero as none of the analytic rings are zero. By Lemma
2.7.11
(2.b), the adic spectrum of
\varinjlim_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}(x_{i})_{\cons}
is a point. But
\varinjlim_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}(x_{i})_{\cons}
is also the fiber of
f
along the sequence
(x_{i})_{i}
, this shows that
f^{-1}((x_{i})_{i})=\{x\}
is a point, proving that
f
is indeed a bijection.
∎

Lemma 2.7.13
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be a morphism in
\AffRing^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
such that
\mathcal{B}=\underline{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{A}/}
and that
\pi_{0}\underline{\mathcal{A}}\to\pi_{0}\underline{\mathcal{B}}
is surjective. Then
F:|\Spa\mathcal{B}|\to|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
is an immersion.

Proof.

Let
x\in\Spa\mathcal{A}
, then
\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{A}(x)_{\cons}
is either
0
or one has a surjection on
\pi_{0}
of
\mathcal{A}(x)_{\cons}\to\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{A}(x)_{\cons}
. One easily verifies that the tensor satisfies the condition (2.b) of Lemma
2.7.11
, this shows that the fiber
F^{-1}(x)
is either empty or a point. Furthermore, any element
g\in\mathcal{B}
can be lifted to an element
\tilde{g}\in\mathcal{A}
, this implies that a rational subspace of
|\Spa\mathcal{B}|
arises as the pullback of a rational subspace of
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
, and that
F
is an immersion.
∎

Proposition 2.7.14
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a bounded affinoid ring. Then the natural map
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|\to|\Spa\mathcal{A}|^{\dagger-\red}
is a homeomorphism preserving rational localizations. Moreover, for any
x\in|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
the underlying discrete rings of
\kappa(x)
and
\kappa_{\cons}(x)
are fields.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.7.13
we have an immersion
|\Spa\mathcal{A}^{\dagger-\red}|\to|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
. It suffices to show that it is bijective. But the constructible residue field of
\mathcal{A}
at
x
factors through
\mathcal{A}^{\dagger-\red}
, proving the claim. Finally, the fact that the underlying discrete rings of
\kappa(x)
and
\kappa_{\cons}(x)
are fields follows from Lemma
2.7.11
.
∎

Corollary 2.7.15
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be as in Lemma
2.7.13
. If
\pi_{0}(I)
is generated by its discrete points
\pi_{0}(I(*))
, then the image of
F
is the Zariski closed subspace
\{|f|=0:f\in I\}
.

Proof.

We can assume that both rings are static. Then
Z=\bigcap_{f\in I}\{|f|=0\}\subset|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
corresponds to the analytic ring
\mathcal{A}_{I^{\dagger}}=\varinjlim_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in I\\
n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{A}\langle\frac{f}{\pi^{n}}\rangle
. Therefore, the map
\mathcal{A}\to(\mathcal{A}_{I^{\dagger}})^{\dagger-\red}
factors through
\mathcal{B}
proving that the image of
|\Spa\mathcal{B}|
in
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
is
Z
by Proposition
2.7.14
.
∎

We do not know if a morphism
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
in
\AffRing^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
that is surjective on
\pi_{0}
induces a closed immersion in the underlying adic spaces. Nevertheless, it defines a closed subspace in a suitable quotient of the locale
S(\mathcal{A})
.

Definition 2.7.16
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\in\AffRing^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
, we let
|\Spa^{\dagger}\mathcal{A}|
denote the quotient of
S(\mathcal{A})
consisting of the idempotent algebras generated under arbitrary intersections and finite unions by iterations of idempotent algebras of the form
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[T]}\mathbb{Z}[[T]]
and
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[T]}\mathbb{Z}((T^{-1}))
for some
\mathbb{Z}[T]\to\mathcal{A}
, and algebras
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{R_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]}R_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle
for a map from a profinite set
S\to\mathcal{A}
.

Lemma 2.7.17
.

The map of locales
S(\mathcal{A})\to|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
factors as a map

S(\mathcal{A})\to|\Spa^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})|\to|\Spa\mathcal{A}|.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.3.1
the open subsets of
|\Spa\mathcal{A}|
are generated by composite of subspaces of the form
\{1\leq|g|\}
and
\{|g|\leq 1\}
. The complement of these spaces correspond to the idempotent algebras
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[T]}\mathbb{Z}[[T]]
and
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[T]}\mathbb{Z}((T^{-1}))
respectively. The lemma follows by Theorem
2.7.2
and the definition of
|\Spa^{\dagger}\mathcal{A}|
.
∎

Lemma 2.7.18
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\in\AffRing^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
, then the natural map

|\Spa^{\dagger}\mathcal{A}^{\dagger-\red}|\to|\Spa^{\dagger}\mathcal{A}|

is an isomorphism of locales.

Proof.

This follows from the invariance of localizations of the form
\mathbb{Z}[T]\to\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
and
R_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]\to R_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[S]\rangle
under the
\dagger
-nil-radical of Proposition
2.6.16
.
∎

Proposition 2.7.19
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be a map of bounded affinoid rings such that
\mathcal{B}
has the induced analytic structure and that is surjective on
\pi_{0}
. Then the natural map

|\Spa^{\dagger}\mathcal{B}|\to|\Spa^{\dagger}\mathcal{A}|

is a closed immersion of locales.

Proof.

By lemma (
2.7.18
) it suffices to construct an idempotent algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
in
|\Spa^{\dagger}\mathcal{A}|
such that we have a factorization
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{B}
and that
\mathcal{A}^{{}^{\prime},\dagger-\red}=\mathcal{B}^{\dagger-\red}
. Let
I=[\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}]
be the fiber, for any profinite set
S
and any map
S\to\pi_{0}(I)
let us consider the base change
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{R_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]}R_{{\square}}\{\mathbb{N}[S]\}^{\dagger}
, and let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
be the colimit of all such algebras. Then, by construction, the map
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
sends
I
to the
\dagger
-nil-radical
\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. This shows that

\mathcal{A}^{{}^{\prime},\dagger-\red}=\mathcal{B}^{\dagger-\red}

as wanted.
∎

2.7.1.
Derived Tate adic spaces

We end this section with the definition of derived Tate adic spaces.

Definition 2.7.20
.

(1)

We let
\Aff_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}:=\AffRing_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}^{\op}
be the
\infty
-category of
solid affinoid spaces
. For a ring
\mathcal{A}\in\AffRing_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}
, we let
\Aff_{\mathcal{A}}
be the
\infty
-category of solid affinoid spaces over
\mathcal{A}
. We also denote by
\AnSpec\mathcal{A}
the representable presheaf on anima over
\Aff_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}
defined by
\mathcal{A}
, we call
\AnSpec\mathcal{A}
the
analytic spectrum
of
\mathcal{A}
.

(2)

We let
\Aff^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}:=\AffRing^{b,\op}_{R_{{\square}}}
be the category of
bounded affinoid spaces
over
R_{{\square}}
. The
analytic topology
in
\Aff^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
is the Grothendieck topology defined by open affinoid coverings of
\Spa\mathcal{A}
.

Lemma 2.7.21
.

The analytic topology of
\Aff^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
is subcanonical.

Proof.

Let
\SpecAn\mathcal{B}\in\Aff^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
, we want to prove that the functor
\Map_{\Aff^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}}(-,\SpecAn\mathcal{B})=\Map_{\AffRing^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}}(\mathcal{B},-)
satisfies descent for the analytic topology of
\Aff^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
. Given
\SpecAn\mathcal{A}
a bounded affinoid ring, by definition of the category of analytic rings,
\Map_{\AffRing_{R_{{\square}}}}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})
is the full subanima of
\Map_{\AniRing_{R_{{\square}}}}(\underline{\mathcal{B}},\underline{\mathcal{A}})
whose connected components are those arrows
f:\underline{\mathcal{B}}\to\underline{\mathcal{A}}
such that any
\mathcal{A}
-complete module is
\mathcal{B}
-complete. Now let
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
be an analytic cover of
\mathcal{A}
, let
\mathcal{C}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{A}_{i}
and let
\mathcal{C}^{n}
be the
n
-th fold tensor product of
\mathcal{C}
over
\mathcal{A}
. The maps
\{\Spa\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i}
form an open cover of the categorified locale
\Spa\mathcal{A}
and by Theorem
2.2.5
one has descent of modules

(2.12)

\Mod(\mathcal{A})\to\varprojlim_{[n]\in\Delta}\Mod(\mathcal{C}^{n+1}).

In particular, the natural map
\underline{\mathcal{A}}=\varprojlim_{[n]\in\Delta}\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{n+1}
is an equivalence. Thus, the map

\Map_{\AffRing^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})\to\varprojlim_{[n]\in\Delta}\Map_{\AffRing^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}^{n+1})

is a fully faithful embedding, and to prove that it is an equivalence it suffices to check that it is essentially surjective, but this follows from (
2.12
) and the fact that
\Mod(\mathcal{B})
is stable under limits and colimits in
\Mod(\underline{\mathcal{B}})
.
∎

Definition 2.7.22
.

We let
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\an}(\Aff^{b}_{R_{{\square}}})
denote the sheaves on anima of bounded affinoid spaces with respect to the analytic topology. A
derived Tate adic space over
R_{{\square}}
(or more shortly, a derived adic space) is a sheaf
X\in{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\an}(\Aff^{b}_{R_{\square}})
that admits an open analytic cover by representable sheaves. We let
\AdicSp_{R_{{\square}}}
be the full
\infty
-subcategory of
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\an}(\Aff^{b}_{R_{{\square}}})
consisting on derived adic spaces over
R_{{\square}}
.

Given
X
a derived Tate adic space, let
\Mod_{X,{\square}}=\varprojlim_{\SpecAn\mathcal{A}\to X}\Mod(\mathcal{A})
be its
\infty
-category of solid quasi-coherent sheaves on
X
, and let
|X|=\varinjlim_{\SpecAn\mathcal{A}\to X}|\SpecAn\mathcal{A}|
be its associated topological space. We let
X_{adic}:=(|X|,\Mod_{X,{\square}})
denote the categorified locale of
X
obtained as the colimit of the categorified locales on bounded affinoid spaces mapping to
X

The following corollary follows from the definitions and Lemma
2.1.3
.

Corollary 2.7.23
.

Let
X
be a derived Tate adic space,
\{U_{i}\}_{i}
an analytic open cover of
X
by affinoid spaces, and
\Mod_{X,{\square}}=\varprojlim_{i}\Mod_{U_{i},{\square}}
. Then
|X|=\varinjlim_{i}|U_{i}|
is a locally spectral space. Morphisms of derived Tate adic spaces
X\to Y
induce morphisms of locally spectral spaces
|X|\to|Y|
. When restricted to derived Tate adic spaces over
\mathbb{Q}\otimes R_{{\square}}
, the functor
X\mapsto X_{adic}
from derived Tate adic spaces to categorified locales over
\mathbb{Q}\otimes R_{{\square}}
is fully faithful (eg. for derived Tate adic spaces over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
).

2.7.2.
Analytification functor

We finish this section by defining an analytification functor. Let
\PSh(\Aff_{R_{{\square}}})
be the category of presheaves on anima of solid affinoid rings over
R_{{\square}}
.

Definition 2.7.24
.

We define the analytification functor
(-)^{\an}
to be the composite
\PSh(\Aff_{R_{{\square}}})\xrightarrow{k^{*}}\PSh(\Aff^{b}_{R_{{\square}}})\to{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\an}(\Aff^{b}_{R_{{\square}}})
, where the first is the restriction along the inclusion
k:\Aff^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}\to\Aff_{R^{{\square}}}
, and the second is sheafification.

By Proposition
2.6.8
, the category
\AffRing_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}
of solid affinoid rings is generated by the compact projective objects
\mathbb{Z}[\underline{T}]_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
, where
\underline{T}
is a finite set of variables, and
S
a profinite set. Therefore, the analytification functor
(-)^{\an}
is the left Kan extension of its restriction to the objects
R\langle\underline{T}\rangle_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]]
. These are computed as follows:

Lemma 2.7.25
.

Let
\mathbb{D}_{R}:=\AnSpec R\langle T\rangle_{{\square}}
be the unit affinoid disc. For
S
a profinite set let us write
\mathbb{A}^{\alg}_{R,S}:=\AnSpec(R_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}[S]])
and
\mathbb{A}_{R,S}:=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\AnSpec(R_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[\pi^{n}S]\rangle)
. Then there is a natural equivalence

(\mathbb{D}_{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{A}_{R,S}^{\alg})^{\an}=\mathbb{D}_{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{A}_{R,S}^{\an}.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that both
k^{*}
and sheafification commute with finite limits, that
\mathbb{D}_{R}^{n}
is already a bounded affinoid space, and that
\mathbb{A}^{\an}_{R,S}
represents the functor on
\Aff^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
given by
\mathcal{A}\mapsto\underline{\mathcal{A}}(S)
.
∎

3.
Tate stacks

In this section we introduce a geometric framework to do derived rigid geometry. Following the theory of analytic stacks of Clausen and Scholze, we use the abstract
6
-functor formalisms of Mann
[
Man22b
,
Man22a
]
, revisited in
[
Zav23
]
and
[
Sch23
]
, to construct very general categories of solid and Tate stacks. We discuss other classical geometric objects and features like finitely presented morphisms of derived Tate adic spaces, the theory of the cotangent complex for analytic rings, formally étale and smooth morphisms, and Serre duality. Finally, we introduce new deformation properties for morphisms, called
\dagger
-formally smoothness and étaleness, that will be key in the theory of the analytic de Rham stack.

3.1.
Recollections on abstract six functor formalisms

In this section we briefly recall the definition of a six functor formalism and some of its most important features, we follow
[
Man22b
,
Man22a
]
and
[
Sch23
]
.

3.1.1.
Abstract six functor formalisms

A
geometric set up
is a pair
(\mathcal{C},E)
consisting on an
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
and a collection
E
of homotopy classes of edges in
\mathcal{C}
such that
E
contains all isomorphisms, and is stable under compositions and pullbacks. Throughout this section we assume that
\mathcal{C}
admits finite limits.

Provided the data
(\mathcal{C},E)
, one constructs a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category of correspondences
\Corr(\mathcal{C},E)
, see
[
Man22b
, Definition A.5.4]
and
[
Sch23
, Definition 2.3]
. In a more instructive way, the homotopy category of
\Corr(C,E)
has the following description: the objects of
\Corr(\mathcal{C},E)
are the objects of
\mathcal{C}
, the symmetric monoidal structure is given by direct products, an arrow from
X
to
Y
is a correspondence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

with
g\in E
, and the composition of two arrows is given by the outer correspondence of the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\times_{Y}W^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z.}

Definition 3.1.1

(
[
Man22b
, Definition A.5.6]
)
.

A
3
-functor formalism (or a pre
6
-functor formalism) on
(\mathcal{C},E)
is a lax symmetric monoidal functor

\mathscr{D}:\Corr(\mathcal{C},E)\to\Cat_{\infty}

where
\Cat_{\infty}
is endowed with the cartesian symmetric monoidal structure.

As it is explain in the paragraph after
[
Sch23
, Definition 2.4]
, the data of a
3
-functor formalism encodes a functor
\mathscr{D}:\mathcal{C}\to\Cat^{\otimes}_{\infty}
from
\mathcal{C}
to symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories, the pullback functors
f^{*}
, and the lower shriek functors
f_{!}
, in such a way that a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

is sent to the functor
g_{!}f^{*}:\mathscr{D}(X)\to\mathscr{D}(Y)
.

Definition 3.1.2
.

A
6
-functor formalism is a
3
-functor formalism for which the symmetric monoidal categories
\mathscr{D}(X)
for
X\in\mathcal{C}
are closed, and the functors
f^{*}
and
f_{!}
have right adjoints
f_{*}
and
f^{!}
respectively.

Remark 3.1.3

(Dual
3
-functor formalism)
.

From the datum of a
3
-functor formalism
\mathscr{D}:\Corr(\mathcal{C},E)\to\Cat_{\infty}
it is possible to construct a
dual

3
-functor formalism
\mathscr{D}^{op}
as in
[
Sch23
, Remark 6.3]
. Concretely,
\mathscr{D}^{op}
is constructed as the composite
\Corr(\mathcal{C},E)\xrightarrow{\mathscr{D}}\Cat_{\infty}\xrightarrow{(-)^{op}}\Cat_{\infty}
, see
[
Lur17
, Remark 2.4.2.7]
. At the level of objects, it maps
X
to the opposite symmetric monoidal category
\mathscr{D}(X)^{op}
.

The following lemma allows us to construct six functors by taking a precomposition.

Lemma 3.1.4
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},E)
be a geometric set up and let
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
be an
\infty
-category with finite limits. Let
F:\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{C}
be a functor preserving final objects and cartesian squares. Let
E^{\prime}
be the class of edges
s
in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
such that
F(s)\in E
, then
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},E^{\prime})
is a geometric set up and
F
induces a natural symmetric monoidal functor

\Corr(F):\Corr(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},E^{\prime})\to\Corr(\mathcal{C},E).

In particular, if
\mathscr{D}:\Corr(\mathcal{C},E)\to\Cat_{\infty}
is a
3
-functor formalism then
\mathscr{D}\circ\Corr(F):\Corr(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},E^{\prime})\to\Cat_{\infty}
is also a
3
-functor formalism.

Proof.

We use the notation of
[
Man22b
, Definitions A.5.2 and A.5.4]
. First, note that the class of arrows
E^{\prime}
is stable under compositions and pullbacks since
F
preserves cartesian squares. Let us first see that the hypothesis imply that there is a natural functor
\Corr(F):\Corr(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},E^{\prime})\to\Corr(\mathcal{C},E)
. Indeed by construction,
\Corr(\mathcal{C},E)
is the simplicial subset of
B(\mathcal{C})
whose
n
-cells are maps
C(\Delta^{n})\to\mathcal{C}
sending vertical edges to
E
and exact squares to pullback squares. Since
F:\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{C}
preserves cartesian squares, the restriction of
B(F):B(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})\to B(\mathcal{C})
to
\Corr(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},E^{\prime})
lands in
\Corr(\mathcal{C},E)
as wanted. For the symmetric monoidal structure, by
[
Man22b
, Definition A.5.4]
one has

\Corr(\mathcal{C},E)^{\otimes}=\Corr((\mathcal{C}^{\op,\bigsqcup})^{\op},E^{-}),

where
\mathcal{C}^{\op,\bigsqcup}
is the symmetric monoidal structure define by co-products, and the class of edges
E^{-}
are those living over
\id:\langle n\rangle\to\langle n\rangle
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
of the form
f:(Y_{j})_{1\leq j\leq n}\to(X_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
where
Y_{i}\to X_{i}
is in
E
. Then, since
F
preserves final objects and cartesian squares, it sends co-products to co-products in the opposite categories, so that we have a symmetric monoidal functor

(F^{\op,\bigsqcup})^{\op}:(\mathcal{C}^{{}^{\prime}\op,\bigsqcup})^{\op}\to(\mathcal{C}^{\op,\bigsqcup})^{\op}.

Moreover, by definition the functor
(F^{\op,\bigsqcup})^{\op}
still sends the edges
E^{{}^{\prime}-}
to
E^{-}
. Then, to finish the proof, we need to see that the natural functor

B((F^{\op,\bigsqcup})^{\op}):B((\mathcal{C}^{{}^{\prime}op,\bigsqcup})^{\op})\to B((\mathcal{C}^{op,\bigsqcup})^{\op})

restricts to a functor in the correspondence categories. This follows from the fact that
(F^{\op,\bigsqcup})^{\op}
still preserves cartesian diagrams and that it sends
E^{{}^{\prime}-}
to
E^{-}
.
∎

One of the major contributions of
[
Man22b
]
is the construction of
6
-functor formalisms from a minimal amount of data that is of easy access in practice, namely, we are usually given a functor
\mathscr{D}:\mathcal{C}\to\Cat^{\otimes}_{\infty}
with values in symmetric monoidal (stable)
\infty
-categories, and two classes of
étale
and
proper
maps
I
and
P
. It turns out that if the data
(\mathcal{C},\mathscr{D},I,P)
satisfies a minimal set of expected properties, one can construct a
6
-functor formalism for
(C,E)
in such a way that all element in
E
is written as
p\circ j
with
p\in P
and
j\in I
, that for
f\in P
one has
f_{*}=f_{!}
, and that for
f\in I
one has
f^{!}=f^{*}
. For the precise statement see
[
Man22b
, Proposition A.5.10]
.

On the other hand, the results of Mann permit the extension of a six functor formalism on
(\mathcal{C},E)
to a very general class of arrows in a suitable category of sheaves on anima of
\mathcal{C}
. To state such an extension theorem we need some definitions, we refer to
[
Sch23
, Appendix of Lecture IV]
for more details.

Let
(\mathcal{C},E)
be a geometric set up and suppose that the six functor formalism
\mathscr{D}:\Corr(\mathcal{C},E)\to\mathcal{P}r^{L,\ex}
takes values in presentably stable
\infty
-categories. Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{psh}
be the
\infty
-category of presheaves of anima of
\mathcal{C}
, and
\mathscr{D}:\Corr(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{psh},\widetilde{E}^{0})\to\mathcal{P}r^{L,ex}
its natural extension to a six-functor formalism on presheaves of anima, where
\widetilde{E}^{0}
are the arrows whose pullbacks to
\mathcal{C}
are representable in
E
(cf.
[
Man22b
, Proposition A.5.16]
).

Definition 3.1.5

(
[
Sch23
, Definition 4.14]
)
.

Consider
\{f_{i}:X_{i}\to Y\}
a family of objects in
\mathcal{C}
.

(1)

The maps
f_{i}
form a
canonical cover
if for all
Z\in\mathcal{C}
an any
Y^{\prime}\to Y
in
\mathcal{C}
with pullback
f_{i}^{\prime}:X_{i}^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}
, the functor
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(-,Z)
satisfies descent along
\{f_{i}^{\prime}\}
.

(2)

The maps
f_{i}
satisfy
universal
*
-descent
if for all pullbacks
\{f_{i}^{\prime}:X_{i}^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}\}
along a map
Y^{\prime}\to Y
in
\mathcal{C}
, the functor
\mathscr{D}^{*}
satisfy descent along
\{f_{i}^{\prime}\}
(i.e. where the transition maps are given by
f^{*}
-maps).

(3)

Assume all
f_{i}
are in
E
. The maps
f_{i}
satisfy
universal
!
-descent
if for all pullbacks
\{f_{i}^{\prime}:X_{i}^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}\}
along
Y^{\prime}\to Y
from a presheaf on anima on
\mathcal{C}
, the functor
\mathscr{D}^{!}
satisfies descent along
\{f_{i}^{\prime}\}
(i.e. where the transition maps are given by
f^{!}
-maps).

A
\mathscr{D}
-cover
is a family
\{f_{i}:X_{i}\to Y\}
of objects in
E
such that they form a cover in the canonical topology, satisfy universal
*
-descent, and satisfy universal
!
-descent. The
\mathscr{D}
-topology
on
\mathcal{C}
is the topology generated by
\mathscr{D}
-covers, we let
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
denote the
\infty
-category of sheaves on
\mathcal{C}
for the
\mathscr{D}
-topology.

Let
\widetilde{E}^{0}
the class of arrows in
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
represented by arrows in
E
. As we saw above, the six functor formalism of
(\mathcal{C},E)
extends to
(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}},\widetilde{E}^{0})
, we want to use the theory of
[
Man22b
, Appendix A.5]
to enlarge the class of arrows
\widetilde{E}^{0}
by localizing the target and the source of a map, this leads to the following definition.

Definition 3.1.6

(
[
Sch23
, Definition 4.18]
)
.

Let
\widetilde{E}^{0}\subset\widetilde{E}
be a class of morphisms in
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
that is stable under pullbacks and compositions.

(1)

The class
\widetilde{E}
is
stable under disjoint unions
if whenever
f_{i}:\widetilde{X}_{i}\to\widetilde{Y}
are morphisms in
\widetilde{E}
then
\bigsqcup_{i}f_{i}:\bigsqcup_{i}\widetilde{X}_{i}\to\widetilde{Y}
is in
\widetilde{E}
.

(2)

The class
\widetilde{E}
is
local on the target
if whenever
\widetilde{f}:\widetilde{X}\to\widetilde{Y}
is a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
such that for all
Y\in\mathcal{C}
with map
Y\to\widetilde{Y}
, the pullback
\widetilde{X}\times_{\widetilde{Y}}Y\to Y
is in
\widetilde{E}
, then
f\in\widetilde{E}
.

(3)

Assume that the six functors of
(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}},\widetilde{E}^{0})
extend uniquely to
(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}},\widetilde{E})
. The class
\widetilde{E}
is
local on the source
if whenever
\widetilde{f}:\widetilde{X}\to\widetilde{Y}
is a morphism in
\widetilde{C}
such that there is some map
\widetilde{g}:\widetilde{X}^{\prime}\to\widetilde{X}
in
\widetilde{E}
that is of universal
!
-descent, and such that
\widetilde{f}\circ\widetilde{g}
lies in
\widetilde{E}
, then
\widetilde{f}\in\widetilde{E}
.

(4)

Assume that the six functors of
(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}},\widetilde{E}^{0})
extend uniquely to
(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}},\widetilde{E})
. The class
\widetilde{E}
is
tame
if whenever
Y\in\mathcal{C}
and
\widetilde{f}:\widetilde{X}\to Y
is a map in
\widetilde{E}
, then there are morphisms
h_{i}:X_{i}\to Y
in
E
and a morphism
\bigsqcup_{i}X_{i}\to\widetilde{X}
over
Y
that lies in
\widetilde{E}
and is of universal
!
-descent.

Theorem 3.1.7

(
[
Sch23
, Theorem 4.20]
)
.

There is a minimal collection of morphisms
\widetilde{E}^{0}\subset\widetilde{E}
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
such that
\mathscr{D}
extends uniquely from
(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}},\widetilde{E}^{0})
to
(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}},\widetilde{E})
, and such that
\widetilde{E}
is stable under disjoint unions, local on the target, local on the source, and tame.

3.1.2.
The Lu-Zheng
2
-category

Let
(\mathcal{C},E)
be a geometric set up with finite limits and
\mathscr{D}
a six functor formalism on
(\mathcal{C},E)
. We assume that
\mathscr{D}:\Corr(\mathcal{C},E)\to\mathcal{P}r^{L,\ex}
takes values in presentable stable
\infty
-categories. Another important tool in the theory of six-functor formalisms is the
2
-category constructed by Lu-Zheng
[
LZ22
]
which encodes the Fourier-Mukai kernels between objects
X
and
Y
in
\mathcal{C}
living over a base
S
.

Definition 3.1.8

(
[
Man22a
, Definition 7.1]
)
.

Let
S\in\mathcal{C}
, the
Lu-Zheng category

\LZ_{\mathscr{D},S}
of
(\mathcal{C},E)
(relative to
\mathscr{D}
and
S
), is the
2
-category with objects given by arrows
X\to S
in
E
, for each pair of objects
X,Y
a
1
-category of functors
\mathrm{Hom}_{\LZ,S}(X,Y)=\mathscr{D}(X\times_{S}Y)
. The identity functor in
\mathscr{D}(X\times_{S}X)
is given by
\Delta_{!}1_{X}
where
\Delta:X\to X\times_{S}X
is the diagonal map. For a triple of objects
X,Y,Z
the composite transformations

\mathrm{Hom}_{\LZ,S}(Y,Z)\times\mathrm{Hom}_{\LZ,S}(X,Y)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\LZ,S}(X,Z)

are given by the Fourier-Mukai transform
M\star N=\pi_{1,3,!}(\pi_{1,2}^{*}N\otimes\pi_{2,3}^{*}M)
for
N\in\mathscr{D}(X\times_{S}Y)
,
M\in\mathscr{D}(Y\times_{S}Z)
and
\pi_{i,j}
the corresponding projection of
X\times_{S}Y\times_{S}Z
.

Remark 3.1.9
.

In
[
Zav23
, Proposition 2.2.6]
, Zavyalov shows that the Lu-Zheng category has a natural
(\infty,2)
-categorical enhancement.

With the help of the Lu-Zheng category one defines smooth and proper objects, cf.
[
Sch23
, Definition 6.1]
.

Definition 3.1.10
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},E)
be a six functor formalism,
S\in\mathcal{C}
and
f:X\to S
an arrow in
E
.

(1)

An object
M\in\mathscr{D}(X)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\LZ,S}(X,S)
is called
f
-
smooth
if it is a left adjoint in
\LZ_{\mathscr{D},S}
.

(2)

An object
M\in\mathscr{D}(X)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\LZ,S}(S,X)
is called
f
-
proper
if it is a left adjoint in
\LZ_{\mathscr{D},S}
.

The following proposition provides different equivalent characterizations of
f
-smooth and
f
-proper objects.

Proposition 3.1.11
.

Let
f:X\to S
be an arrow in
E
,
p_{i}:X\times_{S}X\to X
the projection maps and
\Delta:X\to X\times_{S}X
the diagonal map. Let
Q\in\mathscr{D}(X)
.

(1)

Let
\mathcal{D}_{f}(Q)=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{X}(Q,f^{!}1_{S})
. The following are equivalent

(a)

Q
is
f
-smooth.

(b)

The natural map
p_{1}^{*}D_{f}(Q)\otimes p_{2}^{*}Q\to\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{X\times_{S}X}(p_{1}^{*}Q,p_{2}^{!}Q)
is an equivalence.

(c)

For all
g:S^{\prime}\to S
with pullback
f^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\to S^{\prime}
and projection map
g:X^{\prime}\to X
the following natural functors are equivalences

\begin{gathered}\mathcal{D}_{f^{\prime}}(g^{{}^{\prime}*}Q)\otimes f^{{}^{\prime}*}\to\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{X^{\prime}}(g^{{}^{\prime}*}Q,f^{{}^{\prime}!}),\\
g^{{}^{\prime}*}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{X}(Q,f^{!})\to\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{X^{\prime}}(g^{{}^{\prime}*}Q,f^{{}^{\prime}!}g^{*}).\end{gathered}

If these conditions holds then
\mathcal{D}_{f}(Q)
is also
f
-smooth with right adjoint
Q
.

(2)

Let
\mathcal{P}_{f}(Q)=p_{2,*}(\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{X\times_{S}X}(p_{1}^{*}Q,\Delta_{!}1_{X}))
. The following are equivalent

(a)

Q
is
f
-proper.

(b)

The natural map
f_{!}(Q\otimes\mathcal{P}_{f}(Q))\to f_{*}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{X}(Q,Q)
is an equivalence.

(c)

For all
g:S^{\prime}\to S
with pullback
f^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\to S^{\prime}
and projection map
g:X^{\prime}\to X
the following natural functors are equivalences

\begin{gathered}f^{\prime}_{!}(-\otimes\mathcal{P}_{f^{\prime}}(g^{{}^{\prime}*}Q))\to f^{\prime}_{*}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{X^{\prime}}(g^{{}^{\prime}*}Q,-),\\
g^{*}f_{*}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{X}(Q,-)\to f^{\prime}_{*}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{X^{\prime}}(g^{{}^{\prime}*}Q,g^{{}^{\prime}*}(-)).\end{gathered}

If these conditions holds then
\mathcal{P}_{f}(Q)
is also
f
-proper with right adjoint
Q
.

Proof.

The point (1) is precisely
[
Man22a
, Proposition 7.7]
. For point (2), the equivalence between (a) and (b) is
[
Sch23
, Proposition 6.9]
. The implication (c) to (b) follows by taking
g=\id_{S}
and evaluating the first equivalence at
Q
. For (a), (b) implies (c), consider the natural transformation
\LZ_{\mathscr{D},S}\to\LZ_{\mathscr{D},S^{\prime}}
obtained by taking pullback along
g
, then
g^{*}
preserves adjunctions which implies that
g^{{}^{\prime}*}Q
is
f^{\prime}
-proper with dual
g^{{}^{\prime}*}\mathcal{P}_{f}(Q)
. By
[
Sch23
, Proposition 6.8 (3)]
there is a natural equivalence
g^{{}^{\prime}*}\mathcal{P}_{f}(Q)\cong\mathcal{P}_{f^{\prime}}(g^{{}^{\prime}*}Q)
. Now, the adjunction between
g^{{}^{\prime}*}Q
and
\mathcal{P}_{f^{\prime}}(g^{{}^{\prime}*}Q)
shows that the functor
f^{\prime*}\otimes g^{{}^{\prime}*}Q:\mathscr{D}(S^{\prime})\to\mathscr{D}(X^{\prime})
has by right adjoint the functor
f_{!}(-\otimes\mathcal{P}_{f^{\prime}}(g^{{}^{\prime}*}Q))
, but the first has also by right adjoint
the functor
f_{*}^{\prime}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{X^{\prime}}(g^{{}^{\prime}*}Q,-)
, which provides the first equivalence of functors. The second isomorphism follows from the first, proper base change, and the natural identification
g^{{}^{\prime}*}\mathcal{P}_{f}(Q)\cong\mathcal{P}_{f^{\prime}}(g^{{}^{\prime}*}Q)
.
∎

Proposition 3.1.12

(
[
Man22a
, Proposition 7.11]
)
.

Let
f:Y\to X
and
g:Z\to Y
be maps in
E
, let
P\in\mathscr{D}(Y)
and
Q\in\mathscr{D}(Z)
.

(1)

If
P
is
f
-smooth and
Q
is
g
-smooth then
Q\otimes g^{*}P
is
(f\circ g)
-smooth, and the natural map

g^{*}\mathcal{D}_{f}(P)\otimes\mathcal{D}_{g}(Q)\to\mathcal{D}_{f\circ g}(Q\otimes g^{*}P)

is an equivalence.

(2)

If
P
is
f
-proper and
Q
is
g
-proper then
Q\otimes g^{*}P
is
(f\circ g)
-proper, and there is a natural equivalence

\mathcal{P}_{f\circ g}(Q\otimes g^{*}P)\cong g^{*}\mathcal{P}_{f}(P)\otimes\mathcal{P}_{g}(Q).

Proof.

In
loc. cit.
it is shown part (1), the same argument using the dual six functors
\mathscr{D}^{\op}
recovers part (2), see Remark
3.1.3
.
∎

Remark 3.1.13
.

The equivalence in (2) of Proposition
3.1.12
is not very explicit, it is obtained from a very involved adjunction in the Lu-Zheng category.

Proposition 3.1.14

(Local on the target and stable by base change)
.

Consider a cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

with
f\in E
, and let
Q\in\mathscr{D}(X)
. The following hold:

(1)

If
Q
is
f
-smooth (resp.
f
-proper) then
g^{{}^{\prime}*}Q
is
f^{\prime}
-smooth (resp.
f^{\prime}
-proper).

(2)

If
g
satisfies universal
*
-descent and
g^{{}^{\prime}*}Q
is
f^{\prime}
-smooth (resp.
f^{\prime}
-proper) then
Q
is
f
-smooth (resp.
f
-proper).

Proof.

For smooth objects this is
[
Man22a
, Corollary 7.8]
, the same proof applies in the abstract context. Note that the only property of a
v
-cover that is used in
loc. cit.
is that it satisfies universal
*
-descent which holds in our case by hypothesis. The case for proper objects follows by dual arguments in the sense of Remark
3.1.3
as we describe next: point (1) follows from the fact that the natural transformation of
2
-categories
g^{*}:\LZ_{\mathscr{D},S}\to\LZ_{\mathscr{D},S^{\prime}}
preserves adjunctions. For part (2), by Proposition
3.1.11
(2.b) it suffices to show that the natural transformation

f_{!}(-\otimes\mathcal{P}_{f}(Q))\to f_{*}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{X}(Q,-)

is an equivalence. Let
S^{\prime}_{\bullet}
be the Čech nerve of
g
, and
X^{\prime}_{\bullet}
its pullback to
X
. By universal
*
-descent we have natural equivalences

\mathscr{D}(S)\to\varprojlim_{[n]\in\Delta}\mathscr{D}(S^{\prime}_{n})\mbox{ and }\mathscr{D}(X)\to\varprojlim_{[n]\in\Delta}\mathscr{D}(X^{\prime}_{n}).

Consider the functor

\mathscr{D}(X^{\prime}_{\bullet})\to\mathscr{D}(S^{\prime}_{\bullet})\;\;\;M_{\bullet}\mapsto f^{\prime}_{\bullet,*}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{X^{\prime}_{\bullet}}(g^{{}^{\prime}*}_{\bullet}Q,M_{\bullet}).

By the second equivalence in Proposition
3.1.11
(2.c), it preserves cocartesian sections, so it descends to a functor

\mathscr{D}(X)\to\mathscr{D}(S),

by looking at left adjoints one shows that this functor is actually equal to
f_{*}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{X}(Q,-)
. It follows that the natural map

g^{{}^{\prime}*}f_{*}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{X}(Q,-)\to f^{{}^{\prime}}_{*}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{X^{\prime}}(g^{{}^{\prime}*}Q,-)

is an equivalence of functors. On the other hand, since
g^{{}^{\prime}*}Q
is
f^{\prime}
-proper, the simplicial object
\mathcal{P}_{f^{\prime}_{\bullet}}(g^{{}^{\prime}*}_{\bullet}Q)
is a cocartesian section of
\mathscr{D}(X^{\prime}_{\bullet})
and it descents to an object
B\in\mathscr{D}(X)
. Moreover, the isomorphism of functors

f^{\prime}_{\bullet,!}(-\otimes\mathcal{P}_{f^{\prime}_{\bullet}}(g^{{}^{\prime}*}_{\bullet}Q))\to f^{\prime}_{\bullet,*}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{X^{\prime}_{\bullet}}(g^{{}^{\prime}*}_{\bullet}Q,-)

provided by Proposition
3.1.11
(2.c) descents to an equivalence of functors

f_{!}(-\otimes B)\to f_{*}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{X}(Q,-).

Note that the previous equivalence passes through any base change
S^{\prime\prime}\to S
. Taking the base change along
X\to S
and evaluating at
\Delta_{!}1_{X}
one gets that
B=\mathcal{P}_{f}(Q)
and that the previous arrow is the natural one coming from the adjunction of
[
Sch23
, Proposition 6.9]
, this finishes the proof.
∎

The following are two practical ways to construct
\mathscr{D}
-covers in a six functor formalism, they correspond to smooth and proper descent respectively.

Proposition 3.1.15

(
[
Sch23
, Proposition 6.18]
)
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism in
E
such that
1_{X}
is
f
-smooth. Then

f^{*}:\mathscr{D}(Y)\to\mathscr{D}(X)

is conservative if and only if the natural map

\varinjlim_{[n]\in\Delta^{\op}}f_{n+1,!}f^{n+1,!}(1_{Y})\to 1_{Y}

is an isomorphism (where
f_{n+1}:X^{n+1/Y}\to Y
is the
n+1
-th fold fiber product), and this condition passes to any base change. In that case, the pullback functors

(f^{*}_{n+1})_{n}:\mathscr{D}(Y)\to\varprojlim_{[n]\in\Delta}\mathscr{D}(X^{n+1/Y})\mbox{ and }(f^{!}_{n+1})_{n}:\mathscr{D}(Y)\to\varprojlim_{[n]\in\Delta}\mathscr{D}(X^{n+1/Y})

are equivalences. In particular, if
f
is a canonical cover, then it is a
\mathscr{D}
-cover, and of universal
*
and
!
-descent.

Proposition 3.1.16

(
[
Sch23
, Proposition 6.19]
)
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be an arrow in
E
such that
1_{X}
is
f
-proper, with
f_{n}:X^{n/Y}\to Y
the
n
-th fold fiber product. Assume that the map

1_{Y}\to``{\varprojlim_{[n]\in\Delta}}"f_{n+1,*}1_{X^{n+1/Y}}

is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{Pro}(\mathscr{D}(Y))
; equivalently,
f_{*}1_{X}\in\mathrm{CAlg}(\mathscr{D}(Y))
is descendable.

Then the pullback functors

(f^{*}_{n+1})_{n}:\mathscr{D}(Y)\to\varprojlim_{[n]\in\Delta}\mathscr{D}(X^{n+1/Y})\mbox{ and }(f^{!}_{n+1})_{n}:\mathscr{D}(Y)\to\varprojlim_{[n]\in\Delta}\mathscr{D}(X^{n+1/Y})

are equivalences. In particular, if
f
is a canonical cover, then it is a
\mathscr{D}
-cover, and of universal
*
and
!
-descent.

3.1.3.
Cohomologically smooth and co-smooth maps

Let
\mathscr{D}
be a six functor formalism on
(\mathcal{C},E)
taking values in presentable stable
\infty
-categories, and suppose that
\mathcal{C}
admits finite limits. One of the main advantages of the abstract six functor formalisms is that one can axiomatize cohomological properties of smooth and proper maps in algebraic geometry via the Lu-Zheng category. We follow
[
Man22a
, §8]
and
[
Sch23
, Lecture V]
for the definition of cohomologically smooth maps. For the replacement of proper maps, we will use a weaker notion suggested at the beginning of
[
Man22a
, §9]
which we shall call
co-smooth maps
, this is the same as cohomologically smooth for the dual six functor formalism
\mathscr{D}^{\op}
.

Definition 3.1.17
.

An arrow
f:X\to Y
in
E
is
cohomologically smooth
if
1_{X}
is
f
-smooth and
\mathcal{D}_{f}(1_{X})=f^{!}1_{Y}
is invertible. Similarly,
f
is
cohomologically co-smooth
if
1_{X}
is
f
-proper and
\mathcal{P}_{f}(1_{X})
is invertible.

Translating the definition of cohomologically smooth and co-smooth maps from the Lu-Zheng category to functors, a smooth map
f:X\to Y
gives a natural equivalence of functors
f^{!}1_{Y}\otimes f^{*}\xrightarrow{\sim}f^{!}
while a co-smooth map gives a natural equivalence of functors
f_{!}(-\otimes\mathcal{P}_{f}(1_{X}))\xrightarrow{\sim}f_{*}
. Among smooth and co-smooth maps, there are two special families consisting on étale and proper maps.

Definition 3.1.18

(
[
Sch23
, Definitions 6.10 and 6.12]
)
.

Let
f:Y\to X
be an
n
-truncated map in
E
.

(1)

We say that
f
is
cohomologically proper
if
\Delta_{f}
is cohomologically proper or an isomorphism, and if
1_{Y}
is
f
-proper.

(2)

We say that
f
is
cohomologically étale
if
\Delta_{f}
is cohomologically étale or an isomorphism, and if
1_{Y}
is
f
-smooth.

In coherent cohomology, the maps
f:Y\to X
that are proper in a suitable geometric sense are far from being
n
-truncated. However, one still would expect to have identifications
f_{!}=f_{*}
, and that the functor
f_{*}
preserves “coherent” sheaves. Moreover, if the six functor formalism arises from a geometric decomposition
(I,P)
one would expect that the arrows in
I
and
P
are étale and proper in a suitable sense respectively. As a replacement of cohomologically étale and proper maps we define the following weaker notion:

Definition 3.1.19
.

Consider and arrow
f:Y\to X
in
E
and let
\Delta_{f}:Y\to Y\times_{X}Y
. We say that
f
is
weakly cohomologically proper
(resp. étale) if the following hold:

(1)

1_{Y}
is
\Delta_{f}
-proper (resp.
f
-étale) and there is a (non-canonical) equivalence
\mathcal{P}_{\Delta_{f}}(1_{Y})\simeq 1_{Y}
(resp. an equivalence
\mathcal{D}_{\Delta_{f}}(1_{Y})\simeq 1_{Y}
).

(2)

1_{Y}
is
f
-proper (resp.
f
-étale).

Remark 3.1.20
.

Note that, under the hypothesis of the definition, the equivalence
\mathcal{P}_{\Delta_{f}}(1_{Y})\simeq 1_{Y}
, induces an equivalence
\mathcal{P}_{f}(1_{Y})\simeq 1_{Y}
. In particular, we have (non-natural) isomorphisms of functors
\Delta_{f,!}\simeq\Delta_{f,*}
and
f_{!}\simeq f_{*}
. Similarly for weakly cohomological étale maps.

The following lemma gives a way to construct weakly cohomologically étale and proper maps from
I
and
P
.

Lemma 3.1.21
.

Suppose that the six functor formalism on
(\mathcal{C},E)
arises from a suitable decomposition
(I,P)
. Let
f:\widetilde{X}\to\widetilde{S}
be a map in
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
that is, locally in the
\mathscr{D}
-topology on
\widetilde{S}
, representable by an arrow in
I
(resp.
P
). Then
f
is weakly cohomologically étale (resp. proper).

Proof.

By Proposition
3.1.14
(2) it suffices to prove that an arrow of
I
(resp.
P
) satisfies the conclusion of the lemma, this follows from the proof of
[
Sch23
, Propositions 6.11 and 6.13]
since diagonal maps of arrows in
I
are in
I
(resp.
P
), and by construction
f^{*}=f^{!}
for
f\in I
(resp.
f_{*}=f_{!}
for
f\in P
).
∎

Remark 3.1.22
.

If the category
\mathcal{C}
is
n
-truncated then the maps in
I
and
P
are cohomologically étale and proper as in Definition
3.1.18
. For a general
\mathcal{C}
, it is not clear to the author how to define a natural notion of cohomologically étale and proper maps that contains the
n
-truncated ones, and the arrows of
I
and
P
respectively.

Lemma 3.1.23

(
[
Man22a
, Lemma 8.7]
)
.

Cohomologically smooth and co-smooth maps are stable under pullbacks and compositions, and
*
-local on the target.

Proof.

Stability under pullbacks and local in the target for universal
*
-descent maps follows from Proposition
3.1.14
. Stability under composition follows from Proposition
3.1.12
.
∎

One of the interest of cohomologically smooth and co-smooth maps is that they will preserve smooth or proper objects in a suitable sense.

Proposition 3.1.24

(Local on the source and stable under composition)
.

Let
f:Y\to X
and
g:Z\to Y
be maps in
E
and let
P\in\mathscr{D}(Y)
. The following hold:

(1)

Assume that
g
is smooth.

(i)

If
P
is
f
-smooth then
g^{*}P
is
(f\circ g)
-smooth with dual
g^{*}\mathcal{D}_{f}(P)\otimes g^{!}1_{Y}
.

(ii)

If
g
is a
\mathscr{D}
-cover and
g^{*}P
is
(f\circ g)
-smooth then
P
is
f
-smooth.

(2)

Assume that
g
is co-smooth.

(i)

If
P
is
f
-proper then
g^{*}P
is
(f\circ g)
-proper with dual
g^{*}\mathcal{P}_{f}(P)\otimes\mathcal{P}_{g}(1_{Z})
.

(ii)

If
g
is a
\mathscr{D}
-cover satisfying the hypothesis of Proposition
3.1.16
and
g^{*}P
is
(f\circ g)
-proper then
P
is
f
-proper.

Proof.

Part (1) is
[
Man22a
, Proposition 8.6]
. Part (2.i) follows from Proposition
3.1.12
. For part (2.ii), we cannot directly dualize the argument of
loc. cit
since the functors
f^{*}
and
f_{!}
in
\mathscr{D}^{\op}
might not have right adjoints, instead we will make use of the descendability property of
g
.

By Proposition
3.1.11
(2.c), it suffices to prove that there is some
Q\in\mathscr{D}(Z)
and a natural equivalance of functors

f_{!}(-\otimes Q)\to f_{*}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{Y}(P,-),

that holds after any base change
X^{\prime}\to X
; one then necessarily has that
Q=\mathcal{P}_{f}(P)
by taking the pullback along
Y\to X
and evaluating at
\Delta_{!}1_{Y}
. Let
g_{\bullet}:Z_{\bullet}\to Z
be the Čech nerve of
Z
over
Y
. By Lemma
3.1.23
, co-smooth covers are stable under composition and pullbacks, in particular, any projection
Z_{n}\to Z
is co-smooth and by part (2.i)
g_{n}^{*}P\in\mathscr{D}(Z_{n})
is
(f\circ g_{n})
-proper over
X
. Furthermore, part (2.i) also gives rise a cosimplicial object
(\mathcal{P}_{f\circ g_{\bullet}}(g^{*}_{\bullet}P)\otimes\mathcal{P}_{g_{\bullet}}(1_{Z_{\bullet}})^{-1})_{[n]\in\Delta}
which is a co-cartesian section in
\mathscr{D}(Z_{\bullet})
, defining an object
Q\in\mathscr{D}(Z)
. Therefore, the functor

(3.1)

g_{\bullet}^{*}P\otimes(f\circ g_{\bullet})^{*}:\mathscr{D}(X)\to\mathscr{D}(Z_{\bullet})

descends to the functor
P\otimes f^{*}:\mathscr{D}(X)\to\mathscr{D}(Y)
. For each
[n]\in\Delta
, the functor
g_{n}^{*}P\otimes(f\circ g_{n})^{*}
has by right adjoint
(f\circ g_{n})_{*}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{Z_{n}}(g_{n}^{*}P,-)
which by Proposition
3.1.11
(2.c) is naturally isomorphic to

(f\circ g_{n})_{!}(-\otimes\mathcal{D}_{(f\circ g_{n})}(g_{n}^{*}P))=(f\circ g_{n})_{!}(-\otimes g_{n}^{*}Q\otimes\mathcal{P}_{g_{n}}(1_{Z_{n}}))=f_{!}(g_{n,*}(-)\otimes Q).

Therefore, (
3.1
) has by right adjoint the totalization

\varprojlim_{[n]\in\Delta}f_{!}(g_{n,*}(-)\otimes Q).

Evaluating at a co-cartesian section
M_{n}
of
\mathscr{D}(Z_{\bullet})
, the proof of
[
Sch23
, Proposition 6.19]
shows that the Pro-system
(g_{n,*}M_{n})_{[n]\in\Delta}
is pro-constant, this implies that

\varprojlim_{[n]\in\Delta}f_{!}(g_{n,*}M_{n}\otimes Q)\cong f_{!}(\varprojlim_{[n]\in\Delta}(g_{n,*}M_{n})\otimes Q).

Therefore,
P\otimes f^{*}
has by right adjoint
f_{!}(-\otimes Q)
, which provides the natural equivalence

(3.2)

f_{!}(-\otimes Q)\xrightarrow{\sim}f_{*}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{Y}(P,-).

Finally, note that the formation of (
3.2
) is natural with respect to base change
X^{\prime}\to X
by construction. This finishes the proof.
∎

Definition 3.1.25
.

Let
f:Y\to X
be an arrow in
E
. We say that
f
is a
smooth (resp. descendable)
\mathscr{D}
-cover
if it is smooth (resp. co-smooth), it is a canonical cover, and it satisfies the hypothesis of Proposition
3.1.15
(resp. of Proposition
3.1.16
).

We deduce the following corollary from Proposition
3.1.24
.

Corollary 3.1.26
.

Being cohomomologically smooth is smooth
\mathscr{D}
-local on the source. Analogously, being cohomologically co-smooth is descendably
\mathscr{D}
-local on the source.

Smooth and descendable
\mathscr{D}
-covers provides a description of the coefficients of the quotient as modules and comodules respectively.

Proposition 3.1.27
.

Let
f:Y\to X
be an arrow in
E
.

(1)

Suppose that
f
is a smooth
\mathscr{D}
-cover. Then there is a natural equivalence of stable
\infty
-categories

\mathscr{D}(X)\xrightarrow{\sim}\Mod_{f^{!}f_{!}}(\mathscr{D}(Y)),

where the monad
f^{!}f_{!}
naturally belongs to
\Alg((\End^{L}_{\mathscr{D}(X)}(\mathscr{D}(Y)))
.

(2)

Suppose that
f
is a descendable
\mathscr{D}
-cover. Then there is a natural equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\mathscr{D}(X)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{CoMod}_{f^{*}f_{*}}(\mathscr{D}(Y)),

where the comonad
f^{*}f_{*}
naturally belongs to
\mathrm{CoAlg}((\End^{L}_{\mathscr{D}(X)}(\mathscr{D}(Y)))
.

Moreover, if
f
has a retraction
g:X\to Y
then the monad
f^{!}f_{!}
in (1) arises from an object
\mathcal{D}_{f}\in\Alg(\End_{\mathscr{D}(Y)}(\mathscr{D}(Y)))=\Alg(\mathscr{D}(Y))
, and the comonad
f^{*}f_{*}
arises from an object
\mathcal{C}_{f}\in\mathrm{CoAlg}(\End^{L}_{\mathscr{D}(Y)}(\mathscr{D}(Y)))=\mathrm{CoAlg}(\mathscr{D}(Y))
.

Proof.

In the case of (1), both functors
f^{!}
and
f_{!}
are linear over
\mathscr{D}(X)
, namely
f^{!}=f^{*}\otimes f^{*}1_{X}
and
f^{*}
is
\mathscr{D}(X)
-linear being symmetric monoidal, and
f_{!}
is
\mathscr{D}(X)
-linear by the projection formula. This implies that modules of the monad
f^{!}f_{!}
arises from an object
f^{!}f_{!}\in\Alg(\End^{L}_{\mathscr{D}(X)}(\mathscr{D}(Y)))
.

Similarly, in the case of (2), we have a natural equivalence
f_{*}=f_{!}(-\otimes\mathcal{P}_{f}(1_{Y}))
, proving that
f_{*}
satisfies the projection formula so that is
\mathscr{D}(X)
-linear. Therefore, the comonad
f^{*}f_{*}
arises from an object
f^{*}f_{*}\in\mathrm{CoAlg}(\End^{L}_{\mathscr{D}(X)}(\mathscr{D}(Y)))
.

In order to prove the proposition we only need to show that the functor
f^{!}
is monadic in the case (1), and that
f^{*}
is comonadic in the case (2), see
[
Lur17
, Theorem 4.7.3.5]
. It is clear that both functors are conservative in both situations. In the case of (1), the functor
f^{!}
already preserves colimits being isomorphic to
f^{*}\otimes f^{!}1_{X}
, and the monadicity theorem can be applied. In the case of (2), in order to apply the comonadicity theorem we need to show that
f^{*}
preserves
f^{*}
-split totalizations.

By Proposition
3.1.16
we have that

\mathscr{D}(X)=\varprojlim_{[n]\in\Delta}\mathscr{D}(Y^{n+1/X})

along pullback maps. Let
(M_{m})_{[m]\in\Delta}
be a cosimplicial object in
\mathscr{D}(X)
whose pullback to
\mathscr{D}(Y)
is split. Then, for all
n\geq 0
, the pullback of
(M_{m})_{[m]\in\Delta}
to
\mathscr{D}(Y^{n+1/X})
is split with limit
N_{n+1}
. Moreover, the object
(N_{n+1})_{[n]\in\Delta}
is a cocartesian section in
\mathscr{D}(Y^{\bullet+1/X})
because of the splitting, and it defines an object
N
in the limit
\mathscr{D}(X)=\varprojlim_{[n]\in\Delta}\mathscr{D}(Y^{n+1/X})
. We deduce that

\displaystyle\varprojlim_{[m]\in\Delta}M_{m}

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{[m]\in\Delta}\varprojlim_{[n]\in\Delta}f_{n+1,*}f_{n+1}^{*}M_{m}

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{[n]\in\Delta}f_{n+1,*}\varprojlim_{[m]\in\Delta}f_{n+1}^{*}M_{m}

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{[n]\in\Delta}f_{n+1,*}N_{n+1}

\displaystyle=N.

We deduce that

f^{*}(\varprojlim_{[m]\in\Delta}M_{m})=f^{*}N=N_{1}=\varprojlim_{[m]\in\Delta}f^{*}M_{m},

proving that
f^{*}
preserves
f^{*}
-split totalizations.

Finally, if
f:Y\to X
has a retraction
g:Y\to X
, the functors
f^{!}
and
f_{!}
are
\mathscr{D}(Y)
-linear in the case of (1), and the functors
f^{*}
and
f_{*}
are
\mathscr{D}(Y)
-linear in the case of (2). This shows that the monad and comonad
f^{!}f_{!}
and
f^{*}f_{*}
in (1) and (2) respectively, arise from objects
\mathcal{D}_{f}\in\Alg(\End_{\mathscr{D}(Y)}^{L}(\mathscr{D}(Y)))=\Alg(\mathscr{D}(Y))
and
\mathcal{C}_{f}\in\mathrm{CoAlg}(\End^{L}_{\mathscr{D}(Y)}(\mathscr{D}(Y)))=\mathrm{CoAlg}(\mathscr{D}(Y))
respectively (see
[
Man22b
, Lemma A.4.7]
for the natural equivalence
\End_{\mathscr{D}(Y)}^{L}(\mathscr{D}(Y))=\mathscr{D}(Y)
).
∎

Remark 3.1.28
.

In the part (2) of Proposition
3.1.27
the only important conditions for the statement to hold is that
f_{*}
satisfies the projection formula, and that
f
satisfies universal
*
-descent.

We end this section by recalling how smooth and proper objects are preserved by lower shrieck functors under suitable hypothesis.

Proposition 3.1.29
.

Let
f:Y\to X
and
g:Z\to Y
be maps in
E
. Let
P,Q\in\mathscr{D}(Z)
. The following hold

(1)

If
P
is
(f\circ g)
-smooth and
Q
is
g
-proper then
g_{*}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{Z}(Q,P)=g_{!}(\mathcal{P}_{g}(Q)\otimes P)
is
f
-smooth.

(2)

If
P
is
(f\circ g)
-proper and
Q
is
g
-smooth then
g_{!}(Q\otimes P)
is
f
-proper.

Proof.

Part (1) is
[
Man22a
, Proposition 7.13]
, part (2) is proven with the same argument that we recall down below:

One has a morphisms of
2
-categories
\iota_{!}:\LZ_{\mathscr{D},Y}\to\LZ_{\mathscr{D},X}
mapping
[W\to Y]
to
[W\to X]
and
M\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\LZ,Y}(W,V)=\mathscr{D}(W\times_{Y}V)
to
\iota_{!}M\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\LZ,X}(W,V)=\mathscr{D}(W\times_{X}V)
where
\iota:W\times_{Y}V\to W\times_{X}V
. By hypothesis
P\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\LZ,X}(X,Z)
is a left adjoint and
Q\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\LZ,Y}(Z,Y)
is a left adjoint. Then, since
\iota_{!}
sends left adjoints to left adjoints, one has that

\iota_{!}Q\star P=\pi_{Y,!}(\pi_{Z}^{*}P\otimes\iota_{!}Q)=\pi_{Y,!}\iota_{!}(\iota^{*}\pi_{Z}^{*}P\otimes Q)=g_{!}(Q\otimes P)

is a left adjoint in
\mathrm{Hom}_{\LZ,X}(X,Y)
, proving what we wanted.
∎

Proposition 3.1.30

(
[
Man22b
, Proposition 9.10]
)
.

Let
f:Y\to X
and
g:Z\to Y
be maps in
E
, and
P\in\mathscr{D}(Y)
. The following hold:

(1)

If
g
is cohomologically co-smooth and
P
is
(f\circ g)
-smooth then
g_{*}P
and
g_{!}P
are
f
-smooth.

(2)

If
g
is cohomologically smooth and
P
is
(f\circ g)
-proper then
g_{!}P
is
f
-proper.

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
3.1.29
by taking
Q=1_{Z}
or
Q=\mathcal{P}_{f}(1_{Z})
for point (1), and taking
Q=1_{Z}
for point (2).
∎

We end this section with a couple of lemmas that will be useful later.

Lemma 3.1.31
.

Let
f:Y\to X
, then there is a natural equivalence

f^{!}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{X}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})\cong\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{Y}(f^{*}\mathscr{F},f^{!}\mathscr{G}).

In particular, if
f
is cohomological smooth we have that

f^{*}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{X}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})\cong\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{Y}(f^{*}\mathscr{F},f^{*}\mathscr{G}).

Proof.

This follows from the adjunctions:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{Y}(\mathscr{H},f^{!}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{X}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}))

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{X}(f_{!}\mathscr{H},\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{X}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G}))

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{X}(f_{!}\mathscr{H}\otimes\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{X}(f_{!}(\mathscr{H}\otimes f^{*}\mathscr{F}),\mathscr{G})

\displaystyle\cong=\mathrm{Hom}_{Y}(\mathscr{H}\otimes f^{*}\mathscr{F},f^{!}\mathscr{G})

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{Y}(\mathscr{H},\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{Y}(f^{*}\mathscr{F},f^{!}\mathscr{G})).

The claim about cohomologically smooth maps follows from the fact that
f^{!}=f^{*}\otimes f^{!}1_{X}
and that
f^{!}1_{X}
is an invertible object in
\mathscr{D}(Y)
.
∎

Lemma 3.1.32
.

Let
\mathscr{F}\in\mathscr{D}(X)
and let
f:Y\to X
be an arrow in
E
.

(1)

If
f
is a smooth cover and
f^{*}\mathscr{F}
is
f
-smooth, then
\mathscr{F}
is dualizable.

(2)

If
f
is a descendable cover and
f^{*}\mathscr{F}
is
f
-proper, then
\mathscr{F}
is dualizable.

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
3.1.24
as being
\id_{X}
-smooth or proper is equivalent to being a dualizable object in
\mathscr{D}(X)
.
∎

3.2.
Solid and Tate stacks

In this section we explain how the theory of abstract six functor formalisms of
[
Man22b
,
Man22a
]
and
[
Sch23
]
provides a very general six functor formalism of solid quasi-coherent sheaves for stacks. Throughout this section we fix an uncountable cutoff cardinal
\kappa
as in
[
Man22b
, Definition 2.9.11]
, in real world applications the construction of the six functors down below will be independent of
\kappa
large enough.

By
[
Man22b
, Lemma 2.9.12]
, if
(A,A^{+})
is a discrete animated Huber ring, then the forgetful functor
\Mod((A,A^{+})_{{\square}})\to\Mod(\underline{A})
preserves
\kappa
-small objects. Therefore, if
B\in\Mod((A,A^{+})_{{\square}})
is a
\kappa
-small algebra, the forgetful functor
\Mod((B,A^{+})_{{\square}})\to\Mod(\underline{B})
also preserves
\kappa
-small objects. From now on we will work with
\kappa
-small condensed sets. Recall that a solid affinoid ring
\mathcal{A}
is an analytic
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}
-algebra such that the natural map
(\underline{\mathcal{A}},\mathcal{A}^{+})_{{\square}}\to\mathcal{A}
is an equivalence. We let
\AffRing_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}},\kappa}
denote the
\infty
-category of solid affinoid rings
\mathcal{A}
with
\underline{\mathcal{A}}
being a
\kappa
-small condensed set, we let
\Aff_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}},\kappa}
denote its opposite category of
\kappa
-small solid affinoid spaces, we also let
\SpecAn\mathcal{A}\in\Aff_{\mathbb{Z}_{\square},\kappa}
denote the analytic spectrum of the solid affinoid ring
\mathcal{A}
.

We recall some basic properties of the categories of
\kappa
-small complete modules of analytic rings.

Proposition 3.2.1

(
[
Man22b
, Proposition 2.3.9]
)
.

Let
\AnRing_{\kappa}
be the full subcategory of analytic rings
\mathcal{A}
with
\kappa
-small underlying condensed rings, and let
\Mod(\mathcal{A})_{\kappa}
be the full subcategory of
\Mod(\mathcal{A})
generated under sifted colimits by the objects
\mathcal{A}[S]
with
S
a
\kappa
-small extremally disconnected set. The functor
\Mod(-):\AnRing\to\CAlg(\Cat^{\colim,\ex}_{\infty})
of complete modules restricts to a functor

\Mod(-)_{\kappa}:\AnRing_{\kappa}\to\CAlg(\mathcal{P}r^{L,\ex}),

of
\kappa
-small complete modules. In other words, for
\mathcal{A}
a
\kappa
-small analytic ring,
\Mod(\mathcal{A})_{\kappa}
is a presentably symmetric monoidal stable
\infty
-category, and for a map
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
of
\kappa
-small analytic rings, the base change
\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}-:\Mod(\mathcal{A})\to\Mod(\mathcal{B})
preserves
\kappa
-small analytic modules.

We now want to define a six functor formalism for solid affinoid rings, and then apply
[
Sch23
, Theorem 4.20]
to construct a very large six functor formalism for suitable stacks over
\Aff_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}},\kappa}
. For this, by
[
Man22b
, Proposition A.5.10]
, all we need is a minimal amount of data consisting on
étale
and
proper
arrows
(I,P)
in
\Aff_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}},\kappa}
satisfying some minimal properties, cf. Definition A.5.9 of
loc. cit
. The following definition is due to Clausen and Scholze.

Definition 3.2.2
.

We denote
\mathcal{C}=\Aff_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}},\kappa}
.

(1)

Let
I
be the family of arrows in
\mathcal{C}
consisting on morphisms
f:\SpecAn\mathcal{B}\to\SpecAn\mathcal{A}
such that
f^{*}:\Mod(\mathcal{A})\to\Mod(\mathcal{B})
is an open localization in the sense of Definition
2.2.2
, and such that the associated idempotent algebra
D
lies in
\Mod(\mathcal{A})_{\kappa}
.

(2)

Let
P
be the family of arrows in
\mathcal{C}
consisting on morphisms
f:\SpecAn\mathcal{B}\to\SpecAn\mathcal{A}
such that
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{A}/}
is induced from
\mathcal{A}
.

(3)

We let
E
be the family of arrows in
\mathcal{C}
of the form
f\circ i
with
i\in I
and
f\in P
.

We first need to check that
(\mathcal{C},E)
is a geometric set up.

Lemma 3.2.3
.

The class of arrows
I
,
P
and
E
are stable under composition and pullbacks in
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

The stability under pullbacks and compositions for the class
P
is obvious, for the class
I
follows from Theorem
2.2.5
. Stability under pullbacks of the class
E
follows from the stability for
I
and
P
, we are left to prove stability under composition for
E
. Consider two maps of analytic rings
f:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
and
g:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{D}
. Suppose that we have factorizations
f=j_{1}\circ p_{1}
with
p_{1}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
an induced analytic ring and
j_{1}:\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{B}
an open immersion. Similarly, suppose that
g=j_{2}\circ p_{2}
with
p_{2}:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and
j_{2}:\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{D}
. Then we can write
g\circ f
as the composite

\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}/}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{B}/}\to\mathcal{D},

the first arrow is in
P
by definition, the second and third arrows are open immersions, so it is their composite. This proves that
E
is stable under composition as wanted. Moreover, the idempotent algebra associated to the map
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}/}\to\mathcal{D}
is a "union" in the sense of Proposition
2.2.1
(5) of two
\kappa
-small
\mathcal{B}
-algebras, so it is
\kappa
-small.
∎

Remark 3.2.4
.

Given
\SpecAn\mathcal{A}\in\Aff_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}},\kappa}
, the stable
\infty
-category
\Mod(\mathcal{A})_{\kappa}
is closed by the adjoint functor theorem. Indeed, the inclusion
\Mod(\mathcal{A})_{\kappa}\to\Mod(\mathcal{A})
has a right adjoint
(-)_{\kappa}
given by taking the underlying
\kappa
-small set, and the internal
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}
of
\Mod(\mathcal{A})_{\kappa}
is equal to
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{A}}(-,-)_{\kappa}
. This implies that both internal
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}
’s could differ for general objects
N,M\in\Mod(\mathcal{A})_{\kappa}
. However, after taking some big enough cardinal
\kappa^{\prime}>\kappa
one has that

\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{A}}(N,M)_{\kappa^{\prime}}=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{A}}(N,M).

Actually, the proof of
[
Man22b
, Proposition 2.1.11 (2)]
shows that the choice of the cardinal
\kappa^{\prime}
only depends on
N
: write
N=\varinjlim_{I}\mathcal{A}[S_{i}]
as a small colimit of compact projective generators with
S_{i}
a
\kappa
-small extremally disconnected set. Then,

\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{A}}(N,M)=\varprojlim_{I}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{A}[S],M).

Thus, after taking
\kappa^{\prime}
big enough such that
|I|<\kappa^{\prime}
and
\Mod(\mathcal{A})_{\kappa^{\prime}}\to\Mod(\mathcal{A})
commutes with
|I|
-small limits, one is reduced to prove that
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{A}[S],M)
is
\kappa^{\prime}
-small for all
\kappa
-small extremally disconnected set
S
and
M\in\Mod(\mathcal{A})_{\kappa}
. Writing
M
as a colimit of compact projective generators one just needs to take
\kappa^{\prime}
such that
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{A}[S],\mathcal{A}[S^{\prime}])
is
\kappa^{\prime}
-small for all
\kappa
-small extremally disconnected sets
S
and
S^{\prime}
.

Lemma 3.2.5
.

Keep the notation of Definition
3.2.2
. The pair
(I,P)
is a suitable decomposition of the geometric set up
(\mathcal{C},E)
. Moreover, it satisfies the criteria of
[
Man22b
, Proposition A.5.10]
, so that the functor
\Mod(-)_{\kappa}:\mathcal{C}\to\CAlg(\mathcal{P}r^{L,\ex})
enhances to a six functor formalism

\mathscr{D}=\Mod(-)_{\kappa}:\Corr(\mathcal{C},E)\to\mathcal{P}r^{L,\ex}.

Furthermore, for any arrow
f:\AnSpec\mathcal{B}\to\AnSpec\mathcal{A}
the functors
f^{*}
and
f_{*}
are independent of
\kappa
. For any
f\in E
, the functor
f_{!}
is independent of
\kappa
and there is some
\kappa^{\prime}>\kappa
such that for all
\kappa^{\prime\prime}\geq\kappa^{\prime}>\kappa
the restriction of the functor
f^{!}
from
\kappa^{\prime\prime}
-small modules to
\kappa
-small modules stabilizes.

Proof.

First, we check that the conditions of a
suitable decomposition
hold, cf.
[
Man22b
, Definition A.5.9]
. By definition, the objects in
E
are compositions
p\circ j
with
j\in I
and
p\in P
, so property (a) in
loc. cit.
holds. Next, if
f:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is an object in
I
, then
f^{*}:\Mod(\mathcal{A})\to\Mod(\mathcal{B})
is an open immersion in the sense of Definition
2.2.2
. Then,
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is a localization of analytic rings in the sense that
\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}
, and a morphism
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{D}
extends to
\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{D}
if and only if the natural map
\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{D}
is an equivalence, this shows that
f:\SpecAn\mathcal{B}\to\SpecAn\mathcal{A}
is
-1
-truncated, which implies condition (b). Finally, it is easy to check that
I
and
P
contain the identity maps and that satisfy the two-out-of-three property (use Theorem
2.2.5
for
I
), this gives (c) and (d) in
[
Man22b
, Definition A.5.9]
.

For the existence of a
3
-functor formalism we need to check the following conditions:

(i)

for
[j:\AnSpec\mathcal{B}\to\AnSpec\mathcal{A}]\in I
the following hold

—

j^{*}
admits a left adjoint
j_{!}

—

j_{!}
satisfies the proper base change.

—

j_{!}
satisfies the projection formula.

(ii)

for
f:\AnSpec\mathcal{B}\to\AnSpec\mathcal{A}
the following hold

—

f^{*}
admits a colimit preserving right adjoint
f_{*}
.

—

f_{*}
satisfies proper base change

—

f_{*}
satisfies the projection formula.

(iii)

For every cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

in
\mathcal{C}
such that
j\in I
and
f\in P
, the natural map
j_{!}f^{\prime}_{*}\xrightarrow{\sim}f_{*}j^{\prime}_{!}
is an isomorphism.

We will prove the properties for the derived categories
\Mod(\mathcal{A})
and then show that they preserve
\kappa
-small objects. Part (i) is a consequence of Proposition
2.2.4
. For part (ii),
f_{*}
is the forgetful functor, then the projection formula is clear as
f
is defined by a morphism of analytic rings
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
where
\mathcal{B}
has the induced structure of
\mathcal{A}
. It is left to check that
f_{*}
satisfies proper base change, but if
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{D}
is another morphism of analytic rings, then
\mathcal{B}\otimes^{L}_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{D}=(\underline{\mathcal{B}}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{D})_{\mathcal{D}/}
and the proper base change formula is clear. Finally we prove (iii), let
X
,
X^{\prime}
,
U
and
U^{\prime}
be the analytic spectrum of
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
. Then we have that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}/}
, and that
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{B}
. Let
D\in\Mod(\mathcal{A})
be the idempotent algebra that complements
U
in
X
, then
D^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}D
is the idempotent algebra that complements
U^{\prime}
in
X^{\prime}
. Let
M\in\Mod(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
, by definition we have that

j_{!}f^{\prime}_{*}M=[\mathcal{A}\to D]\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}M

and

f_{*}j^{\prime}_{!}M=[\mathcal{A^{\prime}}\to D^{\prime}]\otimes_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}M,

but
[\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\to D^{\prime}]=[\mathcal{A}\to D]\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\underline{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}
so that

\displaystyle\otimes_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}M

\displaystyle=[\mathcal{A}\to D]\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\underline{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}\otimes_{\underline{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{A}/}}M

\displaystyle=[\mathcal{A}\to D]\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}M

proving that the natural map
j_{!}f^{\prime}_{*}\to f_{*}j^{\prime}_{!}
is an equivalence.

Finally, we need to show that the functors
f_{*}
and
f_{!}
are independent of
\kappa
, and that
f^{!}
is stabilized for
\kappa^{\prime}>>\kappa
large enough. The claim about
f_{*}
follows from
[
Man22b
, Lemma 2.9.12]
and the discussion at the beginning of the section. For the functors
f_{!}
for
f\in E
, it suffices to prove it for
f\in I
or
f\in P
. If
f\in P
then
f_{!}=f_{*}
and we are done, if
f\in I
and
f:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
, then
f_{!}=[\mathcal{A}\to D]\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}-
for
D
the idempotent algebra that complements
\mathcal{B}
. By hypothesis
D
is a
\kappa
-small algebra, which implies that the tensor product
[\mathcal{A}\to D]\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}-
preserves
\kappa
-small objects as wanted. For the stability of
f^{!}
, we can assume that
f\in I
or
f\in P
, in the first case
f^{!}=f^{*}
and we are done, in the second case
f
corresponds to a map
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{A}/}
and
f^{!}=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{B},-)
. Then the stability of
f^{!}
for large enough
\kappa^{\prime}
follows by Remark
3.2.4
.
∎

With the minimalistic
6
-functor formalism for solid affinoid spaces
\mathcal{C}
we can create a very large class of stacks
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
, and a large class of arrows
\widetilde{E}
as in Theorem
3.1.7
. One has the following corollary.

Corollary 3.2.6
.

Let
\mathcal{C}=\Aff_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}},\kappa}
be the category of
\kappa
-small solid affinoid spaces. Let
E
be as in Definition
3.2.2
. Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}={\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathscr{D}}(\Aff_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}},\kappa})
be the
\infty
-category of sheaves on anima for the
\mathscr{D}
-topology where
\mathscr{D}=\Mod_{(-),\kappa}
. Then there is a minimal class of arrows
\widetilde{E}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
containing the arrows represented in
E
such that the six functor formalism
(\mathcal{C},E)
obtained from Lemma
3.2.5
extends uniquely to
(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}},\widetilde{E})
, and such that
\widetilde{E}
is stable under disjoint unions, local on the target, local on the source, and tame.

Definition 3.2.7
.

With the notation of Corollary
3.2.6
, we call
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathscr{D}}(\Aff_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}},\kappa})
the
\infty
-category of
\kappa
-small solid
\mathscr{D}
-stacks
. If
\kappa
is omitted in the notation we write instead
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathscr{D}}(\Aff_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}})
and call it the
\infty
-category of
solid
\mathscr{D}
-stacks
.

Remark 3.2.8
.

The six functor formalism for solid quasi-coherent sheaves constructed before depends on the cardinal
\kappa
, in particular the functors
f_{*}
,
f_{!}
and
f^{!}
might depend on
\kappa
. Nevertheless, in practice we will always have formulas for these functors that will make them independent in large enough cardinals.

Next, we prove that the locale topology of Theorem
2.2.5
gives rise to cohomologically proper and étale
\mathscr{D}
-covers.

Lemma 3.2.9
.

The following hold:

(1)

Let
f:\SpecAn\mathcal{B}\to\SpecAn\mathcal{A}
. If
f
is open in the associated locale then
f
is cohomologically étale. Similarly, if
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{A}/}=\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{B}
is an idempotent algebra over
\mathcal{A}
, then
f
is cohomologically proper.

(2)

Let
\SpecAn\mathcal{A}\in\Aff_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}
and let
\{f_{i}:\SpecAn\mathcal{B}_{i}\to\SpecAn\mathcal{A}\}_{i=1}^{n}
be a collection of morphisms of solid affinoid rings. If
\{f_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is an open cover of locales then
\bigsqcup f_{i}
is a smooth
\mathscr{D}
-cover. Similarly, if
\{f_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is a closed cover of locales then
\bigsqcup_{i}f_{i}
is a descendable
\mathscr{D}
-cover.

Proof.

(1)

Suppose that
f
is open in the associated locale, by definition
f\in I
and
f^{*}=f^{!}
. Moreover,
f
is
-1
-truncated as
\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}
, this shows that
f
is cohomologically étale. Similarly, if
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{A}/}
is an idempotent algebra over
\mathcal{A}
, then
f
is
-1
-truncated and we have
f_{*}=f_{!}
, this implies that
f
is cohomologically proper.

(2)

Let
F=\bigcup_{i}f_{i}:\bigcup_{i}\SpecAn\mathcal{B}_{i}\to\SpecAn\mathcal{A}
be a finite cover. In the case that the
\{f_{i}\}_{i\in I}
form an open cover of the locale, by Proposition
2.7.21
adapted to
\Aff_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}
, the family
\{f_{i}\}
form a canonical cover, and the pullback along
F
is conservative. Then the conditions of Proposition
3.1.15
hold and
F
is a smooth
\mathscr{D}
-cover. Similarly, if
\{f_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is a closed cover on the locales, then it is refined by an open cover and therefore it defines a subcanonical cover. By Proposition
3.1.16
, we are left to prove that
F_{*}1=\prod_{i}\mathcal{B}_{i}
is a descendable
\mathcal{A}
-algebra. But by definition of closed covering in the locales, we know that
\underline{\mathcal{A}}
is equal to the “union” of the algebras
\mathcal{B}_{i}
, which clearly belongs to the thick tensor ideal generated by
F_{*}1
in
\Mod(\mathcal{A})
. This proves the lemma.

∎

We finish this section with the definition of Tate stacks.

Definition 3.2.10
.

Let
R_{{\square}}=(R,R^{+})_{{\square}}=(\mathbb{Z}((\pi)),\mathbb{Z}[[\pi]])_{{\square}}
, and let
\Aff^{b}_{R_{{\square}},\kappa}
be the
\infty
-category of
\kappa
-small bounded affinoid spaces. The
\infty
-category of
\kappa
-small Tate stacks
over
R_{{\square}}
is the category
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathscr{D}}(\Aff^{b}_{R_{{\square}},\kappa})
of sheaves on anima of
\Aff^{b}_{R_{{\square}},\kappa}
for the
\mathscr{D}
-topology, with
\mathscr{D}=\Mod(-)_{\kappa}
. If
\kappa
is omitted in the notation we simply call
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathscr{D}}(\Aff^{b}_{R_{{\square}}})
the category of
Tate stacks
.

The following lemma gives a sufficient criteria for the existence of
!
-functors for a morphism of solid stacks.

Lemma 3.2.11
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a map in
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathscr{D}}(\Aff_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}})
such that there is an epimorphism
\bigsqcup_{I}\AnSpec\mathcal{A}_{i}\to Y
with
\mathcal{A}_{i}
solid affinoid rings, such that for all pullback
X_{i}\to\AnSpec\mathcal{A}_{i}
there is a
\mathscr{D}
-cover
\bigsqcup_{j}\AnSpec\mathcal{B}_{i,j}\to X_{i}
, such that the maps
\mathcal{A}_{i}\to\mathcal{B}_{i,j}
factor through maps

\mathcal{A}_{i}\to\mathcal{A}[T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}]_{{\square}}\to\mathcal{B}_{i,j},

where
\mathcal{A}[T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}]_{{\square}}\to\mathcal{B}_{i,j}
has the induced analytic structure. Then
f\in\widetilde{E}
has
!
-functors for the theory of solid quasi-coherent sheaves. The same holds for
\mathscr{D}
-stacks over
\Aff^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
.

Proof.

By construction, the category
\widetilde{E}
of maps admitting
!
-functors is stable under disjoint unions, local on the target and local on the source, thus it suffices to show that each map
\AnSpec\mathcal{B}_{i,j}\to\AnSpec\mathcal{A}_{i}
has
!
-functors. Since
\widetilde{E}
is stable under compositions, it suffices to see that
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}[T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}]_{{\square}}
has
!
-functors, for which is enough to show that
\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
has
!
-functors. But we can write
\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}[T]\to\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
where the first arrow has the induced analytic structure, and the second is an open immersion of locales (see Proposition
2.3.2
), thus the composite has
!
-functors proving what we wanted.
∎

3.3.
Morphisms of finite presentation

In applications we find different definitions of morphisms of finite presentation depending on the geometry we are studying. In this section we explain a way to treat some formal properties of any of these situations in a more axiomatic way. We shall restrict ourselves to the case of solid affinoid rings.

Definition 3.3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a solid affinoid ring and let
\mathcal{A}[T]
be the polynomial algebra. A
coordinate theory
over
\mathcal{A}
is an idempotent map of solid
\mathcal{A}
-algebras
f:\mathcal{A}[T]\to\mathcal{A}(T)
such that

(i)

\mathcal{A}(T)
is an animated ring stack in
\Aff_{\mathcal{A}}
, i.e. the functor correpresented by
\mathcal{A}(T)
has a given enhancement in animated rings, and
f
is a morphism of animated ring stacks over
\mathcal{A}
.

(ii)

The natural map
\AnSpec\mathcal{A}(T)\bigsqcup\AnSpec\mathcal{A}(T^{-1})\to\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathcal{A}}
is a
\mathscr{D}
-cover.

We define
\mathcal{A}(T^{\pm 1}):=\mathcal{A}(T)[T^{-1}]\otimes_{\mathcal{A}[T^{\pm 1}]}\mathcal{A}(T^{-1})[T]
.

Let us give different examples of coordinate theories that occur in practice:

Example 3.3.2
.

(1)

Of course the trivial example is the identity map
\mathbb{Z}[T]\to\mathbb{Z}[T]
, in this case the “coordinate" is the classical one from algebraic geometry. A more interesting example is the solidification functor
\mathbb{Z}[T]\to\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
, here we think of
T
as the “solid coordinate”.

(2)

In rigid geometry we have at least two examples: the first one is given by
(\mathbb{Q}_{p}[T],\mathbb{Z}_{p})_{{\square}}\to(\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle T\rangle,\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, the second one by
(\mathbb{Q}_{p}[T],\mathbb{Z}_{p})_{{\square}}\to\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle T\rangle_{{\square}}
. The first coordinate is the adic compactification of the affinoid disc, the last is parametrized by the algebra
\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle T\rangle_{{\square}}
. Note that
\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle T\rangle_{{\square}}=\mathbb{Q}_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
, in general, the base change of a coordinate theory is a coordinate theory.

(3)

Let
\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle T\rangle^{\dagger}:=\varinjlim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}\langle p^{\varepsilon}T\rangle
, this "coordinate" is the one used in the theory of dagger spaces over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.

We now define rational localizations and morphims of (almost) finite presentation.

Definition 3.3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a solid affinoid ring and
\mathcal{A}[T]\to\mathcal{A}(T)
a coordinate theory over
\mathcal{A}
.

(1)

A morphism
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is an
\mathcal{A}(T)
-rational localization
if it is a composite of morphisms of the form
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}[T]}\mathcal{A}(T)
or
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}[T]}\mathcal{A}(T^{-1})[T]
. The
\mathcal{A}(T)
-analytic topology
on
\Aff_{\mathcal{A}}
is the Grothendieck topology with covers given by
\mathscr{D}
-covers consisting on finite disjoint unions of
\mathcal{A}(T)
-rational localizations. We let
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathcal{A}(T)}(\Aff_{\mathcal{A}})
denote the category of
\mathcal{A}(T)
-analytic sheaves on anima.

(2)

A morphism
f:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is of
\mathcal{A}(T)
-finite presentation
if it belong to the smallest category of
\mathcal{A}
-algebras stable under finite colimits and containing
\mathcal{A}(T)
. We say that
f
is of
local
\mathcal{A}(T)
-finite presentation
if it is a retract of a morphism of
\mathcal{A}(T)
-finite presentation. If
\mathcal{A}(T)
is clear from the context we simply say that
f
is of (local) finite presentation.

(3)

An
\mathcal{A}(T)
-adic space
is an object in
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathcal{A}(T)}(\Aff_{\mathcal{A}})
which is representable by an affinoid ring locally in the
\mathcal{A}(T)
-analytic topology.

(4)

A morphism
X\to Y
of
\mathcal{A}(T)
-adic spaces is
locally of (local) finite presentation
if it is of (local) finite presentation locally in the
\mathcal{A}(T)
-analytic topology of
X
and
Y
.

Remark 3.3.4
.

We use the name “local of finite presentation” for what
[
Lur04
]
calls “locally of finite presentation”. The reason for this difference is to avoid properties on spaces such as “locally of locally of finite presentation” which might be confusing.

Remark 3.3.5
.

Condition (ii) in Definition
3.3.1
guarantees that we have non trivial rational covers, namely, if
b\in\mathcal{B}
is depicted from a map
\mathcal{A}[T]\to\mathcal{B}
, the localizations

\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}(g)=\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}[T]}\mathcal{A}(T)\mbox{ and }\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}(\frac{1}{g})=\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}[T]}\mathcal{A}(T^{-1})[T]

form a
\mathscr{D}
-cover of
\AnSpec\mathcal{B}
.

Next, given a suitable six functor formalism on solid prestacks
\PSh(\Aff_{\mathcal{A}})
, we want to give a simple criteria for morphisms locally of finite presentation of
\mathcal{A}(T)
-adic spaces to admit
!
-functors. Let
X\in{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathscr{D}}(\Aff_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}})
be a solid
\mathscr{D}
-stack, suppose we are given a finite limit preserving functor
F:\PSh(\Aff_{\mathcal{A}})\to{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathscr{D}}(\Aff_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}})_{/X}
. Let
E^{\prime}
be the class of edges in
\PSh(\Aff_{\mathcal{A}})
such that
\sigma\in E^{\prime}
if and only if
F(\sigma)\in\widetilde{E}
, by Lemma
3.1.4
we have an induced
6
-functor formalism
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}
on
(\PSh(\Aff_{\mathcal{A}}),E^{\prime})
, we let
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}(\Aff_{\mathcal{A}})
denote its category of
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}
-stacks and
\widetilde{E}^{\prime}
the class of arrows of Theorem
3.1.7
.

Proposition 3.3.6
.

Let us keep the previous notation. Suppose that the following condition hold

(1)

F
preserves coproducts.

(2)

F
sends
\mathcal{A}(T)
-analytic covers to
\mathscr{D}
-covers.

(3)

The images by
F
of
\AnSpec\mathcal{A}\to\AnSpec\mathcal{A}(T)
and
\AnSpec\mathcal{A}(T)\to\AnSpec\mathcal{A}
are in
\widetilde{E}
, i.e. they admit
!
-functors.

(4)

For all
n\geq 1
the image by
F
of the map
\AnSpec\mathcal{A}\to\AnSpec(\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\bullet}\mathcal{A}[n])
is in
\widetilde{E}
.

Then
\mathcal{A}(T)
-analytic covers are
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}
-covers, and any map in
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathscr{D}^{\prime}}(\Aff_{\mathcal{A}})
representable, locally in the
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}
-topology, by morphisms of
\mathcal{A}(T)
-adic spaces locally of finite presentation is in the class
\widetilde{E^{\prime}}
of morphisms admitting
!
-functors.

Proof.

Since
F
sends
\mathcal{A}(T)
-analytic covers to
\mathscr{D}
-covers, rational localizations belong to
E^{\prime}
and admit
!
-functors. Indeed, by Remark
3.3.5
any
\mathcal{A}(T)
-rational localization belongs to an analytic
\mathcal{A}(T)
-cover, and since
F
