preserves disjoint unions, the image under
F
of rational localizations must admit
!
-functors. Furthermore, since
F
preserve co-products, disjoint union of rational localizations also admit
!
-functors. This implies that analytic
\mathcal{A}(T)
-covers of
\mathcal{A}(T)
-adic spaces are
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}
-covers. Let
f:Y\to Z
be a map of
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}
-stacks over
\mathcal{A}
representable by a morphism of
\mathcal{A}(T)
-adic spaces locally of finite presentation. Since the class
\widetilde{E^{\prime}}
is both local on the target and the source, to show that
f
admits
!
-functors it suffices to treat the case of a morphism of algebras
\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{D}
of finite presentation. Now, since
\mathcal{A}[T]\to\mathcal{A}(T)
is idempotent, we have

\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}(T)}\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}[T]}\mathcal{A}=\mathrm{Sym}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{A}[1].

Then, as
\mathrm{Sym}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{A}[n+1]=\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathrm{Sym}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{A}[n]}\mathcal{A}
for all
n\geq 1
, all the morphisms
\mathrm{Sym}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{A}[n]\to\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}\to\mathrm{Sym}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{A}[n]
are of
\mathcal{A}(T)
-finite presentation. Moreover, since
F
preserves finite limits, the map
\AnSpec(\mathrm{Sym}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{A}[n])\to\AnSpec\mathcal{A}
belongs to
E^{\prime}
. Therefore, to show that a morphism of finite presentation is in the class
\widetilde{E^{\prime}}
, it suffices to see that it is constructed by composites of pushouts along the maps of the form

—

\AnSpec\mathcal{A}\to\AnSpec\mathrm{Sym}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{A}[n]
,

—

\AnSpec\mathrm{Sym}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{A}[n]\to\AnSpec\mathcal{A}
,

—

\AnSpec\mathcal{A}(T)\to\mathcal{A}
,

—

\AnSpec\mathcal{A}\to\AnSpec\mathcal{A}(T)
.

The claim follows by the following lemma:

Lemma 3.3.7
.

Let
\mathbb{I}:=(I_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of finite sets with almost all
I_{n}
empty, let
\mathcal{A}(T_{\mathbb{I}})
denote the algebra

\mathcal{A}(T_{\mathbb{I}}):=\mathcal{A}(T_{i}:i\in I_{0})\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}^{\oplus I_{1}}[1])\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\cdots\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}^{\oplus I_{n}}[n])\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\cdots.

Let
(f_{n,j})_{j\in J_{n}}
be elements in
\pi_{n}(\underline{\mathcal{A}}(T_{\mathbb{I}}))
such that
J_{n}
is empty for almost all
n
, and such that
f_{0,j}
extends to a map
\mathcal{A}[X]\to\mathcal{A}(X)
. Write
\mathbb{J}=(J_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. We denote by
\mathcal{A}(T_{\mathbb{I}})/^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{\mathbb{J}})
the pushout of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}(T_{\mathbb{J}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}(T_{\mathbb{I}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Then, any
\mathcal{A}
-algebra of
\mathcal{A}(T)
-finite presentation is isomorphic to a composite of algebras of the form
\mathcal{A}(T_{\mathbb{I}})/^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{\mathbb{J}})
.

Proof.

We need to show that the category
\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{A}}
of
\mathcal{A}
-algebras constructed as composites of algebras of the form
\mathcal{A}(T_{\mathbb{I}})/^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{\mathbb{J}})
is stable under finite colimits. By
[
Lur09
, Corollary 4.4.2.4]
it suffices to show that it is stable under pushouts. Note that if
\mathcal{B}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{A}}
then the objects of
\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{B}}
are in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{A}}
. Consider a diagram
\mathcal{C}\leftarrow\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{D}
in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{A}}
, we can write

\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{D})\otimes_{\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}.

It is clear that
\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{D}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{A}}
. We claim that the multiplication map
\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}
is in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{B}}
, if this holds then
\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{D}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{D}}
whose objects are in
\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{A}}
. Let us write
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{A}(T_{\mathbb{I}})/^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{\mathbb{J}})
, then

\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{A}(T_{\mathbb{I}},S_{\mathbb{I}})/^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{\mathbb{J}}(T),g_{\mathbb{J}}(S)).

Since
\mathcal{A}(T)
is a animated ring stack, the maps
\mathcal{A}[T_{i}-S_{i}]\to\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{B}
naturally extend to
\mathcal{A}(T_{i}-S_{i})\to\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{B}
. We have that

\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{B}/^{\mathbb{L}}(T_{\mathbb{I}}-S_{\mathbb{I}})=\mathcal{B}/^{\mathbb{L}}(0_{\mathbb{J}}),

where
0_{\mathbb{J}}
is the sequence of
|J_{n}|
-zeros in
\pi_{n}(\mathcal{B})
for
n\in\mathbb{N}\geq 0
. But for any ring
\mathcal{C}
we have that
\mathcal{C}/^{\mathbb{L}}(0_{n})=\mathcal{C}(T[n+1])
is the free algebra over
\mathcal{C}
with one generator in degree
n+1
. Thus, we can find elements
g_{n+1,J_{n}}
in
\pi_{n+1}(\mathcal{B}/^{\mathbb{L}}(0_{\mathbb{J}}))
such that

\left(\mathcal{B}/^{\mathbb{L}}(0_{\mathbb{J}})\right)/^{\mathbb{L}}(g_{\mathbb{J}})=\mathcal{B},

proving the claim.
∎

∎

3.4.
The cotangent complex

In this section we briefly discuss some basic properties of cotangent complexes for prestacks on analytic rings. We will follow mutatis mutandis
[
Lur04
, §3.2]
.

We let
\PSh(\AnRing^{\op})
be the
\infty
-category of presheaves of anima on
\AnRing^{\op}
. Given
\mathcal{A}\in\AnRing
we let
\SpecAn\mathcal{A}
denote its representable presheaf that we refer as the
analytic spectrum of
\mathcal{A}
. As it is standard, we let

\Mod(-):\PSh(\AnRing^{\op})\to\CAlg(\Cat^{\colim,\ex}_{\infty})

denote the right Kan extension of the functor of complete modules of analytic rings.

Definition 3.4.1

(
[
Lur04
, Definition 3.2.5]
)
.

Let
M\in\Mod(\mathcal{A})
, we say that
M
is
almost connective
if
M[n]
is connective for some
n\geq 0
. Let
\mathcal{F}:\AnRing\to\Ani
be a functor and
M\in\Mod(\mathcal{F})
a quasi-coherent complex, we say that
M
is
locally almost connective
if for all analytic ring
\mathcal{A}
and all
\eta\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{A})
,
M(\eta)\in\Mod(\mathcal{A})
is almost connective.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an analytic ring and
M
a connective
\mathcal{A}
-module. We have an adjunction
F:\AniAlg_{\underline{\mathcal{A}}}\to\Mod_{\geq 0}(\underline{\mathcal{A}}):\mathrm{Sym}^{\bullet}_{\underline{\mathcal{A}}}
between animated algebras over
\underline{\mathcal{A}}
and connective
\underline{\mathcal{A}}
-modules. Given
M\in\Mod_{\geq 0}(\underline{\mathcal{A}})
, we can form the
\underline{\mathcal{A}}
-algebra
\underline{\mathcal{A}}\oplus M
as a condensed animated ring, which is obtained by forgetting the terms of degree
\geq 2
in
\mathrm{Sym}^{\bullet}_{\underline{\mathcal{A}}}M
.

Since
M
is a nilpotent ideal of
\underline{\mathcal{A}}\oplus M
, by Proposition 12.23 of
[
CS20
]
the analytic ring structures on
\underline{\mathcal{A}}\oplus M
and
\underline{\mathcal{A}}
are in bijection, and we have that

(\underline{\mathcal{A}}\oplus M)_{\mathcal{A}/}[*]=\mathcal{A}\oplus\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\underline{\mathcal{A}}}M.

Given
M\in\Mod_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{A})
we shall denote by
\mathcal{A}\oplus M
the
trivial square-zero extension
of
\underline{\mathcal{A}}
by
M
endowed with the analytic ring structure arising from
\mathcal{A}
. Let
\mathcal{F}:\AnRing\to\Ani
be a presheaf, we say that
\mathcal{F}
admits an
absolute cotangent complex
if there exists a locally almost connective quasi-coherent complex
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{F}}
of
\mathcal{F}
such that the functor mapping a triple
(\mathcal{A},\eta,M)
consisting of
\mathcal{A}\in\AnRing
,
\eta\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{A})
and
M\in\Mod_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{A})
to the fiber product of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{A}\oplus M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{A})}

is correpresented by
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{F}}(\eta)
.

One deduces easily the following property:

Proposition 3.4.2

(
[
Lur04
, Proposition 3.2.9]
)
.

Let
\{\mathcal{F}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a diagram of functors
\mathcal{F}_{i}:\AnRing\to\Ani
. Suppose that each
\mathcal{F}_{i}
has an absolute cotangent complex
\mathbb{L}_{i}\in\Mod(\mathcal{F}_{i})
. Let
\mathcal{F}=\varprojlim_{i}\mathcal{F}_{i}
and
\mathbb{L}=\varinjlim_{i}\mathbb{L}_{i}|_{\mathcal{F}}
. Then
\mathbb{L}
is an absolute cotangent complex for
\mathcal{F}
provided that it is locally almost connective.

More generally, let
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}:\AnRing\to\Ani
be two functors and let
\varphi:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
be a natural transformation. We say that the morphism
\varphi
has a
relative cotangent complex
if there is a locally almost connective quasi-coherent complex
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{G}}\in\Mod(\mathcal{F})
such that for all
\mathcal{A}\in\AnRing
,
\eta\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{A})
and any connective
\mathcal{A}
-module
M
, the object
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{G}}(\eta)
correpresents the fiber product

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{A}\oplus M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{A})\times_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})}\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A}\oplus M)}

where the map from
\eta
to
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A}\oplus M)
is induced by the evaluation of
\mathcal{G}
at the zero section
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}\oplus M
.

The relative cotangent complexes satisfy the following properties, whose same proofs also hold in our context.

Proposition 3.4.3

(
[
Lur04
, Proposition 3.2.10]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
be a natural transformation of functor from
\AnRing
to
\Ani
. Suppose that
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{G}}
exists, let
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{G}
be a natural transformation and
\mathcal{F^{\prime}}=\mathcal{F}\times_{\mathcal{G}}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. Then
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{G}}|_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
is the cotangent complex of
\mathcal{F^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

Proposition 3.4.4

(
[
Lur04
, Proposition 3.2.12]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}
be a sequence of natural transformations of functors. Suppose that there exists a cotangent complex
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}/\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}}
. Then there is an exact triangle

\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{F^{\prime}}/\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}}|_{\mathcal{F}}\to\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}}\to\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}

in the sense that if either the second or the third term exists, then so does the other and there is a triangle above.

The following is the analogue to Proposition 3.2.24 of
[
Lur04
]
regarding the existence of relative cotangent complexes for morphisms of analytic rings.

Proposition 3.4.5
.

Let
f:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be a morphism in
\AnRing
and let
f^{\prime}:\SpecAn\mathcal{B}\to\SpecAn\mathcal{A}
be the associated natural transformation at the level of presheaves. Then there exists a cotangent complex
\mathbb{L}_{\SpecAn\mathcal{B}/\SpecAn\mathcal{A}}
that is associated to the
\mathcal{B}
-module
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}}
. Furthermore, if
\mathbb{L}_{\underline{\mathcal{B}}/\underline{\mathcal{A}}}
is the cotangent complex of the map of underlying condensed rings, then

\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}}=\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\underline{\mathcal{B}}}\mathbb{L}_{\underline{\mathcal{B}}/\underline{\mathcal{A}}}.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.4.4
it is enough to show that the functor
\SpecAn\mathcal{A}
has an absolute cotangent complex given by
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{A}}=\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\underline{\mathcal{A}}}\mathbb{L}_{\underline{\mathcal{A}}}
. Let
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{C}
be a morphism of analytic rings and let
M
be a connective
\mathcal{C}
-module. We want to describe the space

(3.3)

\Map_{\AnRing_{/\mathcal{C}}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}\oplus M)

in terms of the cotangent complex of
\underline{\mathcal{A}}
. Since the analytic ring structure of
\mathcal{C}\oplus M
only depends on
\mathcal{C}
, and we have already fixed a morphism of analytic rings
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{C}
, the above space is equivalent to the space

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cond}(\AniRing)/\underline{\mathcal{C}}}(\underline{\mathcal{A}},\underline{\mathcal{C}}\oplus M)

of morphisms of condensed animated rings over
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
. Therefore, (
3.3
) is naturally equivalent to

\mathrm{Hom}_{\Mod_{\geq 0}(\underline{\mathcal{A}})}(\mathbb{L}_{\underline{\mathcal{A}}},M)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\Mod_{\geq 0}(\mathcal{A})}(\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\underline{\mathcal{A}}}\mathbb{L}_{\underline{\mathcal{A}}},M)

proving that
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{A}}=\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\underline{\mathcal{A}}}\mathbb{L}_{\underline{\mathcal{A}}}
is an absolute cotangent complex for
\SpecAn\mathcal{A}
.
∎

Remark 3.4.6
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{C}
be two morphisms of analytic rings. The base change property of the cotangent complex is now given by

\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{A}}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{B})=\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{B}}.

If
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is steady (
[
CS20
, Definition 12.13]
and
[
Man22b
, Definition 2.3.16]
) then it can be written simply as
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{A}}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{B}=\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{B}}
.

We deduce the following construction that helps to identify the
\pi_{0}
of a relative cotangent complex of rings with continuous Kähler differentials.

Proposition 3.4.7

(
[
Lur04
, Proposition 3.2.16]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be a morphism of analytic rings, and let
K
be the cone of this map seen as an object in
\Mod(\mathcal{A})
. Then there is a natural map
\phi:K\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{B}\to\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}}
. Moreover, if
f
is
n
-connected for
n\geq 0
, then
\phi
is
(n+2)
-connected.

Proof.

By
loc. cit
we have a map of the underlying condensed rings

K\otimes_{\underline{\mathcal{A}}}\underline{\mathcal{B}}\to\mathbb{L}_{\underline{\mathcal{B}}/\underline{\mathcal{A}}}

satisfying the prescribed properties of the proposition. Since the analytification functor
\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\underline{\mathcal{B}}}-
preserves connective objects, after
\mathcal{B}
-analytification one has a natural map
\phi
as stated satisfying the same connectness properties.
∎

Corollary 3.4.8

(
[
Lur04
, Corollary 3.2.17]
)
.

A morphism of analytic rings
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is an equivalence if and only if
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A})\to\pi_{0}(\mathcal{B})
is an isomorphism of analytic rings and
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}}=0
.

Proof.

By
[
CS20
, Proposition 12.21]
the analytic ring structures of
\mathcal{A}
and
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A})
are in bijection, so we only need to check that the underlying condensed rings are isomorphic. But then, the same argument of
[
Lur04
, Corollary 3.2.17]
can be applied to deduce the equivalence.
∎

Remark 3.4.9
.

Note that the hypothesis in the corollary asks for the map on connected components to be an isomorphism of
analytic rings
. If
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is a morphism of analytic rings with same underlying condensed ring then
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}=0
.

Example 3.4.10
.

(1)

The cotangent complex of a discrete affinoid ring
(A,A^{+})_{{\square}}
is simply the cotangent complex of the underlying condensed discrete ring.

(2)

Let
f:(A,A^{+})\to(B,B^{+})
be a morphism of Huber pairs, by Proposition (
3.4.7
) we can compute the continuous Kähler differentials of
f
as

\pi_{0}(\mathbb{L}_{(B,B^{+})_{{\square}}/(A,A^{+})})=\Omega^{1}_{(B,B^{+})/(A,A^{+})}=\mathcal{I}/\mathcal{I}^{2}

where
\mathcal{I}
is the augmentation ideal of
\pi_{0}(B\otimes_{(A,A^{+})_{{\square}}}B)\to B
, note that this tensor product coincides with the solid tensor product over
(A,\mathbb{Z})_{{\square}}
which is equal to the classical completed tensor product of Huber rings.

(3)

Let
A
be a
I
-adically complete
I
-torsion bounded ring where
I
is a finitely generated ideal of
A
. We have that
A\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}=(A\langle T\rangle,\mathbb{Z}[T])_{{\square}}
where
A\langle T\rangle
is the
I
-adic completion of the polynomial algebra. Then the cotangent complex

\mathbb{L}_{(A\langle T\rangle,\mathbb{Z}[T])_{{\square}}/(A,\mathbb{Z})_{{\square}}}=\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}/\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}}(A\langle T\rangle,\mathbb{Z}[T])_{{\square}}\cong A\langle T\rangle\cdot dT

is just the usual continuous Kähler differentials.

(4)

Let
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{C}
be an idempotent morphism or analytic rings, i.e. such that
\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}
. Then
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{A}}=0
. Indeed, we have that

\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{A}}=(\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{C})\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{A}}=\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{C}}=\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{C}}=0.

Now let us restrict to morphisms of solid affinoid rings.

Definition 3.4.11
.

We say that a morphism
f:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
of solid affinoid rings is of
solid finite presentation
if it belongs to the smallest category containing
\mathcal{A}[T]_{{\square}}:=\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}}\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
and stable under finite colimits (see Definition
3.3.3
and Example
3.3.2
). If the underlying rings of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are static, we say that
f
is of
solid finite presentation as static rings
if
\mathcal{B}
is a quotient of
\mathcal{A}[T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}]_{{\square}}
by a finitely generated ideal.

One easily deduces the following lemma thanks to Proposition
3.4.2
and the computation of the cotangent complex
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}/\mathbb{Z}}=\mathbb{Z}[T]dT
.

Lemma 3.4.12
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a solid affinoid ring, and let
\mathcal{B}
be an
\mathcal{A}
-algebra of solid finite presentation. Then
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}}
is a finitely presented
\mathcal{B}
-module, in particular it is discrete.

3.5.
Solid étale and smooth maps

In this section we review the definition of formally smooth and formally étale maps in the
\infty
-categories of presheaves on bounded affinoid rings over
R_{{\square}}=(R,R^{+})_{{\square}}=(\mathbb{Z}((\pi)),\mathbb{Z}[[\pi]])_{{\square}}
. We will characterize formally smooth maps of solid finite presentation in more geometric way, in analogy to classical algebraic geometry. Let us first adapt the definition of small extensions to our setting.

Definition 3.5.1

(
[
Lur04
, Definition 3.3.1]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be a morphism of analytic rings and
M
a
\mathcal{B}
-module. A small extension of
\mathcal{B}
by
M
over
\mathcal{A}
is an analytic
\mathcal{A}
-algebra
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
with a morphism
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\to\mathcal{B}
whose underlying condensed ring is a small extension of
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
by
M
as
\underline{\mathcal{A}}
-animated algebra.

Remark 3.5.2
.

Note that the map
\pi_{0}(\underline{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}})\to\pi_{0}(\underline{\mathcal{B}})
is a square zero extension, so the analytic ring structure of
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
is uniquely determined by that of
\mathcal{B}
by
[
CS20
, Proposition 12.23]
. Therefore, the
\infty
-category of square zero extensions of
\mathcal{B}
over
\mathcal{A}
is the full subcategory of square zero-extensions
\widetilde{B}
of
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
over
\underline{\mathcal{A}}
as condensed rings such that the fiber
[\widetilde{B}\to\underline{\mathcal{B}}]
is in
\Mod(\mathcal{B})\subset\Mod(\underline{\mathcal{B}})
.

Definition 3.5.3

(
[
Lur04
, Definition 3.4.1]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}:\AnRing_{\mathbb{Z}}\to\Ani
be a functor, we say that
\mathcal{F}
is
nilcomplete
if for all
\mathcal{A}\in\AnRing
the natural map
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{A})\to\varprojlim_{n}\mathcal{F}(\tau_{\leq n}\mathcal{A})
is an equivalence.

We say that
\mathcal{F}
is
infinitesimally cohesive
if for all small extension
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
of
\mathcal{A}
by an
\mathcal{A}
-module
M
, the natural map

\mathcal{F}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})\to\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{A})\times_{\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{A}\oplus M[1])}\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{A})

is an equivalence.

Definition 3.5.4

(
[
Lur04
, Definition 3.4.3]
)
.

Let
T:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
be a natural transformation of functors
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}^{\prime}:\AnRing\to\Ani
. We say that
T
is

(1)

weakly formally smooth
if it has a relative cotangent complex
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
which is the dual of a connective (discrete) perfect complex.

(2)

formally smooth
if it is weakly formally smooth, nilcomplete and infinitesimally cohesive.

(3)

formally étale
if it is formally smooth and
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}=0
.

We can define solid smooth and étale morphisms as follows.

Definition 3.5.5
.

(1)

A morphism
f:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
of solid affinoid rings is
solid smooth (resp. étale)
if it is formally smooth (resp. formally étale) and of solid finite presentation. If
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are static we say that
f
is
solid smooth or étale as static rings
if it is of finite presentation as static rings (Definition
3.4.11
) and formally smooth or étale with respect to square-zero extensions of static rings.

(2)

A morphism
f:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
of solid affinoid rings is
standard solid smooth
if
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{A}[T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}]_{{\square}}/^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})
for some sequence of elements
f_{i}\in\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}[T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}]_{{\square}})
such that
\mathrm{det}((\frac{\partial f_{i}}{\partial T_{j}})_{1\leq i,j\leq k})
is invertible in
\mathcal{B}
, it is
standard solid étale
if it is standard solid smooth with
n=k
. If
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are static we say that
f
is
standard solid smooth or étale as static rings
if it is the
\pi_{0}
of a standard solid smooth or étale algebra over
\mathcal{A}
.

Our main goal is to show that (1) and (2) in Definition
3.5.5
are equivalent after taking suitable rational covers. If
\mathcal{A}
is discrete, any finitely presented
\mathcal{A}
-algebra is discrete and different characterizations of (classical) smooth morphisms can be deduced from
[
Lur04
, Proposition 3.4.9]
. The main case of interest for us is when
\mathcal{A}
is a bounded affinoid algebra over
R_{{\square}}
. We have the following theorem.

Theorem 3.5.6
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a bounded affinoid algebra over
R_{{\square}}
. Let
T:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be a morphism of bounded affinoid
R_{{\square}}
-algebras. The following are equivalent

(1)

T
is formally smooth and
\mathcal{B}
is of solid finite presentation over
\mathcal{A}
.

(2)

T
is formally smooth and
\pi_{0}\mathcal{B}
is of solid finite presentation over
\pi_{0}\mathcal{A}
as static rings.

(3)

T
is, locally in the analytic topology of
\mathcal{B}
, a standard solid smooth
\mathcal{A}
-algebra.

Furthermore, let
\underline{\mathcal{A}}
be nuclear over
R_{{\square}}
, and
f:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
a morphism of the form
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\rangle_{{\square}}/^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{d})
with
f_{i}\in\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\rangle_{{\square}})
. Let
\mathcal{D}=R\langle X_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\rangle
, then there is a map
\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{A}
and elements
g_{i}\in\mathcal{D}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\rangle
mapping to
f_{i}
in
\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\rangle_{{\square}}
. In particular,
f
is the pushout of a morphism of solid finite presentation over
\mathcal{D}
. Moreover, if
f
is standard solid smooth (resp. étale) we can can take the
g_{i}
to define a standard solid smooth (resp. étale) algebra over
\mathcal{D}
. Finally, let
K
be a non-archimedean field, then a classical standard solid smooth algebra over
K\langle X_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\rangle
is already derived. In particular, solid smooth and étale maps of nuclear analytic
K
-algebras arise, locally in the analytic topology, as pushouts of classical smooth and étale maps from sous-perfectoid rings.

In order to prove the theorem we need some standard preparations in commutative algebra.

Lemma 3.5.7
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\in\AffRing_{R_{{\square}}}^{b}
and let
M
be a finite projective connective
\mathcal{A}
-module together with a surjection
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}\underline{\mathcal{A}}e_{i}\to M
(i.e. a surjection on
\pi_{0}
). Let
E\subset\{1,\ldots,k\}
, there is a maximal analytic open subspace
U_{E}\subset\Spa(\mathcal{A})
such that
\{e_{i}:\;i\in E\}
is a basis of
M
. Moreover, the open subsets
U_{E}
are Zariski open and cover
\Spa(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof.

Let
\Spa(\mathcal{A})\to\Spec(\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A})(*))
be the natural map that sends a basic open Zariski
U_{g}^{\zar}=\{g\neq 0\}
to the analytic set
U_{g}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\{|\pi^{n}|\leq|g|\neq 0\}
. By
[
Sta22
,
Lemma 00O0
]
there is an open cover
\{U_{E}^{\zar}\}_{E\subset\{1,\ldots,k\}}
of
\Spec(\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}(*)))
such that
U_{E}^{\zar}
is the locus where the map
\bigoplus_{i\in E}\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A})(*)e_{i}\to\pi_{0}(M)(*)
is an isomorphism. This implies that over
U_{E}^{\zar}
, the map
\pi:\bigoplus_{i\in E}\mathcal{A}(*)e_{i}\to M(*)
is an equivalence and the same holds for
\bigoplus_{i\in E}\mathcal{A}\to M
since both are discrete
\mathcal{A}
-modules. By taking the analytification
U_{E}\subset\Spa(\mathcal{A})
of
U_{E}^{\zar}
we get the lemma.
∎

Lemma 3.5.8
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a static bounded
R_{{\square}}
-algebra. Let
\mathcal{B}
be a solid finitely presented
\mathcal{A}
-algebra as static rings. Suppose we have a presentation
\mathcal{B}=\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\rangle_{{\square}})/I
with
I
finitely generated and
I/I^{2}
a free
\mathcal{B}
-module. Then
\mathcal{B}
has a (non-derived) presentation of the form
\mathcal{B}=\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}\langle S_{1},\ldots,S_{l}\rangle_{{\square}})/(f_{1},\ldots,f_{c})
such that
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{c})/(f_{1},\ldots,f_{c})^{2}
is a free
\mathcal{B}
-module with basis
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{c})
.

Proof.

This is
[
Sta22
,
Lemma 07CF
]
, we will see that the same proof can be adapted in this setting. Let
f_{1},\ldots,f_{c}\in I(*)
be such that they form a basis of
I/I^{2}
. By Nakayama’s lemma there is
g\in 1+I(*)
such that
g\cdot I\subset(f_{1},\ldots,f_{c})
: this holds for the underlying ring
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\rangle_{{\square}}(*))
, but
I
and
I^{2}
are finitely generated, then by taking the non-derived tensor
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\rangle\otimes_{\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\rangle(*)}-)
the same holds for the condensed ideal
I
. Then,
I[\frac{1}{g}]
is generated by
f_{1},\ldots,f_{c}
and we can write

\mathcal{B}=\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\rangle_{{\square}}[T_{n+1}])/(f_{1},\ldots,f_{c},gT_{n+1}-1).

where the ideal
J=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{c},gT_{n+1}-1)
satisfies that
\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{c},gT_{n}-1\}
is a basis for
J/J^{2}
. On the other hand,
g
maps to
1
in
\mathcal{B}
and we can localize at the open
\{|g|\geq 1\}
. We obtain a presentation

\mathcal{B}=\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{n},T_{n+1}\rangle_{{\square}})/(f_{1},\ldots,f_{c},gT_{n+1}-1)

with kernel
J^{\prime}
satisfying
J^{\prime}/J^{{}^{\prime 2}}=J/J^{2}\otimes_{(\mathbb{Z}[T],\mathbb{Z})}\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}=J/J^{2}
, where the solidification is with respect to
T=g^{-1}
. This proves the lemma.
∎

Lemma 3.5.9
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be a map of static bounded affinoid
R_{{\square}}
-algebras and let
I\subset\mathcal{B}
be a finitely generated ideal. Set
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}=\mathcal{B}/I^{n+1}
. The map
\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}}\to\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}/\mathcal{A}}
induces an isomorphism of non-derived tensors

\pi_{0}(\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}/I^{n})\xrightarrow{\sim}\pi_{0}(\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}/\mathcal{A}}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}\mathcal{B}/I^{n}).

Proof.

By
[
Sta22
,
Lemma 02HQ
]
we know that this is true for the differentials of the underline condensed rings. The lemma follows by taking the analytification with respect to
\mathcal{B}
.
∎

Lemma 3.5.10
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be a morphism of static bounded affinoid
R_{{\square}}
-algebras that is of solid finite presentation as static rings. Let
P=\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\rangle_{{\square}})
and write
\mathcal{B}=\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\rangle_{{\square}})/I
with
I
a finitely generated ideal. Then the sequence

(3.4)

0\to I/I^{2}\to\Omega^{1}_{P/\mathcal{A}}\otimes_{P}\mathcal{B}\to\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}}\to 0.

is exact and split if and only if
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is formally smooth when restricted to static analytic rings.

Proof.

This is the analogue of
[
Sta22
,
Lemma 031I
]
, let us see that the same argument works. First, by invariance of analytic ring structures under nilpotent thickenings
[
CS20
, Proposition 12.23]
, and the fact that
\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\rangle_{{\square}}=\mathcal{A}[T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}]_{{\square}}
, it is clear that the Tate ring
\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\rangle_{{\square}}
is formally smooth over
\mathcal{A}
. Thus, one can easily check that
\mathcal{B}
is formally smooth over
\mathcal{A}
(as static rings) if and only if there is a section of algebras
\mathcal{B}\to P/I^{2}
.

Now, if
\mathcal{B}
is formally smooth we can find a split as above, this provides a section of the map
\Omega^{1}_{P/\mathcal{A}}\otimes_{\mathcal{P}}B\to\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}}
and by applying
[
Sta22
,
Lemma 02HP
]
one gets that the sequence (
3.4
) is also exact (this argument uses Lemma
3.5.9
). Conversely, suppose that the above sequence is exact and split. We want to construct a section of
P/I^{2}\to\mathcal{B}
. Let
\sigma:\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}}\to\Omega^{1}_{P/\mathcal{A}}\otimes_{P}\mathcal{B}
be a section, and let us take
a_{i}\in I
such that
da_{i}=dT_{i}-\sigma(d\overline{T_{i}})\in\Omega^{1}_{P/\mathcal{A}}\otimes_{P}\mathcal{B}
. Consider the map
f:P\to P/I^{2}
sending
T_{i}
to
T_{i}-a_{i}
. We claim that
f
factors through
\mathcal{B}
providing the desired split. Since
I
is finitely generated, it is enough to show that for any
b\in I
we have
f(b)=0
. By exactness of the sequence it suffices to show that
d(b(T_{i}-a_{i}))=0
, but we have that

d(b(T_{i}-a_{i}))=d(b-\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\frac{\partial b}{\partial T_{i}})a_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\frac{\partial b}{\partial T_{i}})\sigma(d\overline{T_{i}})=\sigma(db)=0.

∎

Lemma 3.5.11

(
[
Sta22
,
Lemma 00TA
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be a solid smooth morphism of bounded affinoid rings. Then there is a finite analytic affinoid cover
\{\Spa\mathcal{B}_{i}\}_{i}
of
\Spa\mathcal{B}
such that the composite map
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}_{i}
is standard solid smooth.

Proof.

Let us write
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{B})=\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\rangle_{{\square}})/(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})
, and
I=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})
. Since
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is formally smooth, the morphism
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A})\to\pi_{0}(\mathcal{B})
is formally smooth as static rings. By Lemma
3.5.10
we have a split short exact sequence (
3.4
). In particular,
I/I^{2}
is a projective
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{B})
-module. By Lemma
3.5.7
we can find a finite analytic Zariski cover of
\Spa\mathcal{B}
of the form
\{U_{g}\}_{g\in\pi_{0}(\mathcal{B})}
such that the module
I/I^{2}
restricted to
U_{g}
is free. Taking an open cover by affinoids
\{|\pi^{n}|\leq g\neq 0\}
we find that

\pi_{0}(\mathcal{B}(\frac{\pi^{n}}{g}))=\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{n},T_{n+1}\rangle_{{\square}})/(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k},gT_{n+1}-\pi^{n})

is a presentation with kernel
J
such that
J/J^{2}=\pi_{0}(I/I^{2}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}(\frac{\pi^{n}}{g}))\oplus(dT_{n+1}-\pi^{n})\pi_{0}(\mathcal{B}(\frac{\pi^{n}}{g}))
is a free
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{B}(\frac{\pi^{n}}{g}))
-module. Thus without loss of generality we can assume that
I/I^{2}
is free. Then by Lemma
3.5.8
we have a presentation

\pi_{0}(\mathcal{B})=\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\rangle_{{\square}})/(f_{1},\ldots,f_{c})

such that the elements
f_{1},\ldots,f_{c}
form a basis of
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{c})/(f_{1},\ldots,f_{c})^{2}
. By arguing as in
[
Sta22
,
Lemma 00TA
]
we can find a Zariski cover of the form
\{U_{g}\}_{g}
such that the composition
I/I^{2}\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{0}(\mathcal{B})dT_{i}\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{c}\pi_{0}(\mathcal{B})dT_{i}
is an isomorphism (after reordering the variables for each open
U_{g}
). Thus, we find covers of the form

\pi_{0}(\mathcal{B}(\frac{\pi^{n}}{g}))=\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{n},T_{n+1}\rangle_{{\square}})/(f_{1},\ldots,f_{c},gT_{n+1}-\pi^{n}).

Reordering the variables, we get a standard solid smooth presentation of
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{B}(\frac{\pi^{n}}{g}))
as static rings. Thus, we can assume that the map
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A})\to\pi_{0}(\mathcal{B})
is standard solid smooth as static rings:
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{B})=\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\rangle_{{\square}})/(f_{1},\ldots,f_{c})
with
\mathrm{det}(\frac{\partial f_{i}}{\partial T_{j}})_{1\leq i,j\leq c}
invertible in
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{B})
. Consider
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\rangle_{{\square}}/^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{c})
. We can lift the elements
\overline{T_{i}}\in\mathcal{B}
to a map of rings
\mathcal{A}[T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}]\to\mathcal{B}
that can be completed to
\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\rangle_{{\square}}
. Moreover, the elements
f_{i}
are mapped to
0
in
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{B})
so that this map extends to a morphism of analytic rings
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{B}
which is an isomorphism on
\pi_{0}
. On the other hand, it is clear that the cotangent complex of
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}/\mathcal{A}}
is free and generated by
dT_{c+1},\ldots,dT_{n}
. Thus, the morphism of cotangent complexes

\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}/\mathcal{A}}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}\mathcal{B}\to\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}}

is a surjective morphism of projective
\mathcal{B}
-modules that is an isomorphism on
\pi_{0}
, then it must be an isomorphism. One gets that
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}=0
and by Corollary
3.4.8
we must have an equivalence
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\cong\mathcal{B}
.
∎

Corollary 3.5.12
.

A morphism of bounded affinoid rings
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is solid smooth if and only if locally on the analytic topology of
\mathcal{B}
it factors as a composition
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{s}\rangle_{{\square}}\to\mathcal{B}
where the second arrow is standard solid étale.

Proof.

Suppose
f:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is solid smooth. By Lemma
3.5.11
, locally on
\mathcal{B}
we can write
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\rangle_{{\square}}/^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{c})
as a standard solid smooth map. It is clear that
\mathcal{A}\langle T_{c+1},\ldots,T_{n}\rangle_{{\square}}\to\mathcal{B}
is standard solid étale.

Conversely, suppose that
f
is, locally in the analytic topology, standard solid smooth. Since rational localizations are standard solid étale maps, it suffices to show that standard solid smooth (resp. étale) morphisms is formally smooth (resp. formally étale). By invariance of analytic ring structure under nilpotent thickenings, one is even reduced to show that standard solid étale morphisms are formally étale. But the standard computation of the cotangent complex (using
\mathbb{L}_{\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}/\mathbb{Z}}=\mathbb{Z}[T]dT
) shows that if
f
is standard solid étale then
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}}=0
proving that it is formally étale.
∎

Lemma 3.5.13
.

Let
(K,K^{+})
be a non-archimedean field,
X=\Spa(A,A^{+})
an affinoid sous-perfectoid space over
(K,K^{+})
and
B=A\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\rangle/(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})
a (non-derived) standard solid étale extension of
A
. Then the sequence
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})
is regular, i.e. the natural map of condensed anima

(3.5)

\mathrm{Kos}(A\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\rangle;f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})\to B

is an equivalence.

Proof.

By the open mapping theorem it suffices to show that (
3.5
) is an equivalence for the underlying sets. Without loss of generality we can take
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\in A^{+}\langle T_{1},\ldots T_{n}\rangle
, then we have to prove that the
\pi_{i}
of the Koszul complex

(3.6)

\mathrm{Kos}(A^{+}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\rangle;f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})

have bounded torsion for
i>0
. Since
A
is sous-perfectoid, and the terms of the Koszul complex are free Banach
A^{+}
-modules, we can assume that it is a perfectoid ring. Moreover, the Koszul complex (
3.6
) is
\pi
-adically complete, so by
v
-descent we can even assume that
A
is totally disconnected. Since solid almost
\pi
-adically complete modules glue in the analytic topology of
X
(cf.
[
Man22b
]
), it suffices to prove the claim locally on
X
.

Let
x\in\Spa(A,A^{+})
be a point with residue field
(\kappa(x),\kappa(x)^{+})
. Then
B\otimes_{A}\kappa(x)
is a finite étale extension of
\kappa(x)
, so a finite product of
\kappa(x)
’s since it is algebraically closed. Thus, since
\Spa(\kappa(x),\kappa(x)^{+})=\varprojlim_{x\in U\subset X}U
, there is an open neighbourhood
x\in U_{x}
such that
B\otimes_{A}\mathscr{O}(U)
is just a finite product of copies of
A
. Then, after localizing
B
, we can assume that it is equal to
\prod_{s}A
. In this case, there are almost idempotent elements
e_{1},\ldots,e_{s}\in B^{+}
corresponding to the projections on each component of the product, and the localization
B(\frac{1}{e_{i}})=B\langle T\rangle/(T-e_{i})
corresponds to taking the
i
-th component. Therefore, we can even assume that
B=A
, in this case the Koszul-regularity follows from
[
FS21
, Lemma IV.4.16]
.
∎

Lemma 3.5.14
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a nuclear bounded
R_{{\square}}
-algebra. Let
\mathcal{B}
be a solid finitely presented algebra over
\mathcal{A}
of the form
\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\rangle_{{\square}}/^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{1},\cdots,f_{k})
with
f_{i}\in\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\rangle_{{\square}})
. Let
C=R\langle X_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\rangle
be the Tate algebra over
R
in countably many variables. Then there is a map
(C,R^{+})_{{\square}}\to\mathcal{A}
and elements
g_{1},\ldots,g_{k}\in C\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\rangle
mapping to
f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}
. In particular, we have an equivalence

\mathcal{B}=C\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\rangle_{{\square}}/^{\mathbb{L}}(g_{1},\ldots,g_{k})\otimes_{(C,R^{+})_{{\square}}}\mathcal{A}.

Proof.

Write
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A})
as a quotient of compact projective generators

\bigoplus_{I}R_{{\square}}[S_{i}]\to\pi_{0}(\underline{\mathcal{A}}).

Since
\underline{\mathcal{A}}
is nuclear, we have a surjection

\bigoplus_{I}R_{{\square}}[S_{i}]\otimes_{R_{{\square}}}R\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\rangle\to\pi_{0}(\underline{\mathcal{A}}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\rangle).

Let
f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}\in\pi_{0}(\underline{\mathcal{A}}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\rangle)
, by taking finite disjoint unions of the
S_{i}
’s if necessary, we can find a profinite set
S
and a lift
\widetilde{f}_{i}\in R_{{\square}}[S]\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\rangle
of the
f_{i}
. Then, we can find a quasi-finitely generated subalgebra
E\subset R^{+}
and a quasi-finitely generated
E
-module
M\subset R
such that

\widetilde{f}_{i}\in M_{{\square}}[S]\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\rangle

for all
i
(cf.
[
And21
, §3.1]
). We can write
f_{i}=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\pi^{r_{\alpha}}a_{i,\alpha}\underline{T}^{\alpha}
with
r_{\alpha}\to\infty
as
|\alpha|\to\infty
and the
a_{i,\alpha}
converging to
0
in
M_{{\square}}[S]
.
Let
\NS^{d}(R)\cong R^{+}[[T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}]][\frac{1}{\pi}]
be the space of null-
d
-sequences of
R_{{\square}}
, i.e.
\NS^{d}(R_{{\square}})=R_{{\square}}[\mathbb{N}^{d}\cup\{\infty\}]/(\infty)
. Then, we have a map

\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}\NS^{d}(R_{{\square}})\to R_{{\square}}[S]

defined by the
d
null-sequences
(a_{i,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}
for
i=1,\ldots,k
. Since
\mathcal{A}
is bounded, by multiplying the nullsequences by a power of
\pi
we can assume that the composition
\bigsqcup_{i}\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}\NS^{d}(R_{{\square}})\to R_{{\square}}[S]\to\mathcal{A}
lands in
\mathcal{A}^{0}
. Thus, by definition of
\mathcal{A}^{0}
, it lifts to a morphism of algebras

\mathcal{R}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{k}R_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[\mathbb{N}^{d}\cup\infty]\rangle/(\infty)\to\underline{\mathcal{A}}.

Let
C=R\langle X_{i,\alpha}:1\leq i\leq d,\;\;\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}\rangle
, we can take the composite map

C\to\mathcal{R}\to\underline{\mathcal{A}}

mapping
X_{i,\alpha}
to
a_{i,\alpha}
. Then letting
g_{i}=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}}\pi^{r_{\alpha}}X_{i,\alpha}\underline{T}^{\alpha}\in C\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\rangle
, we see that
g_{i}
maps to
f_{k}
as wanted.
∎

Remark 3.5.15
.

If
\mathcal{A}
admits a surjection from a nuclear
R_{{\square}}
-algebra then the conclusion of Lemma
3.5.14
holds. Indeed, one can lift a finitely presented algebra of
\mathcal{A}
to the nuclear algebra and then apply the lemma.

Proof of Theorem
3.5.6
.

The equivalences between (1)-(3) follow from Lemma
3.5.11
and Corollary
3.5.12
. The second statement for nuclear affinoid rings follows from Lemmas
3.5.13
and
3.5.14
.
∎

Corollary 3.5.16

(
[
Lur04
, Corollary 3.4.10]
)
.

Let
T:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be a morphism of bounded affinoid rings. The following are equivalent

(1)

T
is formally étale and of solid finite presentation.

(2)

T
is formally étale and
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{B})
is of solid finite presentation as static rings over
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A})
.

(3)

T
is, locally on the analytic topology of
\mathcal{B}
, a standard solid étale map over
\mathcal{A}
.

3.6.
Derived rigid geometry

In this section we study properties of solid smooth and étale morphisms of derived Tate adic spaces. We will show that these morphisms are cohomologically smooth and étale respectively, for the solid quasi-coherent six functor formalism. Finally, following
[
CS22
]
, we give a proof of Serre duality for solid smooth maps, by identifying the dualizing sheaf
f^{!}1
with the canonical line bundle.

3.6.1.
Zariski closed immersions

Let us begin with a brief discussion of Zariski closed immersions and affinoid morphisms.

Definition 3.6.1
.

(1)

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism of derived Tate adic spaces. We say that
f
is
affinoid
for the analytic topology if there is an open affinoid cover
\{U_{i}\}_{i}
of
Y
such that
V_{i}=X\times_{Y}U_{i}
is an affinoid analytic ring. We say that
f
is
strictly affinoid
if in addition
V_{i}
has the induced analytic structure from
U_{i}
.

(2)

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism of derived Tate adic spaces. We say that
f
is a
Zariski closed immersion
for the analytic topology if it is strictly affinoid, and we can find a cover as before such that the map of animated condensed rings
\mathscr{O}(U_{i})\to\mathscr{O}(V_{i})
is surjective on
\pi_{0}
.

(3)

More generally, let
f:X\to Y
be a map of Tate stacks, we say that
f
is affinoid (resp. strictly affinoid, resp. Zariski closed immersion) in the
\mathscr{D}
-topology, if
f
is of the form
\AnSpec\mathcal{B}\to\AnSpec\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\AnSpec\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{A}/}\to\AnSpec\mathcal{A}
, resp. surjective on
\pi_{0}
) locally in the
\mathscr{D}
-topology of
Y
. We say that
\mathscr{O}_{X}
is an analytic
\mathscr{O}_{Y}
-algebra and that
X=\AnSpec_{Y}\mathscr{O}_{X}
is the relative analytic spectrum of
\mathscr{O}_{X}
over
Y
.

Lemma 3.6.2
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
be a morphism of bounded affinoid rings. Suppose that the following hold:

—

\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
has the induced analytic structure and is surjective on
\pi_{0}
.

—

\mathcal{B}
is a retract of an algebra of the form
\mathcal{A}\langle\underline{T}\rangle_{{\square}}/^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{d})
for
f_{i}\in\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}\langle\underline{T}\rangle_{{\square}})
.

Then
\mathcal{B}
is a dualizable
\mathcal{A}
-module.

Proof.

By hypothesis
\mathcal{B}
is a retract of an algebra of the form
\mathcal{C}:=\mathcal{A}\langle\underline{T}\rangle_{{\square}}/^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{i})
for some finite set of variables
\{\underline{T}\}
and a finite sequence
(f_{i})_{i}
in
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A})
. We can even assume that
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{B})=\pi_{0}(\mathcal{C})
by killing additional elements. Then, it suffices to show that
\mathcal{C}
is a dualizable
\mathcal{A}
-module, and we can take
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{C}
. Let
a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
be a lift of the variables
T_{i}
, since
\mathcal{A}
is bounded there is some
k\geq 0
such that the map
\mathcal{A}[T_{i}]\to\mathcal{A}
sending
T_{i}
to
a_{i}
extends to
\mathcal{A}\langle\pi^{k}\underline{T}\rangle_{{\square}}\to\mathcal{A}
. By Lemma
2.2.7
, it suffices to prove that
\mathcal{B}
is dualizable locally in the open topology of the locale
S(\mathcal{A})
. Actually, we will show that
\mathcal{B}
is dualizable locally in the topology of the locale
\Spa^{\dagger}\mathcal{A}
of Definition
2.7.16
. By Proposition
2.7.19
we have a closed immersion
\Spa^{\dagger}\mathcal{B}\to\Spa^{\dagger}\mathcal{A}
, let
U
be the open complement. Let
V
be the open subspace of
\Spa^{\dagger}\mathcal{A}
that corresponds to the open localization
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}\langle\pi^{k}\underline{T}\rangle_{{\square}}}\mathcal{A}\langle T\rangle_{{\square}}
. Then
V
contains
\Spa^{\dagger}\mathcal{B}
and we have that
V\cup U=\Spa^{\dagger}\mathcal{A}
. The localization of
\mathcal{B}
at
U
is zero by construction. On the other hand, the localization at
V
of
\Spa^{\dagger}\mathcal{A}
is defined by an analytic ring
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
such that we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\langle\underline{T}\rangle_{{\square}}}

Since
\mathcal{B}
is an
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
-module, we also have that

\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\langle T\rangle_{{\square}}/^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{i}).

Let
c_{i}
be the image of
f_{i}
in
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\langle\underline{T}\rangle)
, then we have a retraction

\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\langle\underline{T}\rangle_{{\square}}/^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{i})\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime}/^{\mathbb{L}}(c_{i})\to\mathcal{B}

proving that
\mathcal{B}
is a perfect
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
-complex, so dualizable.
∎

Remark 3.6.3
.

The proof of Lemma
3.6.2
shows that
\mathcal{B}
is actually a perfect complex locally in the topology of
\Spa^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})
. However, this does not necessarily imply that it is a perfect complex over
\mathcal{A}
, only a dualizable sheaf. If the ring
\mathcal{A}
is Fredholm (cf.
[
CS22
, Definition 9.7]
) then any dualizable
\mathcal{A}
-module is perfect so
\mathcal{B}
would be perfect as well.

Remark 3.6.4
.

We do not know how to prove that if
X\to Y
is an affinoid (resp. strictly affinoid) map of derived Tate adic spaces such that
Y
is affinoid, then
X
is affinoid. One of the main obstacles is that it is not clear (and probably unlikely) whether the category of animated algebras over an analytic ring satisfy analytic descent. Similarly, if
f
is a Zariski closed immersion, even if we assume that both
X
and
Y
are affinoids, we do not know if the map on
\pi_{0}
is surjective (the problem here is the lack of flatness for rational localizations).

Example 3.6.5
.

The hypothesis that
\mathcal{B}
is a retract of an algebra of finite presentation obtained by killing some
0
-cells of a solid Tate algebra is actually necessary. For example,
\mathbb{Z}\to\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{Z}[2])
is a Zariski closed immersion but
\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{Z}[2])
is not a perfect
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra since
\mathrm{Sym}^{n}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{Z}[2])=(\Gamma^{n}_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z})[2n]\cong\mathbb{Z}[2n]
.

3.6.2.
Solid étale and smooth maps

Definition 3.6.6
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism of derived Tate adic spaces over
\mathcal{A}
. We define the following notions for
f
.

(1)

f
is
locally of (local) solid finite presentation
if locally in the analytic topology of
X
and
Y
, it is a morphism of (local) solid finite presentation of bounded affinoid algebras. We say that it is of
(local) solid finite presentation
if it is locally of finite presentation and qcqs for the analytic topology (cf. Definition
3.3.3
).

(2)

f
is
solid smooth (resp. étale)
if it is, locally in the analytic topology of
X
and
Y
, a solid smooth (resp. étale) morphism of bounded affinoid algebras.

In order to relate Zariski closed immerions of solid finite presentation with conormal cones we need the following lemmas.

Lemma 3.6.7
.

Let
A\to B
be a map of animated commutative rings that is surjective in
\pi_{0}
. Then
B\otimes_{A}B=\mathrm{Sym}^{\bullet}_{B}\mathbb{L}_{B/A}
.

Proof.

By
[
Mao21
, Theorem 2.23]
the surjection
A\to B
can be viewed as an animated pair
I\to A
where
I=[A\to B]
is the fiber. Then, since all the formulas commute with sifted colimits, one is reduced to prove the lemma for a animated pair of the form
(\underline{Y})\to\mathbb{Z}[\underline{X},\underline{Y}]
with
\underline{Y}
and
\underline{X}
sets of variables. The lemma follows by taking the Koszul resolution.
∎

Lemma 3.6.8
.

Let
\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{C}
be a morphism of solid affinoid rings of the form
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{B}[\underline{T}]_{{\square}}/^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{i})
with
f_{i}\in\pi_{0}(\mathcal{B}[\underline{T}]_{{\square}})
. Then
\mathcal{C}
is a perfect
\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{C}
-module.

Proof.

We have that

\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{B}[\underline{T},\underline{S}]_{{\square}}/^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{i}(T),f_{i}(S)),

and the multiplication map
\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
factors through

\mathcal{B}[\underline{T},\underline{S}]_{{\square}}/^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{i}(T),f_{i}(S),T_{i}-S_{i})\to\mathcal{C}.

But we have that

\mathcal{B}[\underline{T},\underline{S}]_{{\square}}/^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{i}(T),f_{i}(S),T_{i}-S_{i})=\mathcal{B}[\underline{T}]_{{\square}}/^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{i},\underline{0}_{i})=\mathcal{C}/^{\mathbb{L}}(\underline{0}_{i}),

proving that
\mathcal{C}
is a perfect complex over
\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{C}
(here we use that the
0
’s in the derived quotient are in degree
0
, so that it is indeed a perfect complex being represeted by a Koszul complex).
∎

Proposition 3.6.9
.

Let
X
and
Y
be derived Tate adic spaces over
\mathcal{A}
and let
f:X\to Y
be a Zariski closed immersion of solid finite presentation with
\mathscr{O}_{X}
a perfect
\mathscr{O}_{Y}
-module locally in the analytic topology of
Y
. The following hold

(1)

1_{X}
is
f
-smooth with dual given by
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathscr{O}_{Y}}(\mathscr{O}_{X},\mathscr{O}_{Y})
.

(2)

If
\mathcal{N}^{\vee}_{X/Y}=\mathbb{L}_{X/Y}[-1]
is locally free, then
f^{!}1_{Y}=(\bigwedge^{d}\mathcal{N}_{X/Y})[-d]
where
\mathcal{N}_{X/Y}
is the
\mathscr{O}_{X}
-dual of
\mathcal{N}^{\vee}_{X/Y}
, and
d
is the locally constant rank of
\mathcal{N}_{X/Y}
. In particular
f
is cohomologically smooth.

Proof.

(1)

By hypothesis,
\mathscr{O}_{X}
is a dualizable
\mathscr{O}_{Y}
-module, we also have a natural identification
f^{!}\cong\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathscr{O}_{Y}}(\mathscr{O}_{X},-)
. Thus, the
f
-smooth dual of
\mathscr{O}_{X}
is
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathscr{O}_{Y}}(\mathscr{O}_{X},\mathscr{O}_{Y})
which is a dualizable
\mathscr{O}_{Y}
-complex. This implies that the formation of
f^{!}
and
\mathcal{D}_{f}(1_{X})
commutes with any base change
Y^{\prime}\to Y
, by Proposition
3.1.11
(1) one deduces that
1_{X}
is
f
-smooth.

(2)

To prove part (2) it suffices to show that
f^{!}1_{Y}=(\bigwedge^{d}\mathcal{N}_{X/Y})[-d]
. As
\mathscr{O}_{X}
is a perfect
\mathscr{O}_{Y}
-module locally in the analytic topology, the ideal
I=[\mathscr{O}_{Y}\to\mathscr{O}_{X}]
is discrete relative to
\mathscr{O}_{Y}
locally in the analytic topology, and by Lemma
3.6.7
the space
X\times_{Y}X
is given by the relative analytic spectrum over
Y
of the (locally in the analytic topology) animated algebra
\mathscr{O}_{Z}:=\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}^{\bullet}\mathcal{N}^{\vee}_{X/Y}[1]=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{d}\bigwedge^{i}\mathcal{N}_{X/Y}^{\vee}[i]
. Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

We find that
f^{!}1_{Y}=\Delta^{*}\pi_{1}^{*}f^{!}{1_{Y}}=\Delta^{*}\pi_{2}^{!}1_{X}
. But then

\displaystyle\pi_{2}^{!}1_{X}

\displaystyle=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}(\mathscr{O}_{Z},\mathscr{O}_{X})

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{d}\bigwedge^{i}\mathcal{N}_{X/Y}[-i]

\displaystyle=\mathscr{O}_{Z}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}\bigwedge^{d}\mathcal{N}_{X/Y}[-d]

which shows that
f^{!}1_{Y}=\Delta^{*}(\mathscr{O}_{Z}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}\bigwedge^{d}\mathcal{N}_{X/Y}[-d])=\bigwedge^{d}\mathcal{N}_{X/Y}[-d]
as wanted.

∎

Corollary 3.6.10
.

Let
S
be a derived adic space over
\mathcal{A}
, let
X
and
Y
be solid smooth derived Tate adic spaces over
S
and
f:X\to Y
a Zariski closed immersion with
\mathscr{O}_{X}
a perfect
\mathscr{O}_{Y}
-complex locally in the analytic topology of
Y
. Then
\mathcal{N}^{\vee}_{X/Y}=\mathbb{L}_{X/Y}[-1]
is a locally free sheaf over
X
for the analytic topology. In particular,
f
is cohomologically smooth.

Proof.

We prove that
\mathbb{L}_{X/Y}[-1]
is a locally free sheaf over
X
. For this, we can assume without loss of generality that
S
,
X
and
Y
are affinoid with rings
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
respectively. Furthermore, by Theorem
3.5.6
, we can even assume that
\mathcal{D}
is a standard solid smooth over
\mathcal{B}
, so that
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{B}}
is a free sheaf of constant rank. We have a fiber sequence of cotangent complexes

\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{D}}[-1]\to\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{B}}\to\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{B}}\xrightarrow{+}.

Since both
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{B}}
and
\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{B}}
are free
\mathcal{C}
-modules, and
\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{B}}\to\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{B}}
is surjective on
\pi_{0}
,
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{D}}[-1]
is a projective
\mathcal{C}
-module that is free locally in the analytic topology of
X
by Lemma
3.5.7
. The corollary follows by Proposition
3.6.9
.
∎

Our next goal is to prove Serre duality in derived rigid geometry following the methods of
[
CS22
, Lecture XIII]
. First, we need to prove that solid smooth and étale maps are indeed cohomologically smooth and étale.

Lemma 3.6.11
.

Let
\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{C}
be a standard solid étale map of bounded affinoid rings, then the multiplication map
\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
defines an analytic open immersion at the level of affinoid spaces.

Proof.

By hypothesis we can write
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{B}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\rangle/^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{d})
with
\mathrm{det}(\frac{\partial f_{i}}{\partial T_{j}})_{i,j}
a unit in
\mathcal{C}
that we can assume solid. Moreover, writing
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{B})
as a filtered colimit of quotients of bounded algebras generated by extremally disconnected sets, we can assume that
\mathcal{B}=R_{{\square}}\langle\underline{X}\rangle_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[K]\rangle
with
\underline{X}
a finite set of variables and
K
a profinite set. Then
\mathcal{C}
is of the form

\mathcal{C}=R\langle\underline{X},T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\rangle_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[K]\rangle/^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{d}).

Let
g=\mathrm{det}(\frac{\partial f_{i}}{\partial T_{j}})_{i,j}
, we can assume that
f_{i}\in R^{+}\langle\underline{X},T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\rangle_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[K]\rangle
for all
i
,
|g|\leq 1
and that
|\pi^{n}|\leq|g|
for some fixed
n
. We claim that the multiplication map

R\langle\underline{X},T_{1},\ldots,T_{d},S_{1},\ldots,S_{d}\rangle_{{\square}}\langle\mathbb{N}[K]\rangle/^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{1}(T),\ldots,f_{d}(T),f_{1}(S),\ldots,f_{d}(S))\langle\frac{T_{i}-S_{i}}{\pi^{2n+1}}\rangle_{{\square}}\to\mathcal{B}

is an isomorphism. By Corollary
3.4.8
, since the relative cotangent complex vanishes, it suffices to prove that it is an isomorphism on
\pi_{0}
. For this, let
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{C}\langle S_{i},\frac{S_{i}-T_{i}}{\pi^{2n+1}}:i=1,\ldots,d\rangle_{{\square}}
, we want to show that the ideals generated by
(S_{i}-T_{i})_{i=1}^{d}
and
(f_{i}(S))_{i=1}^{d}
on
\mathcal{C}
are the same. We have Taylor series expansions

f_{i}(S)=f_{i}(T+(S-T))=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}f_{i}^{[\alpha]}(T)(S-T)^{\alpha}

where
f^{[\alpha]}
is the
\alpha
-th
PD
-derivative of
f
with respect to the variables
T
. Indeed, as
(S-T)
is divisible by
\pi^{2n+1}
, the series converges by the explicit growth conditions of Example
2.6.15
. As
f(T)=0
in
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{C})
, we have that

f_{i}(S)=\sum_{k=1}^{d}(\partial_{T_{k}}f_{i})(T)(S_{k}-T_{k})+h_{i}(S-T).

where
h_{i}(S-T)
has bounded coefficients in
\mathcal{C}
and monomials of degree
\geq 2
. Let
(b_{i,j})_{i,j}
be the inverse of the matrix
((\partial_{T_{j}}f_{i})(T))_{i,j}
, then we can write

(3.7)

S_{i}-T_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}b_{i,j}(f_{i}(S)-h_{i}(S-T)).

By hypothesis,
|b_{i,j}|\leq|g^{-1}|\leq|\pi^{-n}|
, so that
|b_{i,j}h_{i}|\leq\pi^{n+1}
in
\mathcal{C}
, this implies that
\sum_{j=1}^{d}b_{i,j}f_{i}(S)
is bounded by
\pi^{n+1}
in
\mathcal{C}
. Iterating the equation (
3.7
) one finds that

S_{i}-T_{i}=\sum_{1\leq|\alpha|\leq 2}c_{i,\alpha}\underline{f}(S)^{\alpha}+h^{(2)}_{i}(S-T)

where the
c_{i,\alpha}\in\mathcal{C}
satisfy that
|c_{i,\alpha}|\leq|\pi^{|\alpha|(n+1)}|
, and
h^{(2)}_{i}(S-T)
has bounded coefficients in
\mathcal{C}
with monomials of degree
\geq 4
. An inductive hypothesis let us write

S_{i}-T_{i}=\sum_{1\leq|\alpha|\leq 2^{k}}c_{i,\alpha}\underline{f}(S)^{\alpha}+h_{i}^{(k)}(S-T)

where
|c_{i,\alpha}|\leq|\pi^{|\alpha|(n+1)}|
and
h_{i}^{(n)}(S-T)
has bounded coefficients in
\mathcal{C}
with monomials of degree
\geq 2^{k}
. Taking limits as
k\to\infty
we get that
S_{i}-T_{i}
belong to the ideal generated by the
f_{j}(S)
for all
i
, which finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 3.6.12
.

The map of analytic rings
f:\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}\to\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
is cohomologically smooth.

Proof.

This is a consequence of
[
CS19
, Theorem 8.1]
. Consider the compactification
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}\xrightarrow{g}(\mathbb{Z}[T],\mathbb{Z})_{{\square}}\xrightarrow{j}\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
. Let us describe explicitly the shriek functors of
f
. Recall that
(\mathbb{Z}[T],\mathbb{Z})_{{\square}}\to\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
is the open localization complement to the idempotent algebra
\mathbb{Z}((T^{-1}))
. Then, we have descriptions for
M\in\Mod((\mathbb{Z}[T],\mathbb{Z})_{{\square}})

j_{!}j^{*}M=[\mathbb{Z}[T]\to\mathbb{Z}((T^{-1}))]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[T]}M\mbox{ and }j_{*}j^{*}M=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbb{Z}[T]}([\mathbb{Z}[T]\to\mathbb{Z}((T^{-1}))],M),

notice that the fiber
[\mathbb{Z}[T]\to\mathbb{Z}((T^{-1}))]
is isomorphic to

\mathbb{Z}[T]^{\vee}[-1]=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{Z}[T],\mathbb{Z})[-1]=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{Z}[T],\mathbb{Z})[-1]

as
\mathbb{Z}[T]
-module. Therefore, the funtor
f^{!}
is isomorphic to

f^{!}N\simeq\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{Z}[T]^{\vee},N)[1],

but
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{Z}[T]^{\vee},-)=f^{*}
, namely
\mathbb{Z}[T]^{\vee}
is a compact projective
\mathbb{Z}
-module, both functors commute with limits and
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{Z}[T]^{\vee},\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}[T]
. In particular, one has that
f^{!}\mathbb{Z}\simeq\mathbb{Z}[T][1]
. Notice that the previous hold for any base ring
A
with
A
a finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra. Now, take
Y=\AnSpec\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
,
X=\AnSpec\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}
and consider the cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Then, under the identification
f^{!}\mathbb{Z}\simeq\mathbb{Z}[T][1]
, one has the natural map
p_{1}^{*}(\mathbb{Z}[T][1])\to p_{2}^{!}\mathbb{Z}[T]
is an equivalence (both being equal to
\mathbb{Z}[T_{1},T_{2}][1]
). This implies that
f
is cohomologically smooth by Proposition
3.1.11
(1.b).
∎

Proposition 3.6.13
.

Let
f:X\to S
be a solid smooth (resp. étale) morphism of derived Tate adic spaces over
\mathcal{A}
. Then
f
is cohomologically smooth (resp. étale).

Proof.

First, by Lemma
3.6.12
, the map
\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}\to\mathbb{Z}[T]_{{\square}}
is cohomologically smooth. Since cohomologically smooth maps are stable under composition and base change, this implies that any map
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\rangle_{{\square}}
of bounded affinoid rings is cohomologically smooth. By Theorem
3.5.6
, any solid smooth map factors, locally in the analytic topology, as a composite of a standard solid étale map and the projection of an affinoid disc. Therefore, for the first assertion it suffices to see that a solid étale map is cohomologically étale. Let us assume
f
solid étale, by Lemma
3.6.11
the diagonal
\Delta_{f}
is an open embedding, so it is
-1
-truncated and by Lemma
3.1.21
it is cohomologically étale. Thus, by Definition
3.1.18
, it suffices to see that
1_{X}
is a
f
-smooth object. We can argue locally in the analytic topology of both
X
and
S
and assume that both are affinoid. Then, we can find a Zariski closed embedding
\iota:X\to S\times\mathbb{D}^{d}_{R}
into some affinoid disc over
S
such that
\mathscr{O}_{X}
is a perfect
\mathscr{O}_{S\times\mathbb{D}^{d}_{R}}
-module. By Corollary
3.6.10
the map
\iota
is cohomologically smooth, as
S\times\mathbb{D}^{d}_{R}\to S
is cohomologically smooth one deduces that
X\to S
is cohomologically smooth which in particular implies that
1_{X}
is
f
-smooth as wanted.

∎

A first application of the previous proposition are some classical facts about étale maps

Proposition 3.6.14
.

Let
S=\AnSpec\mathcal{A}
be a bounded affinoid space and let
X=\AnSpec\mathcal{C}
and
Y=\AnSpec\mathcal{B}
be bounded affinoid spaces whose rings of functions are given by
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{A}\langle\underline{T}\rangle/^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{i})
and
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{A}\langle\underline{T^{\prime}}\rangle/^{\mathbb{L}}(g_{i})
with
f_{i}\in\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}\langle\underline{T}\rangle)
and
g_{i}\in\pi_{0}(\mathcal{A}\langle\underline{T^{\prime}}\rangle)
. Suppose that we have maps
f:X\to Y
over
S
. The following hold

(1)

f
is of local solid finite presentation, i.e. a retract of a morphism of solid finite presentation.

(2)

If
X
and
Y
are solid étale over
S
then
f
is solid étale.

(3)

If
f
is solid étale and a Zariski closed embedding with
\mathscr{O}_{X}
a perfect
\mathscr{O}_{Y}
-module, then it is a rational open subspace associated to an open and closed subspace of
|Y|
.

Proof.

(1)

The algebra
\mathcal{C}
is a retract of
\mathcal{B}\langle\underline{T}^{\prime}\rangle_{{\square}}/^{\mathbb{L}}(g_{i})
, this shows that it is of local solid finite presentation over
\mathcal{B}
.

(2)

By (1) we know that
f
is of local solid finite presentation. On the other hand, since
X
and
Y
are étale over
S
, the fiber sequence of cotangent complexes shows that
\mathbb{L}_{X/Y}=0
, so that
f
is formally étale. One deduces that
f
is solid étale by Corollary
3.5.16
.

(3)

By Proposition
3.6.9
and Lemma
3.6.7
, one deduces that
X\times_{Y}X=X
, which implies that
f
is
-1
-truncated so an immersion. Since
X
has the induced analytic structure from
Y
, one deduces that
\mathscr{O}_{X}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{Y}}\mathscr{O}_{X}=\mathscr{O}_{X}
is an idempotent
\mathscr{O}_{Y}
-algebra, and that
X
defines a closed subspace in the locale of
\Mod_{{\square}}(Y)
. Since
f
is étale and a closed immersion, one has that
f^{!}=f^{*}
and
f_{!}=f_{*}
, which by Proposition
2.2.4
implies that
f
also defines an open embedding in the locale. Let
\mathcal{C}
be the open and closed complement of
\mathscr{O}_{X}
in the locale of
\Mod_{{\square}}(Y)
, then we have that
\mathscr{O}_{Y}=\mathscr{O}_{X}\oplus\mathcal{C}
as
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebras. In particular,
\mathcal{C}
is a locally connective
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebra in the analytic topology of
Y
.

Note that the notion of being a bounded affinoid algebra only depends on
\pi_{0}
, in particular it is also a well defined notion for connective analytic
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebras over
R_{{\square}}
. In particular, Proposition
2.6.16
also holds for
\mathcal{C}
. Now, let
I=[\mathscr{O}_{Y}\to\mathscr{O}_{X}]
be the ideal defining
X
, by hypothesis
I
is a perfect
\mathscr{O}_{Y}
-module, so its
\pi_{0}
is a module generated by its global sections at the point, and by Proposition
2.7.15
the idempotent
\mathscr{O}_{Y}
-algebra
\mathscr{O}_{Y}\{I\}^{\dagger}
is associated to the closed subspace
|X|\subset|Y|
. But we have
I\cong\mathcal{C}
, so
\mathcal{D}=\mathscr{O}_{Y}\{I\}^{\dagger}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{Y}}\mathcal{C}
is equal to its
\dagger
-nil-radical which implies that it is
0
as
T-1
is invertible in
R\{T\}^{\dagger}
and the map
R[T]\to\mathcal{D}
mapping
T
to
1
extends to the overconvergent power series. The previous reasoning shows that in fact

\mathscr{O}_{X}=\mathscr{O}_{Y}\{I\}^{\dagger}.

Now, since
\mathscr{O}_{X}
is
\mathscr{O}_{Y}
-perfect, one has that
\mathscr{O}_{X}=\mathscr{O}_{Y}\langle\frac{I}{\pi^{n}}\rangle_{{\square}}
for some
n>>0
, and
X
defines both an open and a closed subspace arising from the underlying space
|Y|
.

∎

3.6.3.
Serre duality

We want to prove the following theorem.

Theorem 3.6.15

(Serre duality)
.

Let
f:X\to S
be a solid smooth morphism of derived Tate adic spaces. Then
f
is cohomologically smooth and there is a natural identification
f^{!}1_{S}=\Omega^{d}_{X/S}[d]
, where
d
is the locally constant relative dimension of
f
, and
\Omega^{d}_{X/S}:=\bigwedge^{d}\mathbb{L}_{X/S}
is the determinant of the (locally free) cotangent complex.

We have already proved the first part of the theorem in Proposition
3.6.13
, the rest of the proof will follow the same steps of
[
CS22
, Theorem 13.6]
using the deformation to the normal cone.

Remark 3.6.16
.

Note that, if
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
of a morphism solid finite presentation which is surjective on
\pi_{0}
and such that
\mathcal{B}
is a perfect
\mathcal{A}
-module, the ideal
I=[\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}]
is a discrete
\mathcal{A}
-module, i.e. it arises as base change from an ideal of the animated ring
\mathcal{A}(*)
. Therefore,
[
CS22
, Construction 13.4]
of deformation to the normal cone applies in our setting, by taking base change of the construction at the level of underlying discrete rings and taking analytifications as derived Tate adic spaces, see Definition
2.7.24
. The result is a map
\widetilde{Y}\to Y=\AnSpec\mathcal{A}
locally of solid finite presentation. More generally, if
X\to Y
is a Zariski closed immersion of solid finite presentation with
\mathscr{O}_{Y}
a perfect
\mathscr{O}_{X}
-module in the analytic topology, then the deformation to the normal cone glues to a morphism locally of solid finite presentation

\widetilde{X}=X\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\widetilde{Y}\to X\times\mathbb{P}^{1}.

In addition, this construction only requires
X\to Y
to be a Zariski closed immersion locally in the analytic topology on
Y
, namely, taking
U\subset Y
such that
X\to U
is Zariski closed, one can glue
\widetilde{U}
and
Y\backslash X
along the complement of the exceptional divisor.

On the other hand, for
\mathcal{B}
a solid smooth
\mathcal{A}
-algebra, by Lemma
3.6.8
we know that the multiplication map
\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}
realizes
\mathcal{B}
as a perfect
\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{B}
-module, allowing the construction of the deformation of the normal cone for any diagonal embedding
\Delta:X\to X\times_{S}X
for any solid smooth map
X\to S
.

Proof of Theorem
3.6.15
.

Let
f:X\to S
be a solid smooth morphism of derived Tate adic spaces, consider the diagonal map
\Delta_{f}:X\to X\times_{S}X=:Y
, and let
\pi_{i}:X\times_{S}X\to X
denote the projection maps. By Lemma
3.6.11
the map
\Delta_{f}
is a locally Zariski closed immersion such that
\mathscr{O}_{X}
is a perfect
\mathscr{O}_{U}
-complex for some open neighbourhood
U\subset X\times_{S}X
containing
\Delta_{f}(X)
. By smooth base change, we have a natural isomorphism

f^{!}1_{S}=\Delta_{f}^{*}\pi_{1}^{*}f^{!}1_{S}=\Delta_{f}^{*}\pi_{2}^{!}1_{X}.

Therefore, it suffices to prove the statement for the projection
\pi_{2}:X\times_{S}X\to X
, or more generally, that when we have a section
s:S\to X
such that
\mathscr{O}_{S}
is a perfect complex in an open subspace
U\subset X
for the analytic topology, there is a natural equivalence
s^{*}\Omega^{d}_{X/S}[d]=s^{*}f^{!}1_{S}
. Consider the deformation to the normal cone of
s

\widetilde{f}:\widetilde{X}\to\widetilde{S}=S\times\mathbb{P}^{1}

together with the section
\widetilde{s}:\widetilde{S}\to\widetilde{X}
. Over
\mathbb{P}^{1}\backslash\{0\}
the section
\widetilde{s}
is isomorphic to the base change of
S\to X
, and the fiber at
0
is the zero section of the analytification of the normal cone of
s
(see Definition
2.7.24
and Construction
4.3.9
).

The pullback functor
\pi^{*}:\Mod_{{\square}}(S)\to\Mod_{{\square}}(\mathbb{P}^{1}_{S})
is fully faithful. Indeed, the map
\pi:\mathbb{P}^{1}_{S}\to S
is weakly cohomologically proper being the base change of
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}}\to\SpecAn\mathbb{Z}_{{\square}}
, and this last being the glueing of
\AnSpec(\mathbb{Z}[T],\mathbb{Z})_{{\square}}
and
\AnSpec(\mathbb{Z}[T^{-1}],\mathbb{Z})_{{\square}}
along the torus
\AnSpec(\mathbb{Z}[T^{\pm}],\mathbb{Z})_{{\square}}
. Thus, by projection formula and proper base change it suffices to show that
\pi_{*}1_{\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}}}=\mathbb{Z}
which is classical. We make the following claim:

Claim
.

The sheaf
\widetilde{s}^{*}\widetilde{f}^{!}\mathscr{O}_{\widetilde{S}}(d)
belongs to the essential image of
\pi^{*}
, where
\mathscr{O}_{\widetilde{S}}(d)
is the
d
-th Serre twist of
\widetilde{S}=\mathbb{P}^{1}_{S}
.

Suppose this holds true, and let
\iota_{0}:S\to\mathbb{P}^{1}_{S}
and
\iota_{\infty}:S\to\mathbb{P}^{1}_{S}
the
0
and
\infty
-sections. Then we have natural isomorphisms

(3.8)

s^{*}f^{!}1_{S}\cong\iota_{\infty}^{*}\widetilde{s}^{*}\widetilde{f}^{!}\mathscr{O}_{\widetilde{S}}(d)\cong\iota_{0}^{*}\widetilde{s}^{*}\widetilde{f}^{!}\mathscr{O}_{\widetilde{S}}(d)\cong\bar{\iota}_{0}^{*}p^{!}1_{S}

where
p:\mathcal{N}_{X/S}^{\an}\to S
is the (analytic) normal cone of
s
and
\bar{\iota}_{0}:S\to\mathcal{N}_{X/S}^{\an}
is the zero section.

Proof of the Claim.

The formation
\widetilde{X}
is local on
S
and the section
s:S\to X
, thus, by taking rational covers, we can assume that
S=\AnSpec\mathcal{A}
and that
X=\AnSpec\mathcal{B}
is standard solid étale over
S
. Write
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{A}\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{d+c}\rangle_{{\square}}/^{\mathbb{L}}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{c})
with
g=\mathrm{det}(\frac{\partial f_{i}}{\partial T_{j}})_{1\leq i,j\leq c}
invertible. The last
d
coordinates produce a standard solid étale map

g:X\to\mathbb{D}^{d}_{S}.

Thus, the composite
s^{\prime}=g\circ s:S\to\mathbb{D}^{d}_{S}
produces a section. Consider the cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}^{d}_{S},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Then, the section
S\to X
produces a retract
S\xrightarrow{r}S^{\prime}
which is necessarily a Zariski closed immersion, and by Proposition
3.6.14
(3), it is actually a closed and open immersion associated to a closed and open subspace of the underlying adic space. Summarizing, we have the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}_{S}^{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}}

where the square is cartesian and
r
is an open and closed immersion. Writting
S^{\prime}=S\sqcup S^{\prime\prime}
, and replacing
X
with a neighbourhood of
s^{\prime\prime}
of the form
X_{1}\sqcup X_{2}
such that
X_{1}\cap s^{\prime\prime}(S^{\prime})=S
and
X_{2}\cap s^{\prime\prime}(S^{\prime})=S^{\prime\prime}
, we can assume that
S=S^{\prime}
.

We have a diagram of deformations to normal cone

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{1}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathbb{D}^{d}}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{1}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{D}^{d}_{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{1}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{s^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

where the middle square is cartesian. Indeed, this follows from
[
CS22
, Proposition 13.3]
(see also
[
Mao21
, Corollary 3.54]
) since for a surjection
A\to B
with kernel
I=[A\to B]
, the
I
-adic filtration
(I^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is compatible with base change along
A
. In particular,
\widetilde{g}
is solid étale and
\widetilde{g}^{!}=\widetilde{g}^{*}
. Therefore, we find natural equivalences of functors

\widetilde{s}^{*}\widetilde{f}^{!}=\widetilde{s}^{*}\widetilde{g}^{*}\widetilde{p}^{!}=\widetilde{s^{\prime}}^{*}\widetilde{p}^{!},

this reduces the claim to the case of a disc
X=\mathbb{D}^{d}_{S}
. By a change of coordinates, we can even assume that the section
s:S\to\mathbb{D}^{d}_{S}
is given by the zero section. Thus, by base change we can further reduce to the algebraic statement of
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}}=\Spec\mathbb{Z}[T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}]
with the zero section, where this is classical and follows by an explicit computation.
∎

Let
p:\mathcal{N}_{X/S}\to S
be the normal cone of the section
s
, and
\bar{\iota}_{0}:S\to\mathcal{N}_{X/S}\to S
. To end the proof we need to show that there is a natural equivalence

\overline{\iota}_{0}^{*}p^{!}1_{S}=s^{*}\Omega^{d}_{X/S}[d].

It suffices to show more generally that for a vector bundle
\mathcal{E}
of rank
d
over
S
, with analytic geometric realization
q:\underline{\mathcal{E}}^{\an}\to S
and zero section
\iota:S\to\underline{\mathcal{E}}^{\an}
, there is a natural equivalence

\iota^{*}q^{!}1_{S}=\bigwedge^{d}\mathcal{E}^{\vee}[d].

The functor mapping
[\mathcal{E}\to S]
to
\iota^{*}q^{!}1_{S}[-d]
defines a map
*/\mathbf{GL}_{d}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}
of stacks. Equivalently, it defines a line bundle over
*/\mathbf{GL}_{d}
seen as a stack in the analytic topology of
\Aff^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
. Thus, to identify this object it suffices to compute it for the standard vector bundle of rank
d
over
*/\mathbf{GL}_{d}
, this is proven independently in Proposition
4.3.11
.
∎

We finish this section by describing the smooth objects of solid smooth maps for the six functors of solid quasi-coherent sheaves.

Proposition 3.6.17
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a solid smooth morphism of derived adic spaces over
\mathcal{A}
. Then an object
P\in\Mod_{{\square}}(X)
is
f
-smooth if and only if it is dualizable.

Proof.

Suppose that
f
is solid smooth, and let
P\in\Mod_{{\square}}(X)
. Being
f
-smooth is a local property in the analytic topology, we can then assume that both
X
and
Y
are affinoids. Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

By Proposition
3.6.9
,
\Delta
is cohomologically smooth, then
P=\Delta^{*}\pi_{1}^{*}P
is
\id_{X}
-smooth which is the same as dualizable. Conversely, let
P
be dualizable. We then have that
\mathcal{D}_{f}(P)=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{X}(P,f^{!}1_{Y})=f^{!}1_{Y}\otimes P^{\vee}
is dualizable and that the natural map

\pi_{1}^{*}\mathcal{D}_{f}(P)\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}P\to\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{X\times_{Y}X}(\pi_{1}^{*}P,\pi_{2}^{!}P)

is an isomorphism, then
P
is
f
-smooth by Proposition
3.1.24
(1.b).
∎

3.7.
Formally overconvergent étale and smooth maps

In this final section we introduce two new deformation properties that will play a fundamental role in the definition of the analytic de Rham stack.

Definition 3.7.1
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\in\Aff^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
, a
\dagger
-nilpotent ideal
of
\mathcal{A}
is a full sub
\mathcal{A}
-module
I\subset\mathcal{A}
contained in
\Nil^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A})
.

Definition 3.7.2
.

Let
T:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
be a natural transformation of functors
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}^{\prime}:\Aff^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}\to\Ani
. We say that
T
is
\dagger
-formally smooth
(resp.
\dagger
-formally étale
) if it is formally smooth (resp. formally étale) and for all
\mathcal{A}\in\Aff^{b}_{R_{{\square}}}
, and all
\dagger
-nilpotent ideal
I
of
\mathcal{A}
, the natural map of anima

\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{A}/I)\times_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A}/I)}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A})

is surjective (resp. an equivalence).

Remark 3.7.3
.

Since the underlying ring of
\mathcal{A}/I
sits in degree
0
, to check that a formally smooth (resp. étale) functor
T:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is
\dagger
-formally smooth (resp. étale) it is enough to take
\mathcal{A}
an static bounded affinoid ring.

Proposition 3.7.4
.

(1)

A composition of
\dagger
-formally smooth morphisms is
\dagger
-formally smooth.

(2)

If
\{\mathcal{F}_{i}\to\mathcal{F}\}_{i\in I}
is a cofiltered diagram of
\dagger
-formally smooth functors with each arrow
\mathcal{F}_{i}\to\mathcal{F}_{j}
formally étale, then
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\varprojlim_{i}\mathcal{F}_{i}\to\mathcal{F}
is
\dagger
-formally smooth.

(3)

A pullback of
\dagger
-formally smooth maps is
\dagger
-formally smooth.

Similar statements hold for
\dagger
-formally étale.

Proof.

Parts (1) and (3) are proved in the same way as for formally smooth maps. For part (2), note that since the transition maps of the cofiltered limit are formally étale, the cotangent complex
\mathbb{L}_{\varprojlim_{i}\mathcal{F}_{i}/\mathcal{F}}
is still the dual of a connective perfect complex. It is also clear that the map
\varprojlim_{i}\mathcal{F}_{i}\to\mathcal{F}
is nilcomplete and infinitesimally cohesive since limits commute with limits. The
\dagger
